## Motivation And Role Fit

### Core Narrative And Role Fit

#### Tell Me About Yourself

Canonical sales-led version:

> Yeah. The simplest way to explain it is that I’ve spent most of my career selling technical products, first in early-stage enterprise sales and then in a more structured quota environment.
>
> I started at Boxo as the first business hire. We sold an SDK platform to fintechs, banks, and super apps across APAC. I began in outbound and eventually owned deals end to end—from identifying accounts and reaching out, through product and engineering discussions, commercial negotiation, and close. I directly sourced and closed about USD 450K in ARR across three fintech clients.
>
> I then joined Airwallex because I wanted to prove I could perform in a more mature sales organization. I was part of the Singapore outbound team, hunting new business across travel, retail, and ecommerce. I closed 22 new logos and finished above 200% of annual quota.
>
> Over the last couple of years, I’ve continued selling full-cycle through my own AI projects. I’ve had to find customers, run discovery, scope the work, negotiate, and close—while also becoming much more technically fluent in how AI changes business workflows.
>
> So the through-line is really sales. Boxo taught me complex enterprise selling, Airwallex taught me quota discipline, and the founder work made me better at selling AI credibly. That combination is why this OpenAI role feels like a natural next step for me.

#### Recruiter Objection: Connect Boxo, Airwallex, And Founder-Led Full-Cycle Sales

Interview question: "Your background is interesting, but how does it add up to conventional full-cycle sales experience?"

> The cleanest way to connect my experience is that each chapter trained a different part of the full sales cycle.
>
> At Boxo, I learned enterprise complexity. With Unabank, I had to qualify the opportunity, work my way through the account, map the CEO, product, engineering and risk stakeholders, translate objections into product requirements, negotiate the commercial case and stay involved through implementation. That taught me how to make a technical enterprise decision feel safe.
>
> I then joined Airwallex because I wanted a more disciplined quota-carrying environment. I was the second outbound hire in Singapore, helped build the outbound motion, closed 22 mid-market logos and finished above 200% of quota. That taught me pipeline discipline, qualification, CRM hygiene and how to execute repeatedly against a number.
>
> As a founder, I had to combine both. There was no marketing engine or established brand. I sourced the opportunities, ran discovery, mapped the workflow, scoped the solution, wrote the proposal, negotiated the commercial terms, closed the work and stayed accountable for delivery.
>
> So my background is less conventional than someone who has held the same AE title continuously, but the actual work has been full-cycle throughout: create the opportunity, understand the buyer, earn the decision and make sure the promise survives implementation.

Use this as the direct answer to the recruiter-identified objection. Do not precede it with the full career biography.

#### Most Impressive Thing I Personally Built Or Automated With AI

Prompt: "What is the most impressive thing you've personally built or automated with AI? Describe exactly what you did, how it worked, and the measurable outcome."

Canonical answer:

> Yeah, I think the most impressive thing I have personally built is a company second brain and GTM operating workspace. The way I got there was quite fun, because it started with me reading Nader Dabit's "You Could've Invented OpenClaw" article and realizing, oh, this is not just another chatbot.
>
> This is a pattern for turning an LLM into a persistent operating layer.
>
> The way that article explained OpenClaw really clicked for me. Normal ChatGPT or Claude in a browser has a few obvious limitations: it is mostly stateless, it is passive, it is isolated from your actual tools, and it lives in a separate tab instead of the places where work happens.
>
> Real work is in Slack threads, WhatsApp messages, Telegram chats, documents, spreadsheets, CRMs, calendars, and folders.
>
> OpenClaw's answer was: what if the assistant lived closer to that work, with persistent sessions, an identity, tools, permissions, long-term memory, scheduled heartbeats, multiple channels, and even multiple specialized agents?
>
> The architecture was not mystical once I broke it down. You start with a simple messaging bot. Then you add session history, so the assistant remembers the thread instead of treating every message as brand new.
>
> In OpenClaw this can be stored as an append-only log, where each message is saved as a new line, which makes the memory durable and easy to recover if something crashes.
>
> Then you add something like a `SOUL.md`, which defines the agent's identity, behavior, boundaries, and memory rules. Then you add structured tools: read a file, write a file, run a command, search the web.
>
> Then you add permission controls, because once an agent can touch your machine or your work systems, the failure mode is no longer just a bad answer.
>
> It could delete a file, expose a credential, send the wrong message, update the wrong record, or run a command you never intended. So you need allowlists, approvals, logs, and clear boundaries.
>
> Then you add a gateway so different channels can route into the same session and memory. In practice, the three obvious channels for me were Slack, WhatsApp, and Telegram, because that is where a lot of actual work context shows up.
>
> Then you add the operational pieces: context compaction so old conversations get summarized instead of blowing up the context window; long-term memory so important facts survive beyond one chat thread; command queues so two messages do not corrupt the same state at the same time;
>
> cron jobs or heartbeats so the agent can initiate scheduled work without waiting for a human prompt, like morning briefings, follow-up sweeps, meeting prep, or inbox monitoring; and eventually multi-agent routing so a research agent, writing agent, or ops agent can have different instructions and tools.
>
> The big realization for me was that an agent is not just a model with a prompt. It is a runtime around the model: identity, state, tools, memory, permissions, channels, logs, and review paths. That was the mental model I wanted.
>
> But I did not want to use OpenClaw wholesale. The pattern was powerful, but the implementation was not the right surface for my use case. It was changing very quickly, parts of it were buggy, self-hosting was painful, and the security posture made me uncomfortable.
>
> The minute you make an agent always-on and connect it to real tools, security becomes the product.
>
> You have to worry about where credentials live, what ports or endpoints are exposed, what commands the agent can run, what data it can read, what it can write, how auth works for Slack or WhatsApp, whether logs contain sensitive data, and what approval gate exists before the agent takes action.
>
> For me, the lesson was: keep the architecture, strip down the surface area.
>
> So I asked: what is the minimum version of this that would be useful for the work I actually do? I did not need a fully self-hosted always-on OpenClaw clone. I needed a local agent workspace with durable memory, source boundaries, search, skills, and human review.
>
> Basically: keep the agent operating-system pattern, but make it safer and more boring.
>
> Then I combined that with Karpathy's LLM Wiki pattern, which was the second big unlock. His idea is that most RAG systems retrieve from raw documents every time, so the model keeps rediscovering the same knowledge from scratch.
>
> The LLM wiki pattern is different: raw sources stay immutable, and the LLM incrementally maintains a structured markdown wiki on top of them. The wiki becomes a persistent, compounding artifact. It has entity pages, concept pages, summaries, cross-links, contradictions, an index, and a log.
>
> Obsidian is the IDE, the LLM is the programmer, and the wiki is the codebase.
>
> That pattern fit my problem perfectly. Company knowledge was scattered across Drive, Notion, Slack, WhatsApp, CRM notes, call transcripts, pricing material, SOWs, SOPs, and old proposals. The issue was not that people lacked tools. The issue was that context did not compound.
>
> Every new SOW, account brief, or proposal started with people digging through old threads and rebuilding the same context from memory.
>
> The deeper lesson was that the company has to become agent-ready before the agent can help. Structured data, clean SOPs, templates, mapped data flows, one source of truth per category, a tool and subscription tracker, QA standards, and a decision log are not glamorous, but they are the substrate.
>
> Aim an agent at good inputs and it can turn slow hand-built work into a much faster review loop. Aim it at a messy organization and it just scales the mess faster.
>
> So the system I built was basically a stripped-down OpenClaw-style operating workspace plus a Karpathy-style LLM wiki. Raw sources stayed where they belonged as evidence: Notion, Google Drive, Slack or WhatsApp context, documents, transcripts, and exported source files.
>
> I set up recurring ingestion so evidence could be pulled in roughly every six hours, captured, and then compiled into the wiki layer.
>
> The wiki became the maintained knowledge layer. The agent instructions told Codex or Claude how to ingest sources, update the wiki, answer questions, preserve source boundaries, and append to the log.
>
> And because the document set got too large for a simple index, I added QMD for retrieval: keyword search for exact terms, vector search for semantic matches, and local retrieval over markdown so the agent could find the right evidence before drafting.
>
> I also made a deliberate CLI-versus-MCP choice for connectors. For the first version, CLI-style pulls for Notion and Google Drive were faster and easier to debug because I could run the command, inspect exactly what came back, retry failures, and save the source snapshot locally.
>
> WhatsApp and Slack context followed the same idea: get the relevant context into the source lane first, then let the wiki ingestion process handle it.
>
> MCP is cleaner if you want a reusable, authenticated tool boundary across many agents and surfaces, but for this build, local CLI tools were the fastest path to a system I could trust and understand.
>
> Then I turned the actual business workflows into skills. For example: draft an SOW, prepare a commercial pricing proposal, build a deck, generate an account brief, prep for a meeting, write a prebrief, summarize a call into next steps, or create a post-meeting pulse.
>
> The skill mattered because each workflow had its own logic: what sources to check, what structure to follow, what claims needed evidence, and what should never be sent without human approval.
>
> The important thing is that this was not "chat with docs." Chat with docs is the toy version. This was closer to converting messy company memory into reviewed business workflows.
>
> The agent could do the first pass and remove a lot of blank-page work, but the operator still owned judgment, pricing, legal or commercial commitments, and anything customer-facing.
>
> On measurable outcome, the clearest ROI was cycle time.
>
> Work that used to take roughly 30 minutes of gathering context about a deal, then another 30 minutes to draft the first version of a proposal, could be reduced to about 2 minutes of automated retrieval and drafting, followed by around 10 minutes of human editing and review.
>
> So I would not frame it as replacing the operator. I would frame it as compressing the prep and drafting loop from around an hour to a much faster review workflow.
>
> The before state was manual searching and rebuilding context from memory. The after state was a retrievable company memory plus repeatable skills that produced review-ready drafts grounded in the right source material.
>
> That reduced blank-page work, made outputs more consistent, and made it much clearer what the agent had used, what still needed review, and where the human needed to make the decision.
>
> The reason I would choose that as the most impressive thing is that it taught me the real enterprise AI lesson. The model is only one piece. The hard part is the harness around it: source-of-truth design, retrieval, workflow fit, permissions, review gates, and adoption by the people actually doing the work.
>
> That maps very directly to OpenAI's enterprise GTM problem. Customers do not just need to be impressed by AI. They need to see how it fits into real work safely enough to scale.

Architecture map, if asked to go deeper:

Use OpenClaw as the agent-operating-layer pattern and Karpathy's LLM Wiki as the knowledge-compounding pattern. My build was the stripped-down local version: fewer moving parts, safer surface area, and just enough retrieval and workflow logic to make GTM work easier.

| Pattern                  | What I learned                                                                                    | What I kept in my version                                                                                       |
| ------------------------ | ------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------- | --------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------- |
| OpenClaw sessions        | Assistants become useful when they have durable state, not just one-off chat turns.               | Wiki/log/task state that survived across sessions.                                                              |
| `SOUL.md` / instructions | The agent needs identity, boundaries, and operating rules.                                        | AGENTS/CLAUDE-style instructions for source boundaries, review behavior, and workflows.                         |
| Tools                    | The model needs hands: read, write, search, run commands, create artifacts.                       | Local CLI tools, QMD search, file inspection, and artifact generation inside a controlled repo.                 |
| Permissions              | Tools are dangerous without approval boundaries.                                                  | Human review before customer-facing, pricing, legal, commercial, or CRM-impacting actions.                      |
| Gateway/channels         | Work should start where people already work.                                                      | Slack, WhatsApp, and Telegram as the main channel pattern, without taking on the whole channel-gateway burden.  |
| Memory/compaction        | Session history alone is not enough; important knowledge needs to survive.                        | Durable markdown memory plus index/log, rather than relying on a single chat context.                           |
| Karpathy raw layer       | Raw sources should stay as evidence rather than being overwritten by the agent.                   | Notion, Google Drive, Slack/WhatsApp context, documents, transcripts, and exports preserved as source material. |
| Karpathy wiki layer      | The LLM should compile knowledge into a maintained wiki, not re-derive everything on every query. | Agent-maintained markdown pages for company memory, account context, and workflow knowledge.                    |
| QMD retrieval            | Once the wiki grows, the index alone is not enough.                                               | Hybrid local search: exact keyword search plus semantic retrieval over markdown.                                |
| Skills                   | Repeatable workflows should become reusable playbooks.                                            | SOW, pricing proposal, deck, account brief, meeting prep, prebrief, postbrief, and follow-up skills.            |

Short version:

> The most impressive thing I built was a company second brain and GTM operating workspace. The narrative is that I read the OpenClaw article and understood the agent pattern: persistent sessions, identity, tools, permissions, memory, channels, scheduling, and multiple agents.
>
> But OpenClaw itself felt too unstable and security-heavy for my use case, so I stripped it down and combined it with Karpathy's LLM Wiki pattern: immutable raw sources, an LLM-maintained markdown wiki, index/log workflows, and retrieval.
>
> Then I added QMD because the document set got too large, and built workflow skills for SOWs, pricing proposals, decks, account briefs, prebriefs, and post-meeting outputs.
>
> The measurable outcome was taking work that could be 30 minutes of deal-context gathering plus 30 minutes of proposal drafting, and compressing it into roughly 2 minutes of automated retrieval and drafting plus about 10 minutes of human review.

Secondary example: NeoBot AI CRM:

> The project I am most proud of is building NeoBot, an open-source AI CRM workspace for real estate agents in Singapore.
>
> https://neobot-ai-crm.vercel.app/
> [Sethzy/neobot-ai-crm](https://github.com/Sethzy/neobot-ai-crm)
>
> NeoBot is a Next.js/Supabase app that uses AI SDK for chat streaming and is built around Claude Managed Agents. A concrete example workflow is: a real estate agent leaves a viewing, dumps a messy voice-note-style update, and NeoBot turns it into a contact, deal, next steps, and a draft WhatsApp follow-up for approval.
>
> The reason I am proud of it is that building this project taught me the fundamentals behind an agent harness: context, CRM state, memory, tools, reusable skills, human-in-the-loop approvals, trigger-based automations, sandboxing, and evals.
>
> It also taught me how to build across the full software development lifecycle: starting with researching relevant reference repos, writing specs by asking the agent to interview me, implementing using TDD, and running adversarial code reviews using multiple models.
>
> The repo has reached 100+ GitHub stars since I open-sourced it, and real estate agents in Singapore have used it in production.

Mini FAQ: Audit before automation:

Q: "How would you make a company ready for AI workflows or a company brain?"

> I would avoid automating a messy workflow too early. The better sequence is audit the workflow, map the handoffs, identify the highest-value bottleneck, do the first pass manually enough to see edge cases, and then automate the repeatable pieces with review gates.
>
> Otherwise AI can scale confusion instead of fixing the work.

Company-brain readiness checklist:

1. One owner per workflow.
2. One source of truth per category: one brand brief, one pricing home, one place product copy lives.
3. Structured data with consistent formats and naming so an agent can parse it.
4. Documented SOPs for the recurring work.
5. Templates for repeated outputs: briefs, outlines, reports, proposals, and handoffs.
6. A tool and subscription tracker covering each tool, login owner, purpose, and renewal context.
7. Mapped data flows showing where each piece of information comes from and where it goes.
8. A QA standard per format, with a written rubric for what good looks like.
9. A decision log covering what changed, who changed it, and why.
10. A feedback loop where shipped work updates the source material and the standard.
#### Why OpenAI?

Core answer:

> I already use codex every day, and AI has changed how I work.
>
> > Hi my name is Seth, and I'm applying for sales development role.
> 
> I'm applying to join Open AI because I believe everyone should have AI working on their behalf. I myself, use Codex everyday for almost everything. 
> 
> 1. As someone without an engineering background, I use it to build software to run my AI consulting business. 
> 2. I use it to do prospecting, find leads, enrich them, and help draft outreach. 
> 3. I use it as my second brain. It pulls articles from x, linkedin and blog posts, and creates a wiki that teaches me concepts back in a way I understand and helps remember everything important. 
> 
> 4. And on a lighter note, I use for planning my family trips.
> 5. I even use it for sending birthday cards now. 
> 
>It is very empowering..... 
>
>I enjoy using AI and thinking about how it can change the way we work. 
>
>If given the opportunity to join OAI, I would enjoy taking the same creativity in identifying new use cases, mapping painpoints, and building high ROI business cases to help leaders in S

Part 2 expanded: 

>
> And honestly, that is part of why OpenAI is so interesting to me commercially. The happy problem is that, technically, almost every enterprise could be an ICP.
>
> The product surface is so broad that the real question is not "can AI help this company?" It is: where is the best-fit workflow wedge, who owns it, what proof would make it real, and how do we turn curiosity into an opportunity the enterprise team can actually work.
>
> Engineering is the obvious one: Codex across the full SDLC, from understanding a codebase to writing, testing, reviewing, debugging, and maintaining software. But sales is just as interesting to me.
>
> In this region, a lot of sellers still do account research by manually clicking around LinkedIn, copying things into notes, and keeping relationship context in their heads. Even if the market is relationship-based, that does not mean the relationship system is strong.
>
> Meeting notes, transcripts, CRM notes, proposals, WhatsApp context, and follow-ups are usually scattered. A system that can pull that together, keep the phone book clean, update the CRM, and prep the seller before the next conversation would still be hugely valuable.
>
> Customer support is another one. Even top banks here still use old IVR phone menus: "press 1 for card waiver, press 2 for fraud transactions," and so on.
>
> Voice agents and support copilots could make that experience much more natural, but the enterprise question is still workflow-first: what can the agent answer, what system does it check, what requires human approval, and how do we make the customer feel helped rather than trapped in automation.
>
> So the opportunity is not one narrow use case. It is learning how to map an enterprise, score the use cases, find the wedge, and help the customer adopt the technology properly. That is exactly the kind of sales problem I want to get good at.
>
> OpenAI is the company I would most want to learn that from because it has such high talent density across GTM and engineering. One person I have learned a lot from publicly is Peter Steinberger, who built OpenClaw and is now at OpenAI working on agents.
>
> Even if i joined and never got to work directly with someone like that, the idea of taking work from people operating at that level and helping customers adopt it is very motivating.
>
> There is that saying: "If you are the smartest person in the room, you are in the wrong room." At OpenAI, I know I would be far from the smartest person in the room. That is part of the appeal.
>
> I would get to learn from the best people in the field, while building my career around helping companies use this technology properly.

#### How Do You Use Codex Daily As Your Main Driver?

Core answer:

> The best way to describe it is that Codex has become the place where my work can live, not just a coding assistant.
>
> The big idea I took from the Codex-maxxing article is that the unit of work is no longer one prompt and one answer. It is an operating loop: a durable thread, shared memory, tools, steering, recurring checks, clear goals, and an artifact surface where I can review the work.
>
> Durable, auto-compacting threads matter because they let me turn Codex into something closer to a personal chief of staff. The important part is not just that the context window is large.
>
> It is that a pinned thread can keep compressing and preserving the important decisions, preferences, tasks, and open loops over time.
>
> Instead of starting from zero every day, I can keep one operating thread that works from a canonical markdown task list, knows the running context, and keeps a tracker of open loops.
>
> The shape is: here is my plan, here are the tasks, here are the accounts or people I am working on, and here are the rules for how I want to operate. On a one-hour heartbeat, check the places where work actually appears: Gmail, Calendar, Slack, WhatsApp where available, and any Sheets or trackers.
>
> Then surface only what matters, flag conflicts, propose what I should do next, and draft the first-pass replies, follow-ups, meeting notes, outreach updates, or research summaries. I still use my own judgment before sending anything, but the expensive part of staying on top of context is already done.
>
> Voice input gets more of my actual thinking into Codex.
>
> The benefit is not speed. It is that the agent gets the unedited version of my thinking. Codex has built-in voice input, but I also use Wispr Flow because system-wide dictation changes how much context I can feed into everything else too.
>
> If I am planning a piece of work, I might say, "I think there is some guy named Ben in Slack who mentioned this, I do not remember exactly what, just go look." That sentence is too vague and annoying to type, but completely natural to say.
>
> The same thing applies to transcripts. If I want to write a post, I can call someone, record the conversation, or talk to them in person with Granola on my phone, then use the transcript as the starting material.
>
> A lot of plans get better when the model has access to the messy version of what I think, not just the polished one.
>
> Voice becomes more useful when combined with steering.
>
> Steering means adding more direction while Codex is already working instead of waiting for the current step to finish. It lets you inject the next message after a tool call.
>
> If I am reviewing a website, I can keep talking while I look at it: make this smaller, this copy is wrong, the spacing between these two things feels off, once this is done, open a PR, wait for the preview deploy, send the preview link to the person who needs to review it on Slack.
>
> I do not need to wait for each step to finish before deciding the next one. I can keep adding intent while the agent is still working, then walk away with the queue already shaped. Later, Heartbeats can monitor the PR or Slack thread after I leave.
>
> The unit of work stops being "one prompt, one answer." It becomes a small operating loop.
>
> Once threads started lasting longer, they needed shared memory outside any one repo.
>
> The important move is saving things to disk. A long thread can remember a lot, but that memory is trapped inside the thread unless the useful parts get written somewhere durable. The point of the memory system is to turn what the thread learns into artifacts I can inspect, edit, diff, and reuse.
>
> Most of my long-running threads start in an Obsidian vault:
>
> ```text
> vault/
> ├── TODO.md
> ├── people/
> ├── projects/
> ├── agent/
> └── notes/
> ```
>
> At the top level, I keep `AGENTS.md` instructions that tell the model to write things down: as you learn more about people, make progress on projects, make a decision, or close an open loop, update the relevant pages in the vault.
>
> The vault is where the agent lives, separate from any one project. Repositories hold code. The vault holds rolling context around my work: people, decisions, open loops, daily notes, project state, and the bits of understanding that would otherwise get lost between threads.
>
> Once a thread has memory, the next question is what it can touch. The useful distinction in my head is: `$browser` is for local web surfaces I want to inspect and annotate; `@chrome` is for signed-in browser state and multiple tabs; `@computer` is for work that only exists as a GUI.
>
> If I am iterating on a local app, I want `$browser`. If I need to work inside a logged-in browser session, I want `@chrome`. If the only way to do the task is to click through a desktop app or portal, I want `@computer`.
>
> That matters a lot for life admin and back-office work because many real workflows do not have clean APIs. Taxes, government portals, booking links, vendor forms, supplier research, expense flows, insurance forms, or account settings often just live behind a website or desktop interface.
>
> I still review and approve the output, but Codex can gather context, click through the boring parts, prepare the draft, and leave me with the decision.
>
> Connectors like Gmail, Calendar, Slack, Sheets, and messaging surfaces are useful because a lot of work starts in inboxes, calendars, and conversations before it becomes a clean task.
>
> Heartbeats and automations make recurring loops possible: check the inbox, prep the task list before I wake up, keep tasks clean without duplicate entries, watch the calendar for conflicts, schedule meetings from booking links, update an outreach tracker or CRM from email activity, research a supplier or partner,
>
> or draft the next follow-up if someone has not replied.
>
> Remote control makes these longer loops feel portable. OpenAI describes Codex mobile as a way to work with Codex from anywhere, and the practical value is that Codex can keep working from the machine where my files, permissions, credentials, and local setup already live while I check in from my phone.
>
> That changes the rhythm of work. I can kick off a task, go for a walk or do groceries, and still review what Codex found, answer a question, approve the next step, or change direction from mobile. The work does not have to pause just because I am not at my desk.
>
> A thread can keep going, and I can give just enough attention to unblock the next move.
>
> Goals are how I try to keep longer tasks honest. A weak goal is just "implement this plan." A strong goal has a success criterion: pass these tests, produce this artifact, verify this output, or stop when this condition is met. That keeps the agent pointed at an outcome rather than just generating motion.
>
> The side panel is probably the biggest shift in how it feels. Codex can create or inspect markdown, spreadsheets, PDFs, slides, HTML pages, local apps, or browser surfaces, and I can look at the same object, annotate it, and review changes. That turns the output into a work product, not just a chat response.
>
> The learning piece is becoming more concrete for me too. When I finish a Codex session and there is a real artifact or diff, I can ask Codex to teach me from the work it just did.
>
> I have an `explain-diff-html` skill that turns a code change into a self-contained HTML lesson: background on the existing system, the intuition behind the change, a walkthrough of the code, diagrams or toy examples, and a short quiz.
>
> That is different from just asking for a summary. If an agent changed a part of the codebase I do not fully understand, the goal is to build my mental model back up to the level where I can review, modify, and explain the work.
>
> Geoffrey Litt had a line I liked about agents writing code that helps humans understand code.
>
> That is exactly the pattern: the agent does not just produce the output; it can also produce the review surface that helps me judge the output.
>
> The key idea is that the artifact becomes the teaching surface. Instead of rubber-stamping a diff, I can ask Codex: explain the existing system, show me why this change works, walk through the important code paths, call out the failure modes, and test me on it.
>
> The HTML format matters because diagrams, annotated snippets, examples, and quiz questions make the explanation much easier to actually review than a wall of markdown.
>
> That captures how I want to use AI generally: not to outsource thinking, but to compress the loop between doing, feedback, and understanding.
>
> So when I say I use Codex daily, I do not mean "I ask it to write things." I mean I use it to keep work moving through persistent context, tools, verification, and review. That is also why OpenAI feels specific to me.
>
> I have already changed my own operating cadence around these tools, and I want to help companies find the places where the same kind of shift is real for them.

Short version:

> Codex is my main workbench. The important shift is that work no longer starts with a blank page or ends in a chat transcript.
>
> A good Codex loop can act like a personal chief of staff: one durable thread, one canonical markdown task list, memory written into files, tools that can inspect inboxes, calendars, booking links, and trackers, heartbeats that initiate scheduled background work, goals with verification,
>
> and a side panel where I can review the artifact or draft before acting.
>
> That is the pattern I think enterprises will move toward too: not AI as a writing shortcut, but AI as an operating loop around real work.

## Product And Technical Fluency

### Product Surfaces And Objection Handling

#### OpenAI Enterprise Adoption Ladder: Codex → Workspace Agents → Frontier/FDE

Prompt: "What does OpenAI do for enterprises? How would you think about selling Codex, Workspace Agents, and larger enterprise deployments?"

Best answer:

> I would think about OpenAI's enterprise motion as an adoption ladder, not as three disconnected products. The question is where the work compounds.
>
> 1. **Codex / work-surface adoption.** This is the individual and team leverage layer. It is strongest and most obvious for engineering teams, where Codex helps people understand codebases, make changes, run checks, review pull requests, debug issues, and delegate more of the software workflow.
>
> But increasingly, the same pattern applies to knowledge work: people can use governed OpenAI work surfaces to research accounts, synthesize documents, analyze spreadsheets, draft briefs or reports, prepare meetings, generate internal tools or artifacts, and turn messy source material into reviewable work.
>
> 2. **Workspace Agents / shared workflow adoption.** This is where the enterprise story gets more interesting. If every employee is just using AI individually, the gains are real but they stay somewhat siloed. Workspace Agents are the bridge from personal productivity into shared company workflows.
>
> The way I would explain them: Workspace Agents are Codex-powered agents that live inside a company's ChatGPT workspace. A team describes a workflow it does often, connects the relevant tools and skills, gives the agent instructions, tests it, shares it with the team, and improves it over time.
>
> The key characteristics are:
>
> 1. **Shared, not personal.** They are built once and reused by a team in ChatGPT or Slack, instead of every person keeping their own prompt stack.
> 2. **Cloud-running.** They can keep working when the user is away, which matters for long-running workflows and scheduled work.
> 3. **Tool-connected.** They can gather context and take action across connected business tools, files, code, and workspace memory.
> 4. **Triggerable.** They can be invoked from ChatGPT or Slack, run on a schedule, or be triggered programmatically through the Workspace Agents API.
> 5. **Governed.** Admins can control who can build, use, and share agents; which tools and actions they can access; and when approvals are required.
> 6. **Production-ready for bounded workflows.** This is not just "POC-friendly." The point is that OpenAI has already packaged a lot of the hard agent infrastructure: Codex-powered cloud execution, instructions, skills, connected tools, approvals, triggers, schedules, API invocation, sharing, and admin controls.
>
> For a bounded workflow, one strong operator inside the customer, like RevOps, IT, sales ops, product ops, or an AI champion, should be able to scope and launch a real production pilot without OpenAI coming in to build it.
>
> OpenAI support becomes more relevant when the workflow needs custom architecture, bespoke connectors or auth, deeper evals, heavier governance, or operating-model change.
>
> This is the "done-for-you agent infra" angle. If a customer DIY-builds the same thing from scratch, they have to configure the sandbox, tool runtime, MCP/connectors, auth, approvals, schedules, channels, traces, evals, and deployment path themselves. That is where FDE-style help starts to make sense.
>
> Workspace Agents package much of that into a friendlier ChatGPT workspace surface: the customer writes the instructions, connects the tools, sets the approvals, and gets a shared agent that can run in the cloud.
>
> Commercially, I would stress that this is greenfield.
>
> The same way OpenClaw-style agents opened up many unexpected personal and work use cases, the TAM for workspace agents across an enterprise is enormous because every function has repeated coordination work that currently lives in docs, Slack threads, spreadsheets, meetings, and individual judgment.
>
> The seller does not need to predict the whole map upfront. The motion is: land the first high-trust use case, measure output, prove the workflow can run safely, and then expand to adjacent workflows and teams.
>
> I would also frame Workspace Agents as early white space. OpenAI introduced them on April 22, 2026, so the category is still new. In many accounts, the competition is not another agent vendor; it is no automation at all.
>
> It is people copying and pasting between Salesforce, Gmail, Slack, spreadsheets, PDFs, dashboards, and ticketing systems.
>
> That means the wedge does not have to be a futuristic transformation pitch.
>
> It can be: find a painful manual queue with enough volume, find one semi-technical champion inside the customer, help them scope the workflow, run a measured production pilot on their side, and then sell the annual budget for scaling it to a full rollout.
>
> The best early Workspace Agent opportunities probably have six traits: high volume, repetitive judgment, clear source systems, one canonical official SOP, a testable eval set, and an obvious reviewer.
>
> If a team processes 10,000 inbound leads, weekly vendor reviews, security questionnaires, customer escalations, product-feedback items, or finance exceptions, a shared agent can create more visible account-level value than asking every employee to become individually better at prompting.
>
> The canonical SOP and evals are important because the agent should not be freelancing the company's position. It should be grounded in one approved playbook, rubric, policy, or brand voice, then battle-tested against realistic examples before rollout.
>
> That is how the company keeps lead scoring, customer messaging, risk decisions, compliance language, and CRM updates consistent as usage scales.
>
> The best version has an owner, a versioned SOP, a small eval suite of pass/fail examples, human review for sensitive actions, and analytics to show whether the workflow is improving.
>
> The last piece is the production feedback loop. Once the agent is live, the customer should sample real runs, review failures, track quality and drift, and feed new edge cases back into the SOP and eval set.
>
> That is the difference between "we launched an agent" and "we now have a workflow that gets more reliable every month." In shorthand: owned, versioned, evaluated, monitored.
>
> Example wedges: lead qualification; renewal-risk summaries; customer escalation routing; RFP and security-questionnaire drafting; product feedback routing; weekly metrics reporting; vendor risk review; procurement intake; finance close workpapers; hiring-loop coordination; incident postmortem drafting; support QA;
>
> field enablement briefs; executive account prep; partner onboarding; policy exception review; and campaign performance retros.
>
> 3. **Frontier or FDE-style deployment / operating-model adoption.** This is for strategic customers who are not just buying seats or configuring one workflow agent.
>
> They are trying to redesign a core business operation around production AI systems: shared context, system integrations, evals, governance, identity, permissions, change management, and measurable business outcomes.
>
> A concrete example is sales lead qualification.
>
> At Stage 1, one rep or BDR uses Codex or ChatGPT to research an account, summarize call notes, pull together buyer context, and draft a follow-up. That is useful, but most of the context and judgment still live with that person.
>
> At Stage 2, the repeated SOP becomes a Workspace Agent. An inbound lead or CRM event can trigger an agent that checks the company website, account notes, previous calls, LinkedIn context, and a qualification rubric; drafts the follow-up; proposes CRM field updates; and asks the rep to approve before anything is sent.
>
> That is a good Workspace Agent because the workflow is bounded: known trigger, known tools, known approval path, and known output.
>
> At Stage 3, the Frontier or FDE line appears when the customer says, "This is useful, but now we want many agents and teams to run from the same revenue operating layer." The problem is no longer just lead qualification.
>
> It is sales, CS, finance, legal, support, and leadership all needing the same source of truth for customers, policies, approvals, risks, and next actions.
>
> For example, imagine the same account later becomes a renewal-risk case. A customer success manager wants to offer a 20% discount to save the renewal. Is that allowed?
>
> The answer may depend on the ARR in Salesforce, open incidents in Zendesk or PagerDuty, product usage trends, the contract in legal, finance discount policy, a Slack thread where a VP approved an exception, and a precedent from a similar customer last quarter.
>
> A single Workspace Agent can fetch some of that, but it does not automatically create the shared business memory that every future sales, CS, finance, and support workflow should trust.
>
> That is the Palantir / context-graph idea in plain English. The company needs a shared operating layer underneath the agents. The ontology is the map of business objects and allowed actions: Account, Contact, Opportunity, Renewal, Incident, Policy, Approval, Discount Exception, Risk, Next Step, and CRM Writeback.
>
> It tells agents what the business objects are, how they relate, who can act on them, and what actions are allowed.
>
> The context graph is the decision memory on top of that map. It records the decision trace: not just the final CRM field, but the path from context to action.
>
> In the renewal example, the system should not only write "20% discount approved." It should record that the customer was worth $X ARR, there were three Sev-1 incidents, usage was down 30%, the contract allowed renewal flexibility, finance policy normally capped discounts at 10%,
>
> the agent found a similar approved exception from last quarter, the CSM requested an exception, Finance approved it, and the CRM was updated with the final terms.
>
> This matters because most systems of record store the final state, not the reasoning. Salesforce may show "discount = 20%." A warehouse may report that the renewal closed.
>
> But the context graph shows why the exception was allowed, which policy applied, what evidence mattered, who approved it, and what precedent it should create.
>
> That turns judgment into reusable company memory.
>
> Next quarter, when another agent or rep sees a similar renewal risk, it does not have to rediscover the rule from scattered Slack messages and tribal knowledge. It can retrieve the precedent, see the evidence, know the boundary conditions, and route the next decision through the right approval path.
>
> It also gives leadership and governance teams an audit trail: if the agent was wrong, they can inspect which source, policy, approval step, or writeback failed and improve the workflow.
>
> Once that layer exists, agents are not just improvising from scattered tools. They are acting on governed business objects, using shared definitions, respecting permissions, leaving audit trails, getting evaluated, and improving the company's memory over time.
>
> That is why this becomes deployment work: OpenAI or a deployment team is helping the customer model how the business actually runs, not just configure one agent.
>
> As a BDR, I would try to qualify the highest credible next stage. If the account has engineering-productivity pain, Codex may be the right starting point. If they have repeated cross-functional workflows, Workspace Agents may be the right conversation.
>
> If they have executive sponsorship for transforming a high-value operation, that becomes a Frontier or deployment conversation.

Short version:

> I would segment the enterprise motion by where the work compounds. Codex compounds individual and team throughput. Workspace Agents compound repeatable workflows by turning bounded SOPs into shared, governed agents.
>
> Frontier and FDE-style deployments compound at the operating-model layer, where many agents need a shared ontology of business objects, a context graph of decision traces, integrations, governance, evals, and change management across workflows.

Seller job:

- Connect the adoption stage to a real workflow and buying committee.
- Identify which employees are already using AI and where developer experimentation is happening.
- Find the workflow that matters enough to fund, the systems it needs to touch, the proof needed to make it real, and the stakeholders who need to trust it.
- For Workspace Agents, push toward a bounded customer-owned production pilot: one workflow, one team, one internal builder, clear approval path, and measurable time saved or revenue impact.
- Treat Workspace Agents as a land-and-expand motion: win one trusted workflow, measure quality, cycle time, throughput, cost saved, or revenue lift, then use that proof to sell an annual rollout budget across adjacent teams.
- Look for manual internal queues before looking for named competitors: copy-paste work, spreadsheet reconciliation, inbox triage, ticket routing, report generation, or review workflows with repeated human judgment.
- Anchor each Workspace Agent in a canonical SOP and eval set so the company can prove the agent is applying the approved process consistently.
- Make sure production traces, user feedback, and reviewed failures feed back into the SOP and eval set after rollout.
- Qualify the highest credible next stage without over-selling transformation too early.

Internal-build objection:

> If a customer already has AI engineers, the question is not "should you build or buy AI?" It is which parts of the agent system the customer should own and which parts should come from a platform.
>
> The customer should own workflow logic, proprietary context, approval rules, UX, system-of-record decisions, and business KPIs.
>
> OpenAI is relevant where maintained platform capability matters: models, tools, orchestration, evals, guardrails, traces, deployment patterns, and enterprise governance.

Discovery questions:

- Is the buyer trying to improve individual productivity, standardize a repeated workflow, or transform a core operation?
- Who owns the pain: engineering leadership, a business-function owner, IT/CIO, or an executive sponsor?
- Is the workflow repeated often enough to become a shared agent?
- Is the current competitor actually another vendor, or is it manual work and no automation?
- What is the volume: leads, tickets, reports, requests, reviews, exceptions, or handoffs per week?
- What is the official SOP, playbook, rubric, policy, or brand voice the agent should follow, and who owns it?
- What eval examples would prove the agent is making consistent decisions before rollout?
- After launch, who reviews real runs, monitors drift, and turns failures into new test cases or SOP updates?
- What systems does it need to touch: CRM, Slack, Drive, support tools, finance systems, code repos, or data warehouse?
- What actions require human approval?
- Is there an internal RevOps, IT, product ops, sales ops, or AI champion who can build and own the first Workspace Agent production pilot without OpenAI services?
- What ROI metric would convert the pilot into annual rollout budget: hours saved, faster SLA, more qualified leads, higher conversion, lower support load, cleaner CRM data, or fewer manual errors?
- What proof would make the customer believe this is safe and worth scaling?
- Does the customer need a productized workflow surface, or deeper deployment help?

Do not say:

- "Codex is only for coding."
- "Workspace Agents replace all custom deployments."
- "Frontier/FDE is available to every customer in the same way."
- "OpenAI will handle all implementation."
- "The agent can run fully autonomously without review."
- "Workspace Agents return API run outputs synchronously today."

#### Stage 1: Codex / Work-Surface Objection Handling

Prompt: "Why should we use Codex instead of Cursor, Claude Code, OpenCode, or open-source models?"

Best answer:

> Yeah, I would not answer this by claiming Codex beats Claude Code or Cursor at every coding task. They are strong products, developer preference matters, and for a pure terminal or IDE decision I would let the engineering team run a real bake-off.
>
> The more strategic question is whether the customer is buying one coding tool or choosing a broader AI work platform.
>
> The July 9 release made OpenAI's answer much clearer. Chat, Work, and Codex now sit inside one ChatGPT desktop app. Chat is the conversational surface, Work lets knowledge workers delegate longer-running outcomes, and Codex gives technical users the deeper environment for working with repositories, terminals, tests, diffs, pull requests, browsers, and multiple agents.
>
> What is interesting is that OpenAI has said code is foundational to both Work and Codex - the toggle changes the experience more than the underlying capability. Codex exposes the technical machinery; Work makes the same kind of agency accessible to someone producing a financial model, a sales deck, a research report, or an internal site.
>
> Around that, OpenAI has its broader multimodal estate - voice, images, video, data analysis, and document creation - plus apps, plugins, skills, MCP connections, computer use, memory, and automations. So ChatGPT is becoming the horizontal super app for work, while Codex is its engineering workbench and part of the execution layer underneath it.
>
> That is the real enterprise wedge. A company can still respect developer tool choice, but it can also standardize how agents access context, take action across systems, produce reviewable artifacts, request approval, and operate under shared administrative controls.
>
> So I would describe Codex as more than a coding assistant. It is the engineering edge - and possibly part of the execution engine - of the broader ChatGPT work platform.
>
> There are four supporting arguments I would lean on if the interviewer wants to go deeper.
>
> 1. **First-party model-plus-harness fit.** If you want to use OpenAI's best frontier models for coding-agent work, Codex is the most natural place to use them because it is the first-party agent loop they can be trained, evaluated, and productized against. Coding-agent quality is not just the raw model.
>
> It is the co-evolution of model and harness.
>
> The key line is: agent products like Codex are "post-trained with models and harnesses in the loop." Useful primitives get discovered in the product, added to the harness, and then used when training and evaluating the next generation of models.
>
> The practical evidence is that the same model can perform differently depending on the agent harness. On the [Terminal-Bench 2.1 leaderboard](https://www.tbench.ai/leaderboard/terminal-bench/2.1), GPT-5.5 with Codex CLI is listed at 83.4%, while GPT-5.5 with the generic Terminus 2 harness is listed at 78.2%.
>
> That does not prove the whole post-training story by itself, but it is a clean proof point that the harness matters in real terminal-agent tasks.
>
> The GPT-5.6 preview family makes that point more concrete. Sol is the flagship model: strongest for frontier reasoning, long-horizon coding-agent work, scientific reasoning, cybersecurity, and difficult tool-heavy workflows.
>
> The clean benchmark pointer is Terminal-Bench 2.1: if someone brings up Fable, the answer is that OpenAI says Sol is the newer state-of-the-art result on the terminal-agent benchmark. The tradeoff is that Sol is the expensive, highest-capability tier and, during preview, access is limited.
>
> Terra is the balanced everyday model: strong lower-cost performance for teams that want GPT-5.6-class capability without always paying for Sol. The tradeoff is that it is not the absolute frontier tier.
>
> Luna is the fastest and most cost-efficient option: the fast path for high-volume, simpler, or latency-sensitive agent steps.
>
> The tradeoff is that it is not the model I would pick for the hardest multi-step engineering problems.
>
> So the point is not just "OpenAI has strong models." It is: when OpenAI releases models like GPT-5.6 Sol, Terra, and Luna, Codex is the first-party surface where those models can be paired with repo context, tools, terminal execution, tests, browser validation, worktrees, diffs, review, approvals, and enterprise controls.
>
> Over time, the model gets better at the harness, and the harness gets better at exposing the right actions to the model. That is the model-plus-product loop I would want to be closest to.
>
> 2. **Multimodal breadth plus price-performance.** Do not say "Claude is not multimodal" too literally; Claude supports text and image input, vision, and text output. The sharper distinction is that OpenAI has the broader first-party multimodal estate.

| Capability                           | OpenAI | Claude  | Clean OpenAI wedge? | Notes                                                                                                                      |
| ------------------------------------ | ------ | ------- | ------------------- | -------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------- |
| Text, reasoning, coding              | Yes    | Yes     | No                  | Both are serious here; do not pretend Anthropic is weak.                                                                   |
| Vision input                         | Yes    | Yes     | No                  | Both support text and image input, vision, and text output.                                                                |
| Image generation / editing           | Yes    | No      | Yes                 | OpenAI has GPT Image models and image generation/editing tools; Claude docs checked frame images as input / analysis.      |
| Realtime voice                       | Yes    | No      | Yes                 | OpenAI has GPT-Realtime models for low-latency speech-to-speech agents.                                                    |
| Speech / transcription / translation | Yes    | No      | Yes                 | OpenAI has speech generation, transcription, realtime transcription, and live translation models.                          |
| Video generation                     | Yes    | No      | Yes                 | OpenAI has Sora / video generation models.                                                                                 |
| Embeddings / retrieval               | Yes    | Partial | Yes                 | OpenAI has embeddings, vector stores, and file search; Anthropic points to Voyage for embeddings.                          |
| Files, PDFs, data analysis           | Yes    | Yes     | No                  | Both can do serious document, data, and code work.                                                                         |
| Tool use, web search, MCP            | Yes    | Yes     | No                  | Both have serious tool and agent infrastructure.                                                                           |
| Computer use                         | Yes    | Yes     | No                  | Claude is strong here too.                                                                                                 |
| Shared work surfaces                 | Yes    | Yes     | Partial             | Both have agent surfaces; OpenAI's argument is one estate across employees, engineers, builders, and multimodal workflows. |

> That matters because an enterprise rollout quickly stops being only "which terminal agent do developers like?" Support wants voice, marketing wants images and video, sales wants account research and CRM workflows, product wants analysis, and engineering wants coding agents.
>
> OpenAI can credibly be the multimodal AI estate, not just a point coding tool.
>
> The pricing point reinforces this: OpenAI is not always the cheapest, but GPT-5.4 and GPT-5.4 mini sit on or near the price-performance frontier versus comparable Claude tiers, so the buyer gets broad capability without defaulting to the most expensive agent loop.
>
> 3. **Quality of life for both coding work and knowledge work.** The stronger Codex argument is not only governance. It is that Codex is becoming a work surface for everyone. OpenAI says Codex now has more than 5 million weekly active users, up more than 6x since the desktop app launched in February.
>
> Developers are still the biggest group, but knowledge workers are now about 20% of users and are growing more than 3x as fast.
>
> That matters because Cursor and Devin are strongest as developer tools, and Claude Code is probably the closest peer as an agentic workbench.
>
> But Codex is explicitly designed around a wider operating loop: reports, spreadsheets, presentations, contracts, research, data analysis, workflow automation, lightweight internal tools, and software engineering.
>
> The quality-of-life details point in that direction: durable threads, compaction, voice input, steering while the agent is already working, skills, automations and heartbeats, plugins/connectors, browser use, computer use, mobile remote control, side-panel artifact review, plus the engineering loop of repo context,
>
> terminal, tests, worktrees, diffs, code review, and PRs.
>
> For a developer, that means Codex can understand the codebase, make a change, run tests, inspect the rendered app, review the diff, and leave a PR-quality artifact.
>
> For a knowledge worker, it means Codex can start from messy context, work across the places where work actually lives, create a report, spreadsheet, deck, brief, dashboard, contract draft, or internal tool, then leave an artifact a human can inspect and improve.
>
> That is much closer to a personal chief-of-staff loop than a coding-only assistant.
>
> 4. **Enterprise standardization.** "Some engineers are faster with AI" is useful, but it is not yet an enterprise thesis.
>
> The bigger question is whether the company can standardize AI-assisted software development across the SDLC: planning, design, codebase understanding, implementation, testing, review, debugging, deployment, maintenance, and feedback.
>
> That matters because large companies do not only care whether one power user is faster.
>
> They care whether every team can adopt the tool safely; whether junior and senior engineers use it consistently; whether code quality improves or at least stays controlled; whether security and compliance teams know what is happening; whether AI usage is visible, governable, and auditable;
>
> whether best practices can spread across the organization; and whether the company can reduce cycle time without creating unmanaged risk.
>
> Codex is useful here because the workflow can become shared process instead of scattered individual habit: repo instructions, skills, test commands, review rubrics, PR templates, security checks, worktrees, diffs, logs, approvals, and repeatable plan / implement / test / report loops.
>
> It also gives the company one control plane for tools, permissions, approvals, auditability, and spend, while turning individual prompt hacks into reusable company process.
>
> So the live answer is: if a team loves Claude Code or Cursor, I would not rip it out on day one. I would ask whether the company is buying a point coding tool or standardizing an AI work surface. If it is only a terminal or IDE preference, let developers prove the best tool in a bakeoff.
>
> But if leadership wants governed AI adoption across engineering and the rest of the company, Codex is the natural OpenAI wedge: one agent loop people want to use, plus enterprise controls for adoption, permissions, auditability, and spend before every team's AI usage becomes a separate shadow budget.

Discovery questions:

- What are engineers using today: Cursor, Copilot, Claude Code, Codex, OpenCode, Qwen Code, internal tools, or a mix?
- Is the buyer evaluating a developer productivity tool, or trying to standardize an AI work surface across engineering, sales, ops, finance, legal, research, marketing, and support?
- Is engineering trying to make individual developers faster, or standardize AI-assisted SDLC across planning, design, coding, testing, review, deployment, maintenance, and feedback?
- Is the use case autocomplete/chat, or longer-running engineering work like bug reproduction, tests, review, migrations, refactoring, CI failures, and maintenance?
- Which engineering processes should become shared skills or reusable loops: repo onboarding, planning, test commands, review rubrics, PR templates, security checks, CI repair, migrations, or release prep?
- Which non-engineering teams already have AI pull: analysts making reports, operators checking trackers, sales teams doing account research, finance teams reviewing documents, legal teams drafting or summarizing, marketers creating assets, or support teams triaging cases?
- Does the team want individual IDE productivity, a governed engineering workflow, or both?
- What workflow quality-of-life matters most: durable context, mobile review, voice input, scheduled checks, file/artifact review, connectors, browser/computer use, or Git/test/PR integration?
- What matters most: developer preference, model choice, openness, local control, enterprise governance, auditability, SDK/MCP integration, first-party OpenAI model fit, or cost?
- Where do coding agents fail today: wrong context, tool misuse, no tests, weak review, security concerns, poor traceability, or inability to run longer tasks?
- Is the company trying to standardize employee AI access, engineering AI access, or build its own AI product/workflow layer?

Do not say:

- "Cursor is bad."
- "OpenCode is not enterprise."
- "Claude Code is weak."
- "Open-source models are useless."
- "Codex wins every benchmark."
- "OpenAI is always cheaper."
- "OpenAI models only work well in Codex."
- "Codex is fully autonomous, so engineers do not need review."
- "The OpenAI harness article proves every customer can reproduce the same result."
- "Codex is only for developers."

#### Stage 2: Workspace Agents Objection Handling

Prompt: "If customers can build their own agents, why Workspace Agents? What does OpenAI own versus what does the customer own? Why use Workspace Agents instead of building my own agent runtime or forking an open-source harness like OpenCode?"

Visual frame:

![Agent harness rent-versus-own matrix](/Users/sethlim/Documents/openai-interview-prep/_attachments/images/agent-harness-rent-vs-own-matrix-2026-07-04.jpeg)

Use this as a mental model, not as an official benchmark. The useful distinction is capability versus freedom. A tightly matched model-plus-harness can feel very powerful because the model, tools, runtime, product surface, and ecosystem are tuned together.
The cost is that more of the workflow lives on the vendor's terms.

Open or self-built harnesses give more portability and model choice, but the customer owns more setup, maintenance, security, and operational burden.

Best answer:

> The first thing I would lead with is accessibility. For most business teams, the blocker is not imagination. They can usually see the repeated workflow they want to automate: qualify this lead, prepare this account brief, triage this support ticket, summarize this weekly report, route this request.
>
> The blocker is that the moment they try to make it real, it becomes an engineering project: build the runtime, connect the tools, handle permissions, set up Slack, manage logs, add approvals, track usage, and keep the thing working.
>
> Workspace Agents make that first step much easier. For a non-technical business operator, it is one of the easiest and fastest ways to set up a useful agent in an enterprise environment.
>
> The person closest to the work can describe the workflow in plain English, connect the approved tools and context, test it, share it in ChatGPT or Slack, and improve it with the team.
>
> That is the emotional unlock: the operator who understands the work does not have to wait for a full engineering roadmap before they can start turning the workflow into something repeatable.
>
> Then the clean business frame is: the customer owns the business logic, and OpenAI packages the agent infrastructure.
>
> I would not tell a buyer that OpenAI understands their workflow better than they do.
>
> The customer owns the parts that make the workflow real: the SOP, the source of truth, the decision rubric, the edge cases, the approval rules, the tone, the risk threshold, the output format, the human reviewer, and the business metric.
>
> The motivation is basically the same as the Vercel Eve framing: agents today are where the web was before frameworks, with everyone hand-rolling the same plumbing and nothing carrying over to the next one.
>
> Durable sessions, a sandbox-like cloud workspace to run code, approvals, tracing, evals: every team rebuilds these before their agent does anything useful, and none of it transfers to the next project.
>
> Workspace Agents package that infrastructure as the shared enterprise surface, so the governed production path is built in from the first run instead of bolted on at the end.
>
> What OpenAI gives them is the generic but painful plumbing around that logic: a shared agent surface inside ChatGPT and Slack, Codex-powered cloud execution, files and working memory, connected apps and tools, custom MCPs, skills, schedules, API triggers, sharing, approvals for sensitive actions, analytics,
>
> version history, and admin controls.
>
> So a sales ops, RevOps, support ops, or finance ops person can turn a repeated workflow into a shared, governed agent without first asking engineering to build an internal agent runtime.
>
> The build-versus-buy question then becomes: are you trying to operationalize a business workflow, or are you trying to own an agent platform?
>
> If you need provider choice, self-hosting, custom security boundaries, deep product integration, or model routing across different vendors, then owning or forking a harness can be the right call.
>
> But it means you also own auth, connectors, permissions, sandboxing, state, memory, logs, evals, approvals, UI, Slack distribution, monitoring, upgrades, and support.
>
> Workspace Agents are compelling when the goal is faster adoption of repeatable, team-facing workflows: lead qualification, account research, weekly reporting, support triage, product feedback routing, vendor risk review, procurement intake, finance close prep, or sales follow-up.
>
> The business team can describe the workflow in plain English, test it, connect approved tools, share it in ChatGPT or Slack, and improve it over time.
>
> So I would not frame this as proprietary versus open source. I would frame it as rent the frontier versus own the harness. Rent the frontier when speed, model fit, governance, and adoption matter. Own the harness when portability and custom control matter.
>
> Either way, the customer should keep the portable assets in their own hands: SOPs, rubrics, examples, eval cases, source-of-truth docs, approval rules, and success metrics.
>
> That is the commercial point: it is not "OpenAI does the business process for you." It is "bring your business process, and OpenAI gives you a safer, faster way to turn it into a governed workflow."

Plain-language example: inbound lead qualification

> Take inbound lead qualification. The customer owns the ICP definition, scoring rubric, territory rules, CRM fields, disqualification criteria, follow-up tone, approval policy, and success metric. OpenAI does not invent those.
>
> But a Workspace Agent can operationalize the workflow: when a lead comes in, it can gather account context, check the CRM, read approved sales collateral, compare the lead against the qualification rubric, draft a follow-up, propose CRM updates, post a deal brief in Slack,
>
> and ask for approval before anything sensitive is sent or written back.
>
> The value is not that the agent magically knows the company's sales motion. The value is that the company can encode its sales motion once, connect the approved tools, share the agent with the team, review usage, and improve it centrally.

Optional market proof:

> I would only use this if there is time, but the broader market signal is clear. Companies like Tasklet and Lindy have shown that business users want plain-English agents that connect to tools and actually do work.
>
> The demand is not just for chatbots; it is for non-technical operators to turn repeated work into working automations without waiting for a full engineering roadmap.
>
> OpenAI is well positioned because Workspace Agents sit inside the ChatGPT work surface, connect to team tools, run in the cloud, and come with enterprise controls.

If the buyer says, "We have an internal AI team":

> I would treat that as a positive signal. It means they understand the opportunity.
>
> Then I would ask a more precise question: which parts do you want your AI team to own forever, and which parts are generic infrastructure?
>
> If the differentiated value is the workflow logic, proprietary context, custom UX, or deep system-of-record orchestration, they should absolutely own that.
>
> But I would not assume they want scarce AI engineering time spent rebuilding shared agent surfaces, Slack deployment, schedules, API triggers, app authentication, write-action approvals, permissions, analytics, admin controls, and ongoing platform maintenance.
>
> The practical answer is usually hybrid: customer-owned business logic on top of OpenAI-managed agent infrastructure. If the workflow becomes deeply productized or needs application-owned orchestration, then it may graduate into the Responses API, Agents SDK, custom MCPs, or a Frontier / deployment conversation.

If the buyer says, "Why not fork OpenCode, OpenHands, Goose, or build our own harness?":

> I would say that is a credible path if the customer has the technical team and a real reason to own the loop.
>
> Open-source or model-agnostic harnesses are attractive when they need provider choice, self-hosting, custom security boundaries, internal platform control, deep product integration, or the ability to route different steps to different models over time.
>
> But I would make the cost explicit. Owning the harness means owning the runtime, connectors, auth, permissions, sandboxing, memory, traces, evals, approval flows, sharing, admin controls, model upgrades, and ongoing maintenance. That is not wrong. It is just a product commitment.
>
> Workspace Agents are the better starting point when the workflow is team-facing, repeatable, and business-owned: weekly reporting, lead qualification, account research, support triage, product feedback routing, software request review, vendor risk review, procurement intake, or sales follow-up.
>
> The buyer can start quickly, learn where the real workflow value is, and still keep the portable parts of the process: SOPs, rubrics, examples, eval cases, source-of-truth docs, and approval rules.
>
> So I would not frame this as proprietary versus open-source ideology. I would ask: is the customer trying to automate one high-value workflow now, or build a long-term internal agent platform? If it is the former, Workspace Agents are compelling.
>
> If it is the latter, OpenAI can still be part of the stack through the API, Agents SDK, Codex, custom MCPs, and eval-led deployment patterns.

If the buyer says, "Why not just build this in code with the SDK?":

> That is a real option, and I would route based on the workflow. If the customer is building an agent inside their own product, or they need to own orchestration, state, approvals, custom UI, and deep system behavior, then code-first with Responses API or the Agents SDK may be the better path.
>
> Workspace Agents are strongest when the workflow is repeatable, team-facing, and business-owned. The customer can start with one bounded workflow, prove adoption and value, then decide whether it should stay as a Workspace Agent, graduate into a coded agent, or become part of a broader deployment conversation.

If the buyer says, "Isn't this just another GPT or chatbot?":

> I would separate the concepts. A chatbot answers a message. A Workspace Agent is closer to a shared workflow worker. It can be built around a repeated SOP, connected to approved tools, run in ChatGPT or Slack, run on a schedule, be triggered by an API, ask for approval, and be shared across a team.
>
> So the question is not "can it chat?" The question is: can it move a bounded workflow forward using the company's actual process, tools, and review path?

If the buyer says, "Why not just use n8n, Make, Zapier, or Workato?":

> I would respect that, because workflow automation tools are very good at deterministic orchestration. If the process is basically: trigger fires, branch on known rules, call an API, transform data, update a system, retry on failure, and log the result, n8n or Workato may be exactly the right layer.
>
> The question I would ask is: where does the judgment live?
>
> If the judgment is stable and can be turned into rules, keep it deterministic. But if the process requires reading messy context, applying a business rubric, using examples, drafting something a human will review, and improving the SOP over time, that is where Workspace Agents become more interesting.
>
> A Workspace Agent gives the team a shared ChatGPT or Slack surface, skills and files, connected tools, schedules, API triggers, approvals, sharing, and admin controls around that repeatable judgment loop.
>
> So I would not position it as "Workspace Agents replace n8n." The mature pattern may be hybrid. n8n handles triggers, webhooks, retries, and deterministic handoffs. Workspace Agents own the team-facing SOP, context review, draft, recommendation, approval path, and learning loop.

If the buyer worries about governance:

> I would make governance concrete. Who can build the agent? Who can run it? Which tools can it access? Which actions require approval? Which data sources are connected? What does the agent do in Slack versus ChatGPT? How is usage measured? Who reviews failures? Who owns the SOP and versioning?
>
> That is actually the reason Workspace Agents are interesting for enterprises. The alternative is often not a beautifully governed internal agent platform. It is shadow AI: people copying data between tabs, prompts, spreadsheets, Slack, CRM, and docs with no shared process.

Discovery questions:

- What repeated workflow is painful enough to operationalize, not just prompt around?
- Is the buyer trying to operationalize one bounded workflow now, or build an internal agent platform?
- Who owns the SOP today: sales ops, RevOps, support ops, product ops, IT, finance, legal, or a business team?
- What needs to stay portable: SOPs, prompts, skills, eval cases, traces, source documents, or model-routing policy?
- Does the customer actually need model-provider choice, self-hosting, or deep harness control?
- Who will maintain the runtime if they build or fork it?
- What already runs in a workflow tool today: n8n, Make, Zapier, Workato, Salesforce Flow, custom scripts, cron, or an internal platform?
- What is the source of truth: Salesforce, HubSpot, Intercom, Zendesk, Notion, Google Drive, Slack, Snowflake, a data warehouse, or something else?
- What exact judgment does the human apply today: qualification, routing, priority, risk level, approval status, tone, pricing exception, policy match, or next action?
- Which actions are safe to automate, and which need approval?
- What output would make the workflow useful: Slack brief, CRM update, email draft, ticket summary, structured fields, report, workpaper, or handoff note?
- What would prove value in 30-60 days: time saved, faster response, cleaner CRM data, higher conversion, fewer escalations, better SLA, reduced review burden, or more consistent policy adherence?
- Who will maintain the workflow after launch: the business owner, an ops person, an AI champion, IT, or engineering?

Good sound bite:

> Rent the frontier when speed, model fit, governance, and adoption matter. Own the harness when portability and custom control matter. Either way, the customer should own the judgment, and OpenAI should make that judgment easier to turn into a governed, repeatable workflow.

Do not say:

- "Workspace Agents replace internal AI teams."
- "OpenAI owns the customer's business process."
- "Open-source harnesses are bad."
- "Workspace Agents eliminate the need for SDKs or custom agent builds."
- "The harness chart is an official OpenAI benchmark."
- "The customer does not need to define the SOP."
- "Every workflow should be a Workspace Agent."
- "Workspace Agents are fully autonomous for sensitive actions."
- "API triggers return a synchronous answer." The current trigger API queues the run and returns `202 Accepted`.
- "Connector action constraints mean the model can only see filtered data." Connector/action permissions govern actions, but source access still needs careful configuration and review.

#### Workspace Agents — Coordination Cost, Adoption, And Margin FAQ

Use this FAQ to explain the economic and operating-model case for Workspace Agents. The framing comes from Daniel Kornum's July 11, 2026 article, *AI's Biggest Winners Have the Lowest Margins*. The article is an external practitioner point of view, not an OpenAI source or independent industry study. Its language is useful; its labour percentages, client economics, and "8-figure margin uplift" claim should not be repeated as verified facts without underlying customer evidence.

The current official product basis is narrower and concrete: Workspace Agents let teams save repeatable work using instructions, connected context, app actions, approvals, and output formats. API triggers let an outside system start the saved workflow when work begins outside ChatGPT. Triggered runs are asynchronous and write results to a connected destination rather than returning a completed response synchronously. See [Trigger a Workspace Agent from the API](https://developers.openai.com/cookbook/examples/chatgpt/workspace_agents/workspace-agents-api-trigger).

##### What Is The Core Economic Thesis?

> The biggest winners of AI need not be the companies with the most engineers, the largest data teams, or the highest software budgets.
>
> They may be the companies with the lowest margins: manufacturers, trucking carriers, distributors, staffing agencies, field-service operators, retailers, healthcare operators, and other businesses whose economics depend on coordinating a large amount of human work reliably.
