Enterprises need to know who can access what, what the model can see, what actions it can take, and where boundaries are enforced.                                                                                                                                                         |
| Evals                           | Without evals, you do not know whether the system works reliably. A good demo is not the same as measured behavior across real examples, edge cases, failure modes, and quality criteria.                                                                                                                                                                                                                     |
| Tracing, monitoring, and logs   | Without tracing, monitoring, and logs, failure modes are invisible. You cannot debug what context was retrieved, what tool was called, why the answer changed, where cost went, or whether quality is drifting.                                                                                                                                                                                               |
| Latency and cost controls       | Without latency and cost controls, the workflow may not scale even if the answer quality is good. The product still has to make tradeoffs around model choice, context size, tool calls, response time, and budget.                                                                                                                                                                                           |
| Human review and escalation     | Without human review and escalation, high-consequence workflows are not safe enough. The system needs to know when to ask for approval, hand off to a person, or stop.                                                                                                                                                                                                                                        |
| Feedback loop                   | Without a feedback loop, the system does not improve from production use. Reviewed outputs, corrections, decision traces, and user feedback should flow back into better prompts, retrieval, eval datasets, and workflow design.                                                                                                                                                                              |

Bottom line:

> The harness is the product system around the model: context, retrieval, tools, skills, sandbox, schemas, state, permissions, evals, observability, cost controls, review paths, and feedback loops.

#### RAG

> RAG means retrieval-augmented generation. Instead of relying only on the model's training data, the system searches an approved knowledge source first: company documents, policies, tickets, CRM notes, account history, or a vector store.
>
> It retrieves the most relevant context, passes that into the model, and the model uses it to answer.
>
> I would not describe it as just "vector search plus prompt." A serious enterprise retrieval layer usually combines keyword search for exact things, embeddings for meaning, metadata for scope and permissions, reranking for better evidence, and citations so the user can inspect the source.
>
> The reason it matters is that enterprise questions depend on current, internal, permissioned knowledge. A support team needs the current policy. A sales team needs the latest account context. A bank, healthcare company, or legal team needs controlled knowledge, not generic internet memory.
>
> Long context does not replace this. Even if a model has a very large context window, you still do not want to shove the whole company into the prompt. The job of RAG is to route the right slices of knowledge into the model before it reasons.
>
> But RAG is not a guarantee of truth. Retrieval can pull the wrong source, stale source, or a source the user should not have access to. The enterprise-ready version needs source quality, permissions, freshness, metadata, citations, evals, monitoring, and human review for sensitive work.

Mini FAQ if pushed:

**Is RAG just embeddings?**

> No. Embeddings are one retrieval technique, but they are not the whole system. Keyword or BM25 search is better for exact anchors like product names, customer names, IDs, policy titles, dates, error messages, or document names. Embeddings are better when the wording does not match.
>
> For example, "how do we handle refunds?" might retrieve a page called "chargeback policy" because the meaning is similar even if the exact word is different.

**What does metadata mean in plain language?**

> Metadata is the label around a document. It tells the system whether that source is allowed, current, relevant, account-specific, and authoritative for this exact user and workflow.

**Why does metadata matter?**

> Because enterprise retrieval cannot be one giant soup of documents. A support agent should not answer a Singapore enterprise customer using an outdated US SMB policy. A sales assistant should not mix Coinbase account notes with Airtable account notes.
>
> A healthcare workflow should not surface a clinical note unless the user has permission to see it.

**What kinds of metadata matter?**

> Scope, permissions, source type, account, owner, and freshness. In plain terms: is this global or APAC-only? Is the user allowed to see it? Is it an approved help article, a draft, a Slack comment, a contract, or an old support ticket? Does it belong to this customer?
>
> Who owns it: Legal, Product, Support, Security, or Sales?
>
> Was it updated recently, or superseded two years ago?

**Why not just use a long-context model?**

> Long context helps, but it does not remove the need for retrieval. Even a 200K-token model can struggle once you stuff in 100 to 200 pages of mixed material, and enterprise knowledge bases are much larger than that.
>
> My rough mental model is that useful context can start to degrade around the low six figures, roughly 120K tokens, when signal-to-noise gets bad. RAG keeps the model focused on the right sources.

**What can go wrong with RAG?**

> Indexing can be weak, chunks can be badly split, metadata can be missing, retrieval can find the wrong source, sources can be stale, permissions can be wrong, and the model can overstate the evidence. That is why serious RAG needs source quality, evals, monitoring, and review.

**Why do sources get stale so often?**

> Because companies change faster than their knowledge bases. Policies change, pricing changes, product behavior changes, security rules change, customer entitlements change, legal language changes, and one-off exceptions get made in tickets or Slack before the official doc is updated.
>
> The old version often still exists somewhere: in a help article, PDF, onboarding deck, contract, archived ticket, sales note, or copied internal page.
>
> RAG does not automatically know which version is true. If the stale source is still indexed and semantically matches the question, it can be retrieved confidently even though it is outdated.
>
> That is why freshness metadata, source ownership, approval status, last-updated dates, deprecation rules, and monitoring matter so much.
>
> In a real enterprise, retrieval quality is partly a knowledge-ops problem, not just a model problem.

**How would I explain the value to a buyer?**

> RAG is how you make the AI work with the company's actual knowledge instead of generic memory. The better version is not "we embedded some docs." It is a governed retrieval system that finds the right source, checks whether it is allowed and current, shows the evidence, and uses the model to reason over that evidence.

#### What Is Fine-Tuning?

> Fine-tuning is model adaptation. You start with a strong base model, then continue training it on examples, preferences, or reward signals so it behaves better on a specific repeated task.
>
> The key distinction is that fine-tuning changes behavior; it is not the default way to upload current company knowledge.
>
> If the issue is "the model needs current policies, pricing, account notes, or product docs," I would start with RAG or File Search because the knowledge should stay external, refreshable, permissioned, and auditable.
>
> I would say this pretty directly: fine-tuning is not knowledge injection. It can teach style, terminology, output structure, decision patterns, and domain habits.
>
> For example, in healthcare it can help the model handle terminology like "MI" meaning myocardial infarction in the right context, or follow a medical-report review rubric.
>
> But you cannot just shove 50,000 company documents into fine-tuning and expect the model to become a clean, current, permissioned knowledge base. That risks overfitting, stale facts, behavior drift, or catastrophic forgetting, and it makes the system harder to audit.
>
> Fine-tuning becomes more interesting when the model already has the right context but keeps failing the same pattern. Maybe it applies the wrong classification rubric. Maybe the output format is inconsistent. Maybe it struggles with a domain-specific reasoning pattern.
>
> Maybe the team wants to reduce prompt overhead for a repeated behavior.
>
> In those cases, with enough high-quality examples and evals, fine-tuning or model optimization can make sense.
>
> A simple medical-report example makes the distinction clear. RAG can give the model the current patient file, latest policy, and relevant clinical notes. But if the model keeps missing the same findings, extracting the wrong fields, or applying the wrong review rubric, fine-tuning can help.
>
> You train on many medical reports that humans already reviewed and labeled: report type, important findings, fields to extract, missing information, and next step. The goal is not to put patient facts into the model.
>
> The goal is to make the model better at reading that kind of report and following the same review pattern consistently.
>
> The broader enterprise point is that every industry has quirks that can make or break the system. Healthcare has clinical abbreviations and care-pathway nuance. Financial services has filings, risk language, policy exceptions, and product-specific jargon.
>
> Legal work has precedents, clause history, jurisdiction, and citation discipline.
>
> Those quirks usually need a mix of retrieval, metadata, evals, review, and sometimes fine-tuning for repeatable behavior. They should not be treated as one big knowledge upload.
>
> I would not position it as the first lever. I would usually start with model choice, prompting, examples, retrieval, tools, structured outputs, and evals. Then if the same failure repeats across enough examples and the task is clear enough to measure, fine-tuning becomes a serious option.
>
> I would also check current OpenAI docs before promising the exact supported method, because fine-tuning availability and recommended approaches change.

Mini FAQ if pushed:

- Supervised fine-tuning uses labeled input-output examples.
- Direct preference optimization uses preferred versus rejected outputs to steer model behavior.
- Reinforcement fine-tuning uses a grader or reward signal, which can matter for complex tasks where quality can be scored.
- Good fine-tuning needs clean examples, a validation set, evals, and a stable failure pattern.
- Bad fine-tuning can lock in weak examples, overfit, bake in stale facts, or make behavior harder to audit.

### Reliability, Evals, And Safety Concepts

#### Hallucination

> I would frame hallucination less as "the model is dumb" and more as a context-engineering failure. The model is like a very capable analyst with limited working memory.
>
> If you give it the wrong file, stale data, missing information, or no tool to check the source of truth, it can still produce a fluent but unsupported answer. And because of how these systems are designed, they are often eager to complete the task.
>
> They are not naturally as good as a human at saying, "I do not have enough evidence to answer this."
>
> So the enterprise question is: have we loaded the right context, connected the right tools, constrained the output, and created a human review path where the stakes are high?
>
> And then: have we run evals over a representative dataset? For example, take real or realistic support tickets, account questions, policy questions, or compliance scenarios where we know the correct answer, the correct source, and the expected escalation behavior.
>
> Then test whether the system retrieves the right evidence, uses the right tools, cites the right source, avoids unsupported claims, follows the output format, and escalates when confidence is low. That turns hallucination from a vague fear into something you can measure, debug, and improve.

Strong line:

> For regulated buyers, I would not sell certainty. I would sell a controlled workflow: right context, right tools, measurable behavior, visible failures, and review paths for high-stakes decisions.

#### Explain Evals Simply

> So what are evals? Short for "evaluations," they're a comprehensive, rigorous framework to systematically measure and improve an AI system. We're not talking thumbs up/down or even human review of agent outputs.
>
> A strong evaluation suite captures the nuances of judgment, tone, and taste; assesses agentic use of tools; breaks down tasks into specific, scorable dimensions (a "rubric")
>
> This shift to an eval-first mentality isn't just technical plumbing.
>
> It's a change in how we define success for AI: moving from "let's see what it does" to "let's measure precisely what it should do, and improve it until it does." The organizations that figure this out now will turn AI from a cost center into a durable, compounding asset.
>
> In my own words, that means evals are the measurement system for an AI workflow. They force the team to define what good looks like in a way you can actually test: correctness, source grounding, tone, judgment, tool use, escalation behavior, cost, latency, and failure handling.
>
> Practically, the basic pieces are: a dataset of test cases, the run or trace, and graders. The dataset is the set of examples you want the system to handle. The trace is the record of what happened: prompts, retrieved context, tool calls, handoffs, outputs, latency, cost, and errors.
>
> The graders are the checks you apply.
>
> Some graders are deterministic, like "did it call the right tool?" or "did it return the required fields?" Some can use another model as a judge for softer criteria like tone, clarity, or groundedness.
>
> And for high-judgment areas, you still want a human subject-matter expert reviewing examples, because not everything important can be reduced to a simple automated check.
>
> For an enterprise support agent, an eval suite might include 100 realistic tickets: refund requests, angry customers, account access issues, billing questions, policy edge cases, and cases that should escalate.
>
> For each one, you define what good looks like: did the agent retrieve the right SOP, check the right customer record, use the right tool, avoid unsupported claims, draft an appropriate response, and escalate when it should?
>
> A concrete test case could be: an Intercom ticket comes in saying, "I was charged twice for my Pro plan renewal;
>
> please refund one charge." The expected path is that the agent checks the Salesforce account, checks Stripe for duplicate charges, retrieves the Notion refund SOP, drafts an empathetic response, recommends a refund for the duplicate charge, and asks for billing approval before any money movement.
>
> Then the eval checks the actual run. Did it use the right tools: Intercom, Salesforce, Stripe, and Notion? Did it cite the Stripe charge and refund SOP? Did it avoid saying "your refund has been processed" before approval?
>
> Did it return the right fields: issue summary, evidence, recommended action, risk label, and approval status?
>
> Did the tone sound appropriate for a frustrated customer? If it missed the SOP, used stale data, or skipped the approval step, that failure becomes something the team can debug.
>
> On dataset size, I would not give one fake universal number. Typically, we see a recurring pattern: start small, but make the examples real and representative. For a first eval loop, even 5-10 cases can expose obvious issues.
>
> For a proper regression baseline, I would aim for 20-50 high-signal cases from real tickets, manual QA, or actual failures.
>
> As the workflow gets closer to production, I would expand into the tens or low hundreds across core use cases, edge cases, and known failure modes. For high-risk workflows, run multiple trials on the same cases because model outputs can vary. The goal is not a huge dataset for its own sake.
>
> The goal is coverage of the behavior that actually matters.
>
> In a sales conversation, this is the point I would land: evals turn AI quality from vibes into an operating loop. You can compare prompts, models, retrieval changes, tools, and guardrails against the same task set.
>
> You can also catch regressions: if the system used to handle refunds correctly and now fails after a change, the eval tells you before customers do. The companies that get this right build compounding internal assets, because their workflows, domain judgment, and edge cases become reusable measurement infrastructure.

Source note: Garrett Lord's eval-first X Article is indexed in Seth Second Brain at `/Users/sethlim/Documents/Seth Second Brain/raw/intentional/x/2068754262440767500-garrettlord-evals-the-strategic-ip-that-will-define-the-next-era-of-ai-we-ve-spoken-to-hun.md`.

##### Mini FAQ: Is The Main Lever Just Changing The Prompt?

> Not really. Prompting is one lever, and often the fastest one, but evals are really a diagnostic loop. Evals tell you what failed, and then you choose the right improvement lever.
>
> If the agent misunderstood the task, improve the prompt or instructions.
>
> If it gave a fluent but unsupported answer, improve retrieval, source quality, citations, or context.
>
> If it did not check Stripe before recommending a refund, improve the tool flow or make the tool call mandatory.
>
> If it skipped approval before money movement, add a workflow rule, guardrail, or human review gate.
>
> If the output was hard to use downstream, enforce structured outputs or a schema.
>
> If it used the wrong data, fix permissions, metadata, freshness, or source-of-truth routing.
>
> If the model just cannot handle the reasoning reliably, change the model, add examples, or consider fine-tuning for a narrow repeatable task.
>
> If quality degrades after changes, use regression evals to catch it before production.
>
> The point is not "write a better prompt" in the abstract. The point is to identify why the system failed and improve the part of the harness that caused the failure.

### Agent Tooling And Surface Selection

#### CLI Versus MCP For Agent Tool Use

> I would not frame it as "CLI always beats MCP." I would frame it as agent ergonomics.
>
> The same model can perform very differently depending on the interface it has to the outside world. If a tool gives the agent a huge list of possible actions, verbose schemas, and large JSON responses, the model spends more tokens and turns just figuring out what happened.
>
> If the tool gives compact, task-shaped output, the model can make the next decision faster.
>
> A simple Notion example makes this clear. Imagine I ask an agent: "Find my Notion notes about Acme and summarize the three most relevant ones."
>
> First, the CLI path still needs authentication and permissions. It is not magic access. The Notion CLI has to be logged in with `ntn login`, or it needs a token such as `NOTION_API_TOKEN`. The Notion connection still needs access to the pages or databases being searched.
>
> The difference is that once the CLI is authenticated, the agent can use terminal commands to call the Notion API and filter the result locally.
>
> ```bash
> ntn login
> ntn api v1/search -X POST query=Acme page_size:=3 \
>   | jq '.results[] | {id, url, last_edited_time}'
> ```
>
> `jq` is just a command-line JSON filter. It is like `grep` for structured JSON. If Notion returns a large JSON response, `jq` can extract only the fields the model needs, such as page ID, URL, and last edited time. That means the model reads a small answer-shaped packet instead of a giant raw API response.
>
> Then the agent can fetch only the page it actually needs:
>
> ```bash
> ntn api v1/blocks/$PAGE_ID/children
> ```
>
> So the CLI path is still an API call. The advantage is that the agent is using the terminal as a workbench: authenticate once, call the API, filter with `jq`, compose commands, and bring only the useful evidence back into context.
>
> In an MCP workflow, the shape is different. The model usually does not run shell commands directly. A typical flow looks like this:
>
> 1. The user connects Notion MCP to an AI tool through OAuth.
> 2. The AI tool receives an approved Notion tool surface from the MCP server.
> 3. The user asks: "Find my Notion notes about Acme."
> 4. The model chooses a Notion MCP tool instead of writing a shell command.
> 5. The AI host sends a tool call to the Notion MCP server.
> 6. The Notion MCP server talks to Notion using the approved OAuth permissions.
> 7. The MCP server returns structured results to the model.
> 8. The model decides whether to call another tool, fetch a page, or answer.
>
> The model's tool call might look like this:
>
> ```json
> {
>   "tool": "notion_search",
>   "arguments": {
>     "query": "Acme"
>   }
> }
> ```
>
> Then it may ask for a second MCP tool call to fetch one result:
>
> ```json
> {
>   "tool": "notion_fetch_page",
>   "arguments": {
>     "page_id": "abc123"
>   }
> }
> ```
>
> That MCP path is often better for a shared enterprise workflow because OAuth, permissions, approved tool boundaries, auditability, and safe workspace access matter. Notion's own MCP docs frame Notion MCP as a hosted server that gives AI tools secure access to a workspace through OAuth and agent-optimized formatting.
>
> So the commercial answer is: for local engineering or one-off analysis, CLI/API access can be cheaper and faster because the agent can compose commands and aggressively filter output. For enterprise integrations, MCP is often the better substrate because governance matters more than raw token efficiency.
>
> The real lesson is model plus harness: context, tools, permissions, evals, traces, review, and compact tool outputs all shape whether the agent is useful.

#### OpenAI Voice Models — Sales Interview FAQ

**What is the simplest way to understand the voice stack?**

> The simplest way I think about it is that a production voice agent needs four things.
>
> First, a channel such as a phone call or an application. Second, a voice model that can understand the customer and respond naturally. Third, tools and orchestration that let it retrieve information, take action and hand work to a human. Fourth, evaluations that prove the agent completed the task correctly and safely.
>
> Older or more modular systems separate speech-to-text, reasoning and text-to-speech. A native realtime model can combine those into a more direct `audio in → model → audio out` experience, which can improve latency, turn-taking and interruption handling.
>
> But the model is still only one part of the production system. The customer still needs identity, permissions, business systems, approval rules, escalation and monitoring.

**What is GPT-Realtime-2.1?**

> GPT-Realtime-2.1 is OpenAI's developer-facing, low-latency speech-to-speech reasoning model for building live voice agents.
>
> It can understand spoken input, respond in audio, follow instructions, reason through more complex requests and call tools during the conversation.
>
> The improvements I would care about commercially are better interruption handling, better treatment of silence and noisy audio, and better capture of exact details such as names, account numbers or claim references. Those sound like small technical improvements, but they directly affect whether a customer trusts the agent to complete a real workflow.

**What does configurable reasoning mean?**

> It means the customer can balance responsiveness against task complexity.
>
> A simple order lookup should respond quickly and does not need deep reasoning. Troubleshooting, policy interpretation or deciding whether to escalate may justify more reasoning, even if that introduces some additional latency and usage.
>
> The commercial point is that the right configuration depends on the workflow, the cost of a wrong answer and how long the customer is willing to wait. I would use the lowest level of reasoning that reliably completes the task, then validate it using real calls.

**What is VAD, and why does it matter?**

> VAD is voice activity detection. It helps the system decide whether the customer has started or finished speaking.
>
> Traditional VAD mainly looks at sound and silence. Semantic VAD also considers whether the person's thought sounds complete. For example, if someone says, "I think we should..." and pauses, semantic VAD is more likely to wait instead of interrupting.
>
> For a seller, the important point is not the technical settings. It is that turn detection materially affects whether the agent feels patient and natural or constantly talks over the customer.

**What can the voice agent actually do?**

> Realtime 2.1 can use functions, MCP servers and connectors to interact with external systems.
>
> That means the opportunity is larger than replacing an IVR or answering FAQs. A voice agent could retrieve an order, check an appointment, inspect an account, update a support case or initiate a workflow.
>
> But the customer must still define what the agent can access, which actions are safe, what requires confirmation and when the interaction should transfer to a human.
>
> For read-only information, the agent may be able to act directly. For payments, cancellations, account changes or regulated decisions, I would expect stronger confirmation and human-review boundaries.

**When would a customer use a modular voice stack instead?**

> I would start with the customer experience and the operating requirements rather than assuming native realtime is always better.
>
> Native Realtime is the stronger fit when the experience depends on natural live conversation: low perceived latency, fluid turn-taking, interruption handling and the ability to respond to how something was said, not only the transcript. That could matter in customer support, appointment booking, concierge services, sales qualification or field-service assistance.
>
> A modular `speech-to-text → model → text-to-speech` stack can be the better fit when the customer needs tighter control over each component. For example, the transcript may be a regulated system of record; the company may need a particular speech-recognition provider for a language, accent or deployment region; it may want to route simple and complex requests to different models; or it may need independent vendor fallback, auditability and cost controls.
>
> Modular can also make sense when the workflow is less conversational. If the primary job is transcribing a call, summarizing a recording, extracting structured information or generating a spoken response from completed text, the customer may not need a persistent realtime session at all.
>
> The trade-off is that every additional handoff can add latency and strip out information such as tone, hesitation or emotion. The benefit is greater component-level control, clearer transcripts and the ability to change or optimize each layer independently.
>
> In practice, many enterprise architectures will be hybrid. A customer might use a realtime model for the live conversation, preserve a separate transcript for audit and analytics, call a different model for a specialised background task, and route low-confidence or high-risk cases to a person.
>
> So I would qualify the decision with a few questions: How natural does the interaction need to feel? Is the transcript itself a critical record? What languages, regions or compliance requirements matter? Does the customer need independent model or vendor choice? What latency is acceptable? Where must the system fall back or hand off?
>
> The right answer is not the architecture with the most advanced model. It is the architecture that meets the customer's experience, control, risk and economic requirements.

**How should an enterprise buyer evaluate it?**

> I would start with the workflow rather than the model.
>
> What job should the voice agent complete? Which systems must it access? Which actions carry risk? Where should it ask for confirmation or transfer to a person? What would successful completion look like?
>
> Then I would test it on the customer's actual conditions: real accents, background noise, interruptions, exact identifiers, tool failures and escalation scenarios.
>
> A natural voice is not enough. The agent has to complete the task, update the correct system, fail safely and improve the customer or operator outcome.

**What should we measure?**

> I would measure task completion, correct tool use, exact capture of important details, escalation quality, response latency, interruption handling, customer satisfaction and cost per successfully completed task.
>
> Cost per minute can be informative, but cost per completed outcome is the more meaningful business metric.

**One-line interview answer:**

> GPT-Realtime-2.1 gives developers a low-latency voice reasoning model that can understand speech, respond naturally and use tools; the enterprise opportunity is turning that capability into a governed workflow with clear permissions, human handoffs and measurable task completion.

Sources and freshness boundary: [GPT-Realtime-2.1 model page](https://developers.openai.com/api/docs/models/gpt-realtime-2.1), [Realtime and audio guide](https://developers.openai.com/api/docs/guides/realtime), [Realtime prompting guide](https://developers.openai.com/api/docs/guides/realtime-models-prompting) and [Voice activity detection guide](https://developers.openai.com/api/docs/guides/realtime-vad). Recheck exact model availability, pricing and technical limits before using them in a customer-specific recommendation.



## Prospecting, APAC, And Account Strategy

Use this section as a **question cluster**, not one giant answer. The default question it answers is:

> How would you build a strategic prospecting motion for OpenAI in APAC?

If the interviewer simply asks "How do you prospect?", use only the core answer. Pull the follow-up modules only if they ask about account prioritization, messageability, stakeholder mapping, proof, target accounts, or campaigns.

### Account Selection And Prospecting Motion

#### What Makes A Company A Fit For OpenAI?

> The sharper way I think about fit is that companies are not the unit of qualification. Workflows are. A bank, retailer, or airline can be a great logo but still be a weak opportunity if the workflow is vague, low-value, or impossible to move.
>
> So I would qualify fit at the workflow level: is the problem valuable, is OpenAI specifically relevant, can the company actually act, is there a reason now, and do we have a path to the people who can move it forward?
>
> The shorthand is value, readiness, and timing. But the more useful version is: workflow value, OpenAI fit, ability to act, reason now, and path to power.
>
> Workflow value means there is a meaningful business problem: knowledge work, customer volume, developer pressure, research or reporting load, compliance operations, support burden, revenue impact, or expensive manual coordination.
>
> OpenAI fit means I can place the workflow on the right OpenAI rung, not just say "AI would help." If the problem is broad employee productivity or executive adoption, the likely surface is ChatGPT Enterprise or Business as a governed work surface.
>
> If it is engineering velocity, codebase work, internal tooling, or developer workflows, the likely surface is Codex. If it is a repeatable team SOP that needs judgment, sources, tools, approvals, Slack, schedules, or API triggers, the likely surface may be Workspace Agents.
>
> If the customer wants to embed AI into its own product or internal systems, the likely surface is the API, tools, agents, retrieval, and SDK layer. If it is a large, regulated, multi-system transformation, the conversation may belong in the Frontier, deployment, or FDE-style lane, subject to account-team confirmation.
>
> Ability to act means there is enough ownership and infrastructure to move: technical champions, data sources, governance owners, security/procurement engagement, executive priority, and a measurable outcome.
>
> Reason now is the compelling-event layer. "AI is important" is not a compelling event. A better reason might be a new product launch, cost pressure, compliance change, support-volume spike, developer backlog, expansion market, competitive pressure, or executive mandate.
>
> Path to power means there is a credible route to the people who can sponsor, validate, block, or advance the opportunity. If there is no workflow owner, no stakeholder path, and no proof criteria, it may be interesting but it is not ready for AD time.
>
> If the account already shows demand through usage, inbound interest, hiring, public AI goals, events, or pilots, I would treat that as demand capture and qualify quickly.
>
> If the workflow value is high but ability to act, reason now, or path to power is weak, I would treat it as demand creation or nurture: educate, monitor, look for the right trigger, and avoid burning the account too early.

Fit table:

| Lens                | Strong fit                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                              | Weak fit                                                                                                             | BDR action                                                                                                            |
| ------------------- | ------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------- | -------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------- | --------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------- |
| Workflow value      | Important workflow with cost, risk, volume, revenue impact, customer experience impact, or strategic upside.                                                                                                                                                                                            | Generic "AI could help" idea with no clear business consequence.                                                     | Build an account thesis around the workflow and business metric.                                                      |
| OpenAI fit / ladder | Workflow maps to a clear OpenAI surface: ChatGPT Enterprise or Business for governed employee work, Codex for engineering/developer workflows, Workspace Agents for shared repeatable SOPs, API/tools/agents for embedded builds, or Frontier/deployment for strategic production-agent transformation. | "AI could help" but no clear OpenAI surface, or a small deterministic workflow better solved by existing automation. | Identify the ladder rung before positioning the opportunity; do not force every workflow into the same product story. |
| Ability to act      | Technical champion, data source, governance owner, security/procurement path, executive priority, measurable outcome.                                                                                                                                                                                   | Curiosity without owner, data access, process, or evaluation path.                                                   | Map stakeholders and test whether the account can move beyond a pilot.                                                |
| Reason now          | Compelling event such as cost pressure, support spike, product launch, compliance change, developer backlog, expansion, or executive mandate.                                                                                                                                                           | "AI is important someday."                                                                                           | Decide whether this is active demand or a nurture account.                                                            |
| Path to power       | Clear route to workflow owner, champion, technical validator, governance/security, executive sponsor, or AD-relevant next step.                                                                                                                                                                         | Only a curious user, generic inbox, or vague AI interest.                                                            | Hand off only when the AD would get a sharper opportunity than a raw lead.                                            |

OpenAI fit ladder:

| Rung | Best fit | Qualification question |
|---|---|---|
| Work surface: ChatGPT Enterprise or Business | Broad knowledge work, executive adoption, research, analysis, drafting, synthesis, company context, and governed employee rollout. | Is this mainly about getting employees productive inside a trusted AI workspace? |
| Engineering work surface: Codex | Engineering productivity, codebase understanding, debugging, reviews, migrations, internal tools, and developer agent loops. | Is the buyer trying to improve software delivery or technical workflow throughput? |
| Shared workflow layer: Workspace Agents | Repeatable team SOPs with judgment, sources, tools, approvals, Slack, schedules, or API triggers. | Can this recurring workflow become a shared, governed agent instead of a personal prompt habit? |
| Builder layer: API / Responses / tools / Agents SDK | Embedded product features, custom internal systems, retrieval, tool calls, structured outputs, and bespoke agent workflows. | Does the customer need to build AI into a product or workflow they own? |
| Transformation lane: Frontier / deployment / FDE-style support | High-value, multi-system, regulated, cross-functional production-agent transformation. | Is this a core operating-model change that needs technical deployment help, governance, evals, and change management? |

#### If Asked: Role Of AI In SDR Prospecting

Question it answers: "How do you think AI changes the SDR role in prospecting?"

Position:

> I would frame this specifically around cold account prospecting. In a warm or inbound motion, the account has already raised its hand in some way: a demo request, product usage, a pricing-page visit, or an obvious intent signal. Cold prospecting is harder.
>
> You are starting from a company that has not asked to speak to you, so the job is to decide whether there is a credible, respectful reason to interrupt them.
>
> The clearest mental model for me is intelligence versus judgment. I first picked this up from Sequoia's "Services: The New Software" framing: today's judgment can become tomorrow's intelligence as AI systems learn from domain-specific examples of good judgment. In cold prospecting, that distinction is very practical.
>
> The cleaner way to say it is: the email draft is the downstream, more automatable part once the prospecting judgment has already been made.
>
> AI can read the account context, summarize what changed, map the signal to a possible business pain, find the relevant proof point, adapt the language to a persona, and generate a few tight versions.
>
> That is information synthesis and language generation, so AI can help a lot.
>
> The harder judgment is whether the email deserves to exist in the first place. Is this account actually worth interrupting? Is the signal real or just generic? Is it a crowded trigger everyone will use? Does this person own the problem? Is now the right time?
>
> Is the angle specific enough that the buyer might feel understood instead of prospected?
>
> That is the part I would not want to hand fully to automation.
>
> Intelligence is where AI is already very strong.
>
> It can build and clean the named-account universe, enrich companies and contacts, read websites, job postings, news, leadership quotes, product pages, case studies, technographics, funding announcements, and competitor signals, then turn that unstructured research into an account hypothesis.
>
> The tools connect it to the real world: CRM, enrichment sources, company websites, job boards, LinkedIn-style public data, review sites, sequencers, data warehouses, and internal knowledge.
>
> For me, the biggest unlock is source-of-truth context. For cold accounts, the useful context sits in two places.
>
> First, there is external evidence about the account: what they are hiring for, what they launched, what markets they are entering, what their website says, what their execs are talking about, what tools they appear to use, and what problems show up in public language.
>
> Second, there is internal pattern memory, which mostly lives in call transcripts, Salesforce, Slack discussions, old deal notes, and closed-won or closed-lost reviews: which segments we win in, which triggers actually convert, which objections show up, which proof points land, which titles care,
>
> and which lookalike companies resemble accounts where the motion has already worked.
>
> If an SDR has to manually hunt across all of that for every cold account, prospecting becomes slow and shallow.
>
> If an agent can query the right systems, pull evidence into a clean account brief, explain why the signal matters, distinguish generic fit from a real timing trigger, suggest the buyer map and outreach angle, and package the AD handoff, the SDR can spend more time on the part AI should not fully own: taste, timing,
>
> trust, and commercial judgment.
>
> The idea for handling intelligence with AI is to minimize subjectivity in the repeatable parts of cold prospecting. Make the inputs clear, connect the right tools, define the output schema, cite the evidence, and reduce the manual hunting a seller has to do before they can form a point of view.
>
> The output should not just be "write an email." It should be: why this cold account, why now, why this buyer, why this problem, what evidence supports the angle, what proof point to use, and what next action makes sense.
>
> Judgment is different. I would start by doing that part manually myself, because it forces me to understand how I judge: what I consider good or bad, what edge cases matter, and what the tells are of a result that looks good from far away but is not actually good.
>
> In sales, that could be deciding whether a signal is truly messageable, whether a champion has real influence, whether an account is ready for a pilot, or whether a technically impressive use case is commercially urgent enough.
>
> Once that judgment is explicit, I would start transferring it into an agent skill piece by piece: ICP rules, examples of good and bad cold signals, messageability criteria, buyer-mapping rules, crowded-signal warnings, edge cases, tone standards, routing logic, and what the agent should do when confidence is low.
>
> That takes a lot of iteration.
>
> Sometimes you patch one edge case and create another one. Sometimes the skill becomes a game of whack-a-mole and you have to simplify or rebuild it from scratch.
>
> So I would not aim to automate 100 percent of meaningful prospecting that requires both intelligence and judgment.
>
> The better target is: automate as much of the account research and signal-synthesis work as possible, then gradually iterate on the repeatable qualification and use-case judgment: what makes an account worth attention, what makes a signal messageable, what use case is plausible,
>
> and what proof point would make the outreach credible.
>
> The final judgment still belongs with a human who has taste and context. That last mile can be the most expensive part, and in prospecting it is often where the difference between spam and relevance lives.
>
> That is how I would describe my own angle. I use agent workflows as leverage to make prospecting sharper: better account selection, cleaner signal interpretation, stronger buyer mapping, more specific outreach angles, cleaner CRM context, and better AD handoff.
>
> The operating test is simple: does AI make the SDR more prepared, more specific, and more useful to the buyer, or does it just let them send more generic messages faster?

How I would break it down:

- **Cold account universe:** use AI to turn ICP, territory, firmographics, technographics, hiring, website language, funding, expansion, and competitor signals into a sharper named-account list.
- **Signal interpretation:** do not just say "they raised funding" or "they are hiring." Ask what changed, what workflow it might affect, who owns it, whether the signal is crowded, and whether it is actually messageable.
- **Offer-specific timing:** distinguish "this company fits our ICP" from "there is a specific reason to reach out now." Fit is useful for monitoring; timing is what earns the interruption.
- **Buyer mapping:** identify the likely business owner, technical influencer, economic buyer, and operational user, then make clear what each person would need to believe.
- **Account research:** compress scattered public context and internal pattern memory into a source-backed brief: company priority, likely workflow, stakeholder map, risk, proof point, and next best angle.
- **Outreach angle:** do the first version manually. For a cold account, the message strategy has to be found by a human first: the insight, tone, timing, and reason the message deserves to be sent.
  Once that judgment is explicit and the angle has shown signs of working, AI can help scale the variable parts from evidence: account context, proof points, role-specific wording, and follow-up variations.
- **Prospecting queue:** turn signals into a prioritized action list: contact now, monitor, enrich further, route to AD, or disqualify.
- **CRM and AD handoff:** capture why the account is worth attention, what evidence supports the angle, who to contact, what was sent, what happened, and what the AD should know next.
- **Learning loop:** feed replies, meetings booked, no-shows, objections, closed-won patterns, and closed-lost patterns back into the signal and account-selection logic.

Guardrail:

> The test is whether AI increases judgment density, not message volume. Bad AI SDR systems make it easier to send generic outreach faster. Good AI SDR systems make the rep better prepared, more specific, more useful, and more disciplined about what deserves a buyer's attention.

#### If Asked: Sales Ops / RevOps Skills I Would Build

Question it answers: "What practical AI workflows would you build for a sales team?"

> I would be super inspired by OpenAI's own sales plugin skills, which I have used and found quite strong once you tailor them to the specific use case.

> I would think about sales ops as the layer that keeps the sales system honest: clean CRM data, clear next steps, strong rep preparation, and clean handoffs. I would not start with a giant autonomous seller.
>
> I would build small, reviewable skills around the places sales teams leak time and data quality: calls, emails, CRM fields, decks, follow-ups, routing, forecasts, proposals, and handoffs.
>
> The common pattern is simple: connect to the source systems, produce a structured output, show the evidence, and only write back after sandbox testing or human approval. That keeps the CRM hygienic without removing the commercial judgment from the rep.

Examples I would build:

1. **Post-call scorecard and CRM update:** Use the call transcript, email thread, and CRM record to score whether the call achieved its intended outcome, then propose updates to stage, next step, follow-up date, stakeholders, pain, objections, proof gaps, and deal risk.
I would run it as a dry-run patch first, standardize the fields, and require human approval before high-impact writebacks.
2. **Daily rep inbox and sales brief:** Use read-only CRM, calendar, email, and call-transcript context to tell each rep what to work today: which accounts need follow-up, what next step was promised, what deal is stale, what call needs prep, what coaching theme keeps appearing,
and what account has a new signal worth acting on.
3. **Pre-call prep coach:** Before a meeting, pull the account history, prior transcripts, open tasks, stakeholders, recent signals, likely objections, proof points, and discovery gaps. Output the meeting objective, questions to ask, stakeholder-specific angle, and what a good next step would look like.
4. **Deal and account health scorer:** Score opportunities and customers across qualification coverage, multithreading, compelling event, next-step hygiene, adoption, value realized, sentiment, renewal risk, and expansion potential.
The output should be evidence-bound: what will kill the deal or account, why, and what action would improve the odds.
5. **Multi-thread detector and stakeholder map updater:** Detect single-threaded deals, missing economic buyers, weak champions, blockers, technical evaluators, legal/security/procurement owners, and stale stakeholder data.
Refresh the org map from CRM, call mentions, email threads, LinkedIn or public sources, and seller corrections.
6. **AE or AD handoff packet:** Turn SDR discovery into a clean opportunity packet: account thesis, use case, stakeholder map, champion quality, objections, urgency, proof needed, source links, and recommended next step.
This is especially useful for a Strategic BDR motion because the handoff quality determines whether the opportunity is real.
7. **Deck, proposal, and business-case builder:** Connect to the preferred deck tool, approved templates, brand rules, pricing/SOW guidance, product proof points, account research, and call transcripts.
Then generate a review-ready deck, proposal, SOW, or business case that uses third-party research and first-party discovery instead of starting from a blank page.
8. **Follow-up and recap generator:** After a call, draft the recap email, action items, open questions, mutual next steps, stakeholder-specific follow-ups, and CRM notes from the transcript and email thread. The rep still edits the tone and decides what deserves to be sent.
9. **Inbound qualifier, lead routing, and SLA monitor:** For inbound or product-led signals, enrich the account, check ICP, territory, ownership, source, and urgency, route it to the right rep, create the CRM task, and monitor whether the SLA is met. If nobody follows up, the system should surface it.
10. **Revenue sentry and pipeline hygiene auditor:** Scan CRM for opportunities with no next step, slipped close dates, no champion, missing economic buyer, weak business case, inactive stakeholders, unresolved security/procurement questions, stale follow-up, or forecast risk.
Output the risk, evidence, and recommended recovery action.
11. **Objection and content-gap loop:** Mine transcripts for repeated objections, competitor mentions, missing proof, confusing product explanations, and buyer questions the team cannot answer well. Feed that back into enablement, deck content, battlecards, FAQ material, and seller coaching.
12. **Closed-won and closed-lost learning loop:** Join transcripts, CRM outcome, objections, competitor mentions, pricing issues, security blockers, and next-step history to update playbooks. The goal is to make future qualification, objection handling, routing, and discovery better, not just summarize the past.

Guardrails I would keep:

- **Read-only first, writeback later:** start by drafting updates and recommendations before letting the agent write into CRM or send messages.
- **Structured outputs:** use consistent fields for stage, next step, date, stakeholder role, risk, source, confidence, and owner.
- **Evidence links:** every CRM update or recommendation should point back to the transcript, email, CRM record, or source that supports it.
- **Human approval for judgment calls:** stage changes, commercial commitments, pricing, concessions, external emails, and high-risk fields should stay human-reviewed.
- **Measure the workflow:** track CRM completeness, follow-up SLA, prep time, handoff quality, forecast hygiene, ramp time, and whether opportunities actually move.

#### If Asked: Creating A Prospecting Playbook For OpenAI

Question it answers: "How would you approach creating a prospecting playbook for OpenAI?" Also covers: "Walk me through your prospecting motion."

Short answer:

> My prospecting motion would start with account strategy, not message volume. The way I think about it is: TAM, thesis, signal, stakeholder, message, learn.
>
> Begin with the full account universe, turn it into a named target list, tier accounts by fit and readiness, collect first-party, second-party, and third-party signals, test messageability, map the buying committee, and write outreach around a specific workflow hypothesis.
>
> For OpenAI, I would not begin with "which companies are interested in AI?" That is too broad. I would first align with the AD on the account universe: financial services, life sciences, retail, regional platforms, regulated enterprises, developer-heavy companies, or regional HQs.
>
> Then I would pick a narrow workflow wedge, learn the market manually, separate baseline ICP from best-case timing signals, tier the accounts, test the message by hand, map the buyer committee, and build the operating cadence with the AD.
>
> The important part is that I would not qualify an enterprise AI opportunity by asking whether a company is "interested in AI." I would ask where the use case sits in the business, who owns the workflow, what system is the source of truth, what risk would block adoption,
>
> and what proof would make the buyer comfortable moving from curiosity to a real pilot.
>
> Then the outreach is problem-first. It should sound like: I saw this signal, I think it may connect to this workflow or priority, and I am curious whether this is a live problem for your team. That keeps the first message useful instead of turning it into a generic OpenAI pitch.
>
> The important thing is that a playbook is not just sequences. It is a living system: account rules, signals, proof points, stakeholder maps, messaging angles, discovery questions, CRM notes, AD handoff standards, and a feedback loop from every reply, meeting, objection, and closed-lost.
>
> After each touch or meeting, I would update the account thesis and hand the AD the real state: why this account, why now, who matters, what proof is missing, and what next step is credible.

Airwallex example and playbook:

| Principle                                           | Airwallex detail                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                   | Playbook lesson                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                             |
| --------------------------------------------------- | ---------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------- | --------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------- |
| **Diagnosis before tactics**                        | I was the second outbound hire in Singapore. When I joined, outbound was still a small part of the revenue mix. A lot of revenue came from paid marketing and channel partnerships, but some of those deals were lower quality: smaller brands, weaker retention, or compliance risk that could create offboarding exposure. My job was to help outbound bring in stronger, more recognizable Singapore logos, but there was not yet a neat territory playbook.                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                    | The first problem was not "send more emails." It was diagnosing where outbound could improve revenue quality: stronger logos, better fit, clearer retention, and lower compliance risk. I needed to understand the market before building the cadence.                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                      |
| **TAM segmentation before ICP**                     | The market I chose was travel, but even that had subsegments. There were inbound travel agencies and destination management companies, corporate travel agencies, and outbound tour operators. After a few initial discovery calls, I quickly realized that only the outbound tour operators were really valuable for Airwallex at that point, because the other subsegments often needed credit-facility functionality that we did not yet have. Outbound operators had a cleaner fit: they needed to send money overseas to guides, hotels, operators, and partners, often under time pressure. The best prospects had a specific shape: outbound tours, frequent overseas payouts, payments to many guides, tight tour timelines, seasonal June/December transaction spikes, margin pressure from bank fees, manual reconciliation pain, and harder corridors like China, Russia, or Central Asia. A Europe-focused tour agency had a different payment profile from a China-focused one, so the destination mix mattered too. This required real ICP discipline: a good playbook should say who not to pursue, not just who to pursue. Inbound travel, DMCs, and corporate travel agencies were not necessarily bad businesses, but they were weaker fits for Airwallex at that product stage.                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                 | A TAM is not the same as an ICP. "Travel" was sharper than "all Singapore businesses," but the actual ICP was narrower: outbound travel operators with overseas payout pain. A good playbook should quickly separate subsegments that are actually winnable from the ones that only look attractive on paper, then separate baseline ICP fit from timing pain, urgency, operational friction, and evidence that the problem is live. Anti-ICP is part of the playbook too: if a subsegment needs capabilities the product does not yet have, it should be deprioritized, nurtured, or handled differently instead of consuming the same sales effort as the best-fit wedge. |
| **Market map before account list**                  | I did a few cold calls and sent a bunch of cold emails, but the market was difficult to penetrate. Travel agencies were historically traditional and trusted banks as the main provider. They viewed fintechs as unknown, and they were understandably worried about legitimacy because they moved millions in payments every year. So I decided the best strategy was face-to-face. Looking at Google Maps, I quickly learned that roughly 90% of the travel agencies I cared about were clustered near People's Park, so I did old-fashioned door knocking. I walked in and asked to see the finance manager, not to pitch, but to learn about the challenges they faced and put a face to the name. The market stopped being an abstract spreadsheet and became a physical map with real people and workflows that needed solving.                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                              | A good prospecting motion starts with a market map, not just a list. When trust is the blocker, channel choice matters. In this case, face-to-face learning gave me faster buyer truth than generic cold outreach because it let me see the offices, meet the people, hear the real objections, and understand why banks still felt safer.                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                  |
| **Finite-TAM trust**                                | The travel market was narrow. If I sent lazy messages or walked into offices without context, that reputation would travel. In a finite market, every bad touch burns territory.                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                   | Narrow wedges compound only if trust is protected. I would rather make fewer touches with better context than spray the market and teach buyers to ignore me.                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                               |
| **Tiering**                                         | Very quickly, I got a sense of who the top accounts were, including names like EU Holidays and Chan Brothers, by talking to people in the market. I also learned who looked good on paper but seemed to be declining. Sometimes it was obvious from the storefront, the size and energy of the office, or by scrolling their Instagram and seeing who had active campaigns, big layouts, and real marketing momentum. It was hard to get an audience with the bigger companies at first, so I decided I had to build a reputation as the person who understood travel better than anyone else. I worked a few smaller accounts first, closed them, and got real proof points that Airwallex was legitimate, our service was better, and I had found message-market fit. At the same time, I kept researching how the most credible travel brands operated.
