                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                  | Tiering is not just company size. It is credibility, momentum, accessibility, timing, and proof strategy. Sometimes you earn your way into the top accounts by winning smaller but representative accounts first, learning the workflow properly, and creating proof that feels close enough for the larger accounts to trust.                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                              |
| **Right signal, right timing**                      | The main signal was not just "this is a travel agency." It was catching them at the right moment with the right context. I timed outreach for right after the travel season, when I knew they had some downtime and were not buried in peak operations. I also paid attention at travel fairs to understand what destinations and tour products they were pushing, because a Europe-focused agency and a China-focused agency had very different payment needs.                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                    | A signal has to become messageable. It needs to connect to timing, workflow, buyer pressure, and a respectful reason to talk now. The stronger message was not "you are in travel"; it was "I understand the payment and operations pressure created by the specific tours you are selling right now."                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                      |
| **Proof proximity**                                 | Early wins made later conversations easier because the proof was close: same industry, same geography, same payment workflow, same seasonal pressure, same guide-payout pain. A travel agency did not need a generic fintech case study; they needed to know whether this worked for someone like them.                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                            | Proof gets stronger when it feels close. Same workflow, same region, same buyer pressure, and same risk model reduce the buyer's perceived leap of faith.                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                   |
| **Buyer committee**                                 | Even in travel, the buyer was not just "the owner." It was critical to get buy-in from all the stakeholders. Finance worried about fees, transfer reliability, legitimacy, documentation, and whether money would move safely through sensitive corridors. Operations cared about guide payouts, peak-season issues, and reconciliation. They were often doing reconciliation manually because the bank portal did not have a clean export function, so they were logging into bank portals, copying and pasting into Excel, and introducing errors. Commercial cared about whether finance could support expansion into new tours in more exotic countries. Frontline teams felt the stress when a payment was stuck and customers or guides were already in-country. When I pitched the CEO, I had to unify those concerns into one Airwallex platform story.                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                    | Stakeholder mapping matters even in a midmarket motion. The seller has to understand who feels the pain, who owns the process, who worries about risk, who wants growth, who signs, and who will be blamed if the workflow breaks. The CEO story only works when it integrates the finance, operations, commercial, and frontline concerns into one business case.                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                          |
| **Talk tracks by stakeholder / message market fit** | The message worked because it used the customer's own workflow language. I was not pitching "better fintech infrastructure" in the abstract.<br><br>**Finance: payment reliability, fees, and corridor risk.** Travel agencies had very specific cross-border payment issues. They often had to pay local tour guides or tour leaders overseas on short timelines, because the gap between a booked tour and the actual tour date could be very tight. If the guide did not receive money on time and the group was already in-country, the stress was immediate.<br><br>A huge share of transactions clustered around June and December, so they cared not only about price, but whether their provider would be responsive and alert during peak-volume windows when payments, checks, or operational issues could not wait.<br><br>One more layer was corridor complexity. Because these agencies were dealing with tours, some destinations could be more exotic or compliance-sensitive. They would sometimes run into issues sending money into China, Russia, or Central Asia. China was a strong example for us because Airwallex's China payment rails were well integrated and we were licensed there. For the more sensitive corridors, I would check internally with compliance and get the customer a practical checklist of documents to have ready: source of funds, reason for transfer, invoices, and other supporting documents. Then, when those payments came through, I would flag them internally so they could be reviewed quickly during peak operating windows.<br><br>**Operations: reconciliation and error reduction.** I could talk about the manual reconciliation pain: logging into bank portals, downloading or copying payment data, pasting it into Excel, and cleaning errors because the bank did not have a useful export flow. I could show how Airwallex exports could be customized into a CSV format they could actually use without copy-paste, and where they used NetSuite, how the two-way ERP integration could eliminate manual work and reduce errors.<br><br>**Commercial: new tour revenue.** The economics were also specific. A single 10 day trip could involve payments to up to 20 providers on the ground, and each bank transfer could carry 50$ fees. By cutting it to 0, we could increase their margins significantly. I could also connect the finance workflow back to growth: if finance could support more corridors reliably, commercial could sell more ambitious tours into exotic countries instead of avoiding them because payments were painful or risky.                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                  | Message-market fit comes before scale. The buyer should feel that I understand their day, not just my product. The message becomes credible when it names the workflow, the moment of stress, and the stakeholder-specific value: finance gets reliability and cost control, operations gets cleaner reconciliation, and commercial gets destination expansion.                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                             |
| **Gap selling to CEO**                              | When I pitched the CEO, I tied the stakeholder concerns together using a gap-selling frame.<br><br>**Current state:** The bank was trusted, but the operating model was painful: expensive overseas transfers, manual reconciliation, payment uncertainty during peak travel windows, and extra friction whenever tours involved more sensitive corridors.<br><br>**Desired state:** A finance and payments workflow that could support outbound tours reliably: lower transfer cost, cleaner reconciliation, faster operational support, better corridor readiness, and fewer manual errors.<br><br>**Measurable shortfall:** A single 10 day trip could involve payments to up to 20 providers on the ground, and each bank transfer could carry 50$ fees. On top of that, operations was spending time copying data out of bank portals into Excel, and errors or payment delays could create stress when guides or customers were already in-country. I would usually send a simple two-slide champion package after discovery that turned this into a business case the finance or operations lead could sell internally:<br><br>1. **Dollar savings:** benchmark a high-volume peak-season month, count the number of overseas transfers, multiply by the typical bank transfer fee, and compare that against the Airwallex cost structure. This made the savings concrete instead of theoretical.<br><br>2. **Time savings:** count how many transfers needed reconciliation, how many people touched the process, and how much time they spent exporting, copying, pasting, checking, and cleaning data from bank portals into Excel. Then show how cleaner CSV exports or NetSuite integration could remove that manual loop.<br><br>3. **New opportunities:** map the payment rails Airwallex had, especially stronger China rails and support for more complex corridors, against the commercial team's tour roadmap. If they wanted to launch or scale tours into certain destinations but finance was nervous about payment reliability, documentation, or review timelines, that became revenue upside rather than just back-office efficiency.<br><br>The point was to give the champion a package they could take internally: short memo, ROI logic, operational implementation outline, expected finance/ops objections, compliance document checklist, and a next-step email.<br><br>**Root cause:** The bank workflow was trusted, but it was not built for the way outbound tour operators actually worked: many small overseas payments, seasonal spikes, corridor-specific documentation, and reconciliation needs across finance and operations.<br><br>**Cost of doing nothing:** Margin leakage from fees, manual work, higher error risk, slower expansion into exotic tour destinations, and more peak-season operational stress.<br><br>**Bridge:** Airwallex became the bridge from current state to desired state: lower fees, stronger China rails, corridor document prep, faster internal review when needed, customizable CSV exports, NetSuite integration where relevant, and better support during peak windows. That made the switch from a known and trusted bank feel less like fintech risk and more like an operational growth decision. | Gap selling is the CEO version of message-market fit. Do not stop at "they have pain." Define the current state, desired state, measurable shortfall, root cause, cost of the status quo, and consequence if nothing changes. Then position the product as the bridge. The CEO does not need every feature; they need to see why the current path is expensive, risky, or hard to defend. For strong accounts, the BDR/AD motion should also build the champion package: short memo, ROI logic, implementation outline, security or procurement questions, and the next-step email the champion can use internally.                                                         |
| **Main friction / what made it hard**               | The hardest part was not proving that Airwallex had better pricing or faster rails. The real friction was trust and switching cost. These agencies had moved millions through banks for years, so even if the bank workflow was painful, it was familiar. Airwallex was a fintech, which made people ask: is this legitimate, will payments arrive, who helps us if something breaks during peak season, and what happens if finance switches the workflow and something goes wrong?<br><br>I overcame that by making the risk feel smaller in stages. First, I went face-to-face instead of relying only on cold email, so they could put a person to the company. Then I built proof through smaller accounts and workflow-specific examples: overseas guide payouts, China rails, sensitive-corridor document checklists, reconciliation exports, and peak-season support. For the stronger accounts, I sent a simple ROI / champion package showing dollar savings, time savings, and new tour opportunities. And once they were ready to try, I made the first steps feel safe: RBAC, maker-checker controls, permissions, platform walkthroughs, and WhatsApp-level support when issues came up.                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                             | In a trust-heavy sale, the buyer is not only evaluating product superiority. They are evaluating the risk of changing behavior. The job is to reduce perceived risk step by step: show up in person, speak the buyer's workflow language, quantify the cost of staying the same, prove the wedge in a low-risk way, and make onboarding feel supported enough that the switch becomes an operational improvement rather than a leap of faith.                                                                                                                                                                                                                               |
| **Fast follow-up loop and onboarding**              | After a warm interaction, event chat, intro, reply, or call, I tried to move quickly because momentum decays fast, especially when the buyer is busy and the product feels new. For the travel agencies that were serious, I would go to their office and help them set everything up in about an hour, answer questions on the spot, and make the first steps feel easy. The setup usually meant getting the role-based access controls right: who could create payments, who could approve them, who needed view-only access, and how to preserve the maker-checker flow finance teams were used to. I would also walk them through the platform very practically: what to click, how to initiate a transfer, where to upload supporting documents, how to export reports, and who should do what internally. After that, I stayed one WhatsApp message away. If they had an issue, they could send me a screenshot and I would label it with the next steps. It was basically free value-added service, but it also gave them confidence that I was fully committed to implementing Airwallex according to their timeline.                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                      | Follow-up is part of the product experience. In a trust-sensitive market, speed and hands-on onboarding reduce perceived risk. The playbook should force fast follow-up after any meaningful interaction, clarify the next step, remove setup friction, get permissions and approval flows right, and show the buyer they will not be left alone after saying yes.                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                          |
| **Wedge product and land-and-expand**               | The product wedge was not "move everything to Airwallex on day one." That would have felt too risky because banks were familiar and trusted. The wedge was the highest-pain workflow: overseas payouts for outbound tours, especially when timing, fees, corridor complexity, and reconciliation were painful. Risk removal came through a small next step: instead of asking them to move everything at once, we would start with smaller transactions first, monitor them closely, and show that Airwallex delivered as sold. Once I could prove that Airwallex was legitimate, faster, easier to reconcile, and useful for the corridors they cared about, the conversation expanded into two natural adjacencies: issuing Airwallex employee cards to tour guides so they could reconcile expenses cleanly, and using Airwallex as the payment gateway for website checkout and offline PoS. In the end, the deal became a bundle that created stickiness and vendor lock-in.                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                  | The land-and-expand lesson is to start with the product wedge that maps to the buyer's most painful, provable workflow. The best first wedge is not the cleverest product surface; it is the workflow where the buyer can name the pain, approve a small pilot or first transaction, and recognize the before/after improvement quickly. Do not ask them to trust the whole platform before they trust the first use case. Win the narrow workflow, prove reliability in a controlled scope, reduce perceived risk, then expand into adjacent workflows that make the platform more embedded and harder to replace.                                                         |
| **AI increases judgment density**                   | If I were running the same motion today, I would use agents to research agencies, detect payout or expansion signals, build account briefs, identify likely buyer roles, summarize corridor risk, draft message angles, and keep CRM notes cleaner. But I would still own the judgment on which accounts deserved manual effort, what to say, whether to walk in, and when to pull in the right internal team.                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                     | AI should not just increase volume. It should increase judgment density: better account selection, sharper signals, stronger prep, cleaner handoffs, and less wasted buyer attention.                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                       |
| **Living feedback loop**                            | Every conversation updated the playbook. I learned new names, bigger players, real objections, seasonal details, corridor issues, compliance document requirements, support expectations, internal language, proof gaps, and next-best actions. The motion got better because the market kept teaching me.                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                         | A prospecting playbook is a living system. Every reply, meeting, objection, win, and loss should update the account rules, messaging, proof points, stakeholder map, discovery questions, and follow-up standards.                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                          |

#### Airwallex Closed-Deal Metrics Drill

This is the cut-down version. Use it only for sales numbers and metric fluency. The Stanley/process notes specifically flagged these numeric probes:

- Target / quota.
- Run-rate deals monthly or quarterly.
- Deals closed per month.
- Average deal size.
- Run-rate pipeline.
- Close rate, if relevant.
- Cycle length, if relevant.

Quick GP explanation:

- **GP = gross profit.** In Airwallex terms, this is the net amount Airwallex earns after direct costs. It is not TPV / payment volume.
- **New-account target:** roughly USD 5K of first-30-days GP from newly signed accounts.
- **Book scale:** roughly USD 500K annual revenue across the book, and about USD 900K when I left.
- **Live rule:** do not explain the internal quota mechanics unless they ask. The simple version is stronger.

Interview-safe stat stack:

> At Airwallex, quota was based on gross profit, so the net amount Airwallex earned after costs, not TPV.
>
> The simple live phrasing is: "I was measured on GP, not payment volume. My new-account target was roughly USD 5K of first-30-days GP from newly signed accounts."
>
> Across the book, I was responsible for roughly USD 500K in annual revenue, and by the time I left my book was about USD 900K.
>
> I closed 22 midmarket logos across the year, plus a higher-volume SME motion of about eight smaller closes a month, so roughly 10 total closes a month. The honest midmarket deal size was around USD 40K annualized gross profit, with a range from roughly USD 1K on the low end to about USD 100K on the largest deal.
>
> The travel account I would use as the deal story was the largest one: around USD 100K annualized GP on roughly USD 60M TPV. My close rate on non-disqualified opportunities was high, roughly 60%, because I disqualified hard and concentrated effort on high-conviction accounts. Typical sales cycle was about three months.

Interview-ready direct answers:

| If they ask                                       | Sharp answer                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                      |
| ------------------------------------------------- | --------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------- |
| **What was your quota or target?**                | "At Airwallex, I was measured on gross profit, not TPV. The clean version is that my new-account target was roughly USD 5K of first-30-days GP from newly signed accounts."                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                       |
| **What was your attainment?**                     | "Across the book, I was responsible for roughly USD 500K in annual revenue, and by the time I left my book was about USD 900K."                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                   |
| **How many deals did you close per month?**       | "Across the year, I closed 22 midmarket logos plus a higher-volume smaller-SME motion. The clean cadence is roughly two midmarket logos a month, plus about eight smaller SME closes, so roughly 10 total closes a month. I would anchor on the 22 midmarket logos because that is the cleaner strategic number, but the SME motion mattered for volume and activation practice."                                                                                                 |
| **What was your average deal size?**              | "For midmarket, the honest average was around USD 40K annualized gross profit. The range was broad: small SME deals could be around USD 1K, while the largest deal was about USD 100K annualized GP."                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                             |
| **What was your biggest deal?**                   | "The largest one was the travel account I would use as my deal story: about USD 100K annualized GP on roughly USD 60M TPV. That is a good example because the account had real payment volume, clear workflow pain, and expansion potential."                                                                                                                                                                                                                                     |
| **What was your run-rate pipeline?**              | "I would frame pipeline as coverage against the monthly target. My new-account M1 GP target was roughly USD 5K, so I wanted about 3x coverage, or roughly USD 15K in M1 GP pipeline. In broader book terms, the annual revenue target was about USD 500K, so roughly USD 40K a month. A comfortable pipe shape was at least two midmarket opportunities worth around USD 40K each, plus about 20 smaller opportunities worth around USD 10K each."                                |
| **What was your close rate?**                     | "On non-disqualified opportunities, roughly 60%. The important caveat is that I qualified hard. I was not trying to keep every weak deal alive. I dropped poor-fit accounts quickly and concentrated on accounts where the GP, payment volume, and expansion path justified the effort."                                                                                                                                                                                          |
| **What was your sales cycle?**                    | "Typically around three months from first touch to first meaningful transaction or close. Expansion could come after that once we proved reliability through the first workflow."                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                 |
| **How do you summarize the whole metrics story?** | "Airwallex was my quota discipline chapter. I was measured on gross profit, with a new-account target of roughly USD 5K first-30-days GP from newly signed accounts. Across the book, I was responsible for roughly USD 500K in annual revenue, and when I left the book was about USD 900K. The way I got there was not volume for its own sake; it was tight ICP, aggressive disqualification, and concentrating effort where the payment volume and expansion path were real." |

Metric backup:

| Stanley metric | Strong answer | Math / caveat |
|---|---|---|
| **Target / quota** | "I was measured on GP, not payment volume. My new-account target was roughly USD 5K of first-30-days GP from newly signed accounts." | This is the cleanest live version. Do not walk them through internal cohort mechanics unless they specifically ask. |
| **Book scale / attainment** | "Across the book, I was responsible for roughly USD 500K in annual revenue, and when I left my book was about USD 900K." | This is easier to understand than percentage math and avoids dragging the interviewer into Airwallex-specific reporting logic. |
| **Run-rate deals monthly or quarterly** | "I had two motions. On the midmarket side, I closed 22 logos across the year. On the SME side, the cadence was much higher, closer to eight smaller closes a month." | 22 midmarket logos/year = about 1.8 midmarket deals/month or 5.5/quarter. If SME cadence was about 8/month, total close cadence was roughly 10 deals/month or 30/quarter. |
| **Deals closed per month** | Best live phrasing: "Roughly 10 total closes a month: around two midmarket logos and about eight smaller SME deals. The 22 midmarket logos are the cleaner number I would anchor on; the SME count is the high-volume long tail." | This sounds stronger and more precise than "dozens," while still separating the clean logo metric from the higher-volume SME motion. |
| **Average deal size** | "The honest midmarket deal was around USD 40K annualized GP; the broader range was about USD 1K to USD 100K." | Define this as annualized gross profit / expected annual GP, not TPV and not SaaS ARR. The prior USD 40K-60K prep range is still usable as an annualized GP range if asked for average deal size. |
| **Largest / travel account** | "Largest was about USD 100K annualized GP, and that is the travel account I would use as my closed-deal story." | This is the strongest specific deal example. |
| **TPV / payment volume** | "That travel account had roughly USD 60M TPV." | If USD 100K GP and USD 60M TPV are same-period annual numbers, implied net take is about 0.17%, or 17 bps. That is plausible for payments, but keep it approximate. |
| **Run-rate pipeline** | "I would frame pipeline as coverage against the monthly target. My new-account M1 GP target was roughly USD 5K, so I wanted about 3x coverage, or roughly USD 15K in M1 GP pipeline. In broader book terms, the annual revenue target was about USD 500K, so roughly USD 40K a month." | Comfortable operating shape: at least two midmarket opportunities worth around USD 40K each, plus about 20 smaller opportunities worth around USD 10K each. Use this as pipeline discipline / coverage math, not a fake-precise CRM-export claim. |
| **Close rate** | "Around 60% for non-disqualified opportunities." | Caveat immediately: this was high because weak-fit deals were dropped early; it was not a spray-and-pray denominator. |
| **Sales cycle / cycle length** | "Typically around three months." | Define as first touch to first transaction or first meaningful close. Expansion often came after the first trust-building transaction. |

Safe answer if a number is missing:

> I want to be precise rather than inflate it. The clean version I can defend is that Airwallex measured me on GP, not TPV. My new-account target was roughly USD 5K of first-30-days GP from newly signed accounts.
>
> Across the book, I was responsible for roughly USD 500K in annual revenue, and when I left my book was about USD 900K.
>
> I closed 22 midmarket logos across the year plus a higher-volume smaller-SME motion, with a midmarket deal size around USD 40K annualized GP and a range from roughly USD 1K to USD 100K.
>
> For pipeline, I typically wanted about 3x coverage against the monthly target: roughly USD 15K in M1 GP pipeline against a USD 5K M1 GP target, with a comfortable shape of at least two midmarket opportunities around USD 40K each plus about 20 smaller opportunities around USD 10K each.
>
> The operating logic was disciplined: qualify hard, drop weak deals quickly, and concentrate effort on accounts where the payment-volume, gross-profit, and expansion path were real.

How that maps to OpenAI:

> I would approach OpenAI the same way. I would pick a narrow vertical or workflow and learn it deeply enough that the outreach feels native.
>
> Customer operations in a regional platform is different from legal review in a bank, developer productivity in a software company, claims review in insurance, or research/reporting inside a conglomerate.
>
> For each wedge, I would want to know: what work is repetitive but judgment-heavy, where people are opening ten tabs today, what system is the source of truth, what risk stops automation, what metric matters, what proof would make the buyer comfortable, and which stakeholder would look good if the pilot worked.
>
> The best first wedge is the workflow with clear before/after proof: something the buyer can name as painful, approve as a small pilot, and recognize as improved quickly.
>
> Then I would build the playbook with the AD: define the account universe, tier it, identify baseline ICP and best-case signals, map the buyer committee, test the message manually, capture objections, and turn the pattern into account rules, discovery prompts, proof points, and handoff notes.
>
> AI would help me move faster, but the actual sales judgment is still choosing the right wedge, the right account, and the right reason to talk.
>
> Doing this unconventionally is not easy. You face rejection externally and sometimes internally because the thesis may not look obvious at first. But that is part of the job. Be creative, hands-on, persistent, and willing to jump through obstacles. The goal is not to run the most polished campaign on day one.
>
> The goal is to find the account wedge that is true enough, urgent enough, and differentiated enough to become a repeatable OpenAI playbook.

Sales doctrine behind the answer:

- Do not import a playbook before diagnosing the market.
- Separate baseline ICP from best-case timing signals.
- A good playbook should say who not to pursue, not just who to pursue.
- Tier accounts so the best accounts get the most human attention.
- A signal can be true but not messageable.
- Finite TAM means every generic touch spends trust.
- Territory quality beats territory sprawl.
- The best first wedge is the workflow with clear before/after proof.
- Make the next step small enough for the buyer to trust.
- Proof has to feel close to the buyer's region, workflow, role, risk model, and business pressure.
- Founder-style market learning matters before automation: buyer language, objections, demo failure modes, and bad-fit patterns.
- AI should increase judgment density, not message volume.
- The playbook should preserve deal craft: account-specific evidence, warm paths, buyer co-authorship, proof gaps, human review, and AD-ready handoff notes.

#### If Asked: How I Run A Conference Playbook

Question it answers: "How do you run a conference playbook?" Also covers: "How do you get value from events?", "How do you create pipeline from conferences?", and "Tell me about an outbound or field-marketing motion you have run."

Short answer:

> The main conference I ran was Executive War College, held in New Orleans.
>
> My biggest learning is that a conference playbook has to start with the goal. Am I there to meet current customers, partners, prospects, find new customers or partners, or create content and collateral?
>
> Most people default to "creating leads," but that is by far the hardest goal. The best ROI I have seen is using conferences to create partnerships, referrals, field intelligence, and sharper messaging.
>
> Before the event, I would research exhibitors, sponsors, speakers, and target accounts, build a target list, and connect with people on LinkedIn. During the event, I would spend as little time as possible sitting in talks unless a priority prospect was speaking. The value is in conversations, partner booths, side events, coffee meetings, hallway moments, and the live market feedback you get when you pitch the same one-liner 50 times.
>
> After the event, I would turn the field notes into follow-up, CRM context, content, referral asks, partner next steps, and a better message for the next campaign.
>
> The lesson for OpenAI is that events are not just booth traffic. They are a way to find account signals, relationship paths, workflow language, partner routes, and high-conviction reasons for an AD to engage an account.

Full conference playbook:

| Principle | How I would run it | Interview-ready lesson |
|---|---|---|
| **Start with the primary goal** | Be clear whether the event is for current customers, partners, prospects, new customers, new partners, leads, content, or collateral. | A conference is not automatically a lead-gen channel. The operating plan changes depending on whether the goal is partnerships, referrals, pipeline, customer meetings, market learning, or content. |
| **Do not over-focus on cold lead creation** | Most people focus on "creating leads," but that is the most difficult conference goal. I have seen more success using conferences to create partnerships and referrals. | Leads are the visible output, but relationship paths and partner routes are often the higher-leverage output. |
| **Find conferences where ideal partners exhibit** | If the ideal partner has a booth, you do not need to rely only on pre-scheduled meetings or cold outreach. You can walk up, ask good questions, and learn who they sell to or serve. | Exhibitor booths reduce access friction. They turn cold outreach into live discovery. |
| **Use the event for content** | If there is already a content machine or audience working, the conference should generate material: "just attended this talk, biggest takeaways," "here is what I am hearing at the event," "just met X and had a great conversation," photos with the team, speakers, partners, or customers. | Content compounds the event beyond the room. It also creates inbound from people searching the hashtag or following the event. |
| **Work the LinkedIn hashtag** | Most conferences have a hashtag. Use it to find attendees if the list is not available, like their posts, send connection requests, post your own takeaways, and make yourself findable. | The hashtag is a live attendee graph. It helps with both pre-event targeting and real-time social proof. |
| **Research exhibitors, sponsors, and speakers ahead of time** | Build a target list before going. Send LinkedIn requests to exhibitors, sponsors, speakers, and priority attendees. Identify which talks matter because a cold priority lead is speaking. | The conference floor should not be a random walk. Pre-research turns the event into a named-account motion. |
| **Attend fewer talks** | In general, try to attend as few talks as possible. The value is in meeting people, not sitting and listening. The exception is when a priority prospect is speaking and you can reference a real note from their talk. | Talks are useful when they create account context. Otherwise, conversations are usually more valuable. |
| **Use the event to refine messaging** | Have 3-4 crisp one-liners and test what resonates. You will answer "what do you do?" and "how is that different from X competitor?" many times, and it quickly becomes clear what is confusing. | A conference is live message-market-fit testing. The best output may be sharper web, ad, sales, and outbound copy. |
| **Prefer smaller, focused conferences** | Smaller focused events are often better than large broad events, for example "mental health marketing conference" instead of "HLTH." | Specificity beats scale when the buyer or partner profile is narrow. |
| **Best ROI is talk or panel plus booth** | The best ROI comes when you can give a talk or panel and have a booth. People see your talk, then have an easy way to find you later. A booth alone can create a cold-start problem. | Authority plus accessibility beats passive presence. Speaking creates trust; the booth captures demand. |
| **Speaker hack** | If you have no connections, message organizers: "I am sure you are fully booked up on speakers, but if anyone drops on a panel or talk, I would be happy to sub in last minute; I am best for anything at the intersection of HIPAA and marketing." | Organizer responsiveness can be surprisingly practical. Last-minute speaker gaps are an opening if the topic is specific. |
| **You do not always need a ticket** | You can set up meetings at a coffee shop or bar nearby. I found this surprising when I first heard it, but roughly 20-30% of people around a conference may not have bought tickets. | The event gravity extends beyond the venue. Nearby coffee shops, bars, hotel lobbies, and dinners can be part of the playbook. |
| **Get phone numbers for scheduled meetings** | If scheduling many meetings, get cell numbers and share yours. There is a lot of last-minute rescheduling and it is hard to find people without direct contact. | Event logistics are messy. Phone numbers protect the meeting. |
| **Host casual cohosted side events** | Hosting events can be high ROI. The best shape is casual, like happy hour rather than dinner; cohosted with complementary firms that are bigger or more credible and sell into the same clients with a different product; scheduled early or at a time with minimal competition, like the night before the conference starts. | Side events work when commitment is low, credibility is borrowed, audiences are complementary, and timing avoids the main event crush. |
| **Let casual moments create conversations** | Some of the best connections happen when alone, catching up on work, or grabbing food. Instead of going back to the room, sit at a roundtop or table where someone is alone. After sending a few emails or taking a few bites, introduce yourself casually. Do not force it. Often they ask what you do and it turns into a strong lead. Same for elevators, coffee shops, and walks from the hotel. | Be aware and open without obviously working the room. Natural proximity can create higher-trust conversations than aggressive networking. |
| **Do not be pushy** | Do not obviously work the room. That is bad for everyone involved. | The tone matters. The goal is to be useful, curious, and present, not extractive. |
| **Still get value if you are not attending** | Chime in on posts from people who are there. Post about going or not going with the hashtag so people find you in research. | You can participate in the event graph without being physically present. |

What I would do differently:

> Conferences were incredibly effective for us, probably responsible for roughly 30% of revenue over a six-month period. But I think we could have gotten more from them.
>
> My strategy was low cost, slow roll, wide spread: going to 10-12 conferences as an attendee, with the plan to invest in a few in a bigger way the following year.
>
> I am not sure that was necessarily correct. There is a strong argument for going splashier. I underestimated how much partners and clients view your willingness to invest in booths or sponsorships as a sign of success and credibility.
>
> In hindsight, credibility and speed to market may have improved by spending more money and time at one major conference. The challenge was that I did not know which one to pick, but I think I could have figured that out through advisors.
>
> I also do not think I spent enough time trying to get on panels, speaking, or doing direct prospect outreach.

Guerrilla marketing tactics discussed:

1. **Truck advertising:** rent and park a truck outside the conference, paint it with the company logo for around $3,000, or use digital sides. This can be cheaper than official sponsorship.
2. **Strategic location control:** buy out nearby coffee shop bagels, target unclaimed real estate around the conference venue, and focus on areas where attendee eyeballs naturally go.
3. **Buzz example:** at a gaming conference, Exploding Kittens placed stickers on urinals, generated conversation, and drove hundreds of visitors per day to a small booth despite limited budget.

OpenAI version:

> For OpenAI, I would run a conference playbook as an account and signal engine, not just an event-attendance exercise.
>
> Before the event, I would align with the AD on the priority accounts, industries, and workflow hypotheses. Then I would map which target accounts are attending, sponsoring, speaking, hiring around AI, posting about governance, or already showing product/developer interest.
>
> During the event, I would use conversations to learn buyer language: where AI interest is real, where governance is blocking rollout, which workflows are painful, which competitors are already in the account, and what proof would make the AD conversation worth taking.
>
> After the event, I would turn that into AD-ready account notes: why this account, why now, who matters, what workflow came up, what proof is missing, and what next step is credible.
>
> The goal is to leave with better account intelligence, warmer paths, sharper messaging, partner/referral opportunities, and a few high-conviction opportunities, not just a badge scan list.

#### If Asked: How I Run A First Discovery Call

Question it answers: "How do you run discovery?", "What makes a good first call?", or "How do you qualify before handing to the AD?"

Core answer:

> First-call success is next-step quality, not whether the prospect liked the conversation. A good first call should answer: did we learn the business pain, confirm OpenAI relevance, identify who else is affected, understand what proof would make the buyer comfortable, and earn a concrete next step?
>
> I would show up with a point of view and account research, but I would not turn the call into a checklist. I do not need to force every MEDDICC field into the first conversation. The goal is to have a mutual business conversation and learn whether there is real pain where OpenAI can help.
>
> For OpenAI specifically, I would try to move from abstract AI interest into a specific workflow: what work is being done today, who owns it, what systems contain the truth, where the friction or risk is, what success would look like,
>
> and who would need to be involved before the company could seriously evaluate a pilot or deployment.

Call structure:

1. **Start with the account thesis:** "I saw this signal, and my hypothesis is that it may connect to this workflow or priority. Is that actually relevant, or am I off?"
2. **Understand the current workflow:** how the work gets done today, who touches it, what tools are involved, and where context gets lost.
3. **Find the business gap:** what is slow, expensive, risky, manual, inconsistent, or hard to scale, and what happens if nothing changes.
4. **Test urgency and timing:** executive priority, cost pressure, backlog, compliance concern, support issue, or AI initiative.
5. **Ask what proof would matter:** "What would you need to see to believe this could work in your environment?"
6. **Map stakeholders lightly:** workflow owner, users, technical evaluator, security/governance, finance/procurement/legal, blockers.
7. **Recap and confirm:** "So it sounds like you want to fix this because [reason], the gap is [gap], and the proof you would need is [proof]. Did I get that right?"
8. **Earn the next step:** technical discovery, workflow scoping, AD follow-up, governance conversation, pilot discussion, or disqualification.

Discovery question bank:

| Stage | Questions to keep in the pocket |
|---|---|
| Account thesis / priority | 1. "What's happening in the business now, that's driving [X] to be a priority?" |
| Current workflow / prior attempts | 2. "Have you tried to solve this before? What happened?" |
| Competing priority | 3. "What trumps that as an even larger initiative this year?" |
| Urgency / timing | 4. "Why now? What's different about this year versus last?" |
| Commitment / urgency test | 5. "Silly question — but fast forward 90 days. Are we okay if we haven't addressed this yet? Or are we committed to solving it now?" |
| Day-to-day owner | 6. "Who's feeling this the most on a day-to-day basis?" |
| Risk / proof | 7. "What's the biggest single risk to making this a success?" |
| Desired state | 8. "What does good look like 6 months from now?" |
| Buyer co-authorship | 9. "If you had built this slide, what would you add?" |
| Decision process | 10. "Walk me through how you'd make a decision like this." |
| Seller concern / concrete ask | 11. "My biggest concern is [X] — is it fair to ask [action]?" |
| Cost of inaction | 12. "What happens if you don't fix this?" |
| Recap | "So it sounds like you want to fix this because [reason], the gap is [gap], and the proof you would need is [proof]. Did I get that right?" |
| Next step or disqualification | Chris Pisarski disqualification line: "it sounds like you don't actually have a massive pain point right now. am I missing something, or does it make more sense to chat again in six months when this is a higher priority?" |

AD handoff fields:

| Field | What I want to know |
|---|---|
| Business pain | What problem is real, not just interesting? |
| Root cause | Why does the problem exist today? |
| Impact | Time, cost, risk, errors, delay, customer experience, developer velocity, or strategic priority. |
| Urgency | Why now, and what happens if nothing changes? |
| Workflow | Which process could OpenAI realistically improve? |
| Stakeholders | Business owner, technical evaluator, security/governance, finance/procurement/legal, users, blockers. |
| Proof criteria | What the buyer needs to see before they trust the solution. |
| Champion quality | Friendly user, evaluator, real champion, business owner, or unknown. |
| Missing proof | Security, ROI, technical fit, implementation plan, executive priority, procurement path. |
| Next step | Concrete meeting/action with owner, date, and purpose. |

Avoid:

- Turning the first call into a rigid qualification checklist.
- Asking "are you interested in AI?" and treating that as qualification.
- Pitching every OpenAI product surface before understanding the workflow.
- Running a group demo too early.
- Ending with vague next steps like "send me more info."
- Handing the AD a meeting with no account story.

Short version:

> I would judge the first call by next-step quality. Did we understand the workflow, business pain, urgency, stakeholders, proof criteria, and risk path well enough to know whether this is worth AD time?
>
> If yes, the handoff should make the AD smarter: here is why this account, why now, who matters, what pain is real, what proof is missing, and what next step is credible.

#### If Asked: Account Prioritization / TAM Mapping

Question it answers: "How would you decide which accounts to prioritize?"

Use if asked how to "map the TAM," build a named account list, or decide where to spend time.

- Start with the full universe of accounts that could plausibly become strategic opportunities.
- Apply ICP filters: vertical, geography, size, regulatory complexity, knowledge-work intensity, developer/product surface, and likely business value.
- Score fit at the workflow level: workflow value, OpenAI fit, ability to act, reason now, and path to power.
- Separate demand capture from demand creation: some accounts already show usage, inbound interest, hiring, events, public AI goals, or pilots; others need education, monitoring, and the right trigger.
- Build a named account list with tiering, exclusions, and verification path.
- Choose the first workflow wedge before choosing the first message.
- Separate true signals from messageable signals.
- Map who has to believe, approve, defend, implement, and use the workflow.
- Track proof gaps: business value, technical feasibility, security/governance, procurement, executive priority, and champion strength.
- Handoff to the AD only when the account has enough opportunity shape to be worth a strategic conversation.

#### If Asked: Messageability

Question it answers: "How do you know whether a signal is worth acting on?"

> A signal can be true but not messageable. A company can hire AI engineers, attend an event, publish an AI strategy, or use a competitor, but if I cannot connect that signal to a person, workflow, business pressure, and respectful reason to engage, it may belong in nurture rather than outbound.

Messageability checklist:

- Is the account a fit?
- Is the signal recent enough?
- Is there a named person or stakeholder group?
- Is there a workflow hypothesis?
- Is there a business pressure behind the workflow?
- Is there a proof point close to their world?
- Is there a respectful reason to talk now?
- Can the first message be understood in 15 seconds?
- Would I be comfortable explaining the source of the insight if the buyer asked?

#### If Asked: Stakeholder Mapping

Question it answers: "How would you map stakeholders in a strategic account?"

> I would map stakeholders around the decision path, not just title seniority.
>
> In an OpenAI account, I want to know who owns the workflow, who can champion the use case, who validates technical fit, who worries about security and governance, who controls budget or procurement, who would implement it, who would use it every day, and who could block it.
>
> The job is to understand what each person needs to believe before the account can move from AI curiosity to a real opportunity.

Stakeholder proof gaps:

- Business owner: workflow cost, value metric, current friction.
- Champion: internal narrative, proof point, risk answer, next meeting objective.
- Technical evaluator: systems of record, APIs, data quality, integration path.
- Security/governance: permissions, auditability, data boundaries, human review.
- Finance/procurement: TCO, usage controls, pilot scope, buying path.
- Executive sponsor: why now, why this workflow, why it matters strategically.
- End users: adoption path, workflow fit, escalation, feedback loop.

#### If Asked: Target Accounts First

Question it answers: "Which accounts or account types would you target first?"

Default answer:

> I would start with OpenAI's internal proof, not a blank spreadsheet of large logos.
>
> I would want to deeply understand the case studies that already work: what was the workflow, who owned it, what pain made the buyer move, what risk had to be underwritten, what proof made the deployment credible, and what changed after implementation.
>
> Then I would look for the best APAC lookalikes where that proof point would really stick. If there is a strong OpenAI story around customer operations, I would look for regional platforms, banks, insurers, telcos, retailers, and healthcare groups with similar support volume or knowledge-work pressure.
>
> If there is a strong story around developer velocity or internal knowledge, I would look for engineering-heavy companies, regional HQs, and regulated enterprises where the same workflow could translate.
>
> I would still probably start in Singapore because it concentrates regional HQs, financial services, public-sector innovation, regulated buyers, English-language enterprise selling, and cross-border workflows.
>
> But the motion is not "start with Singapore because it is convenient." It is: take the strongest OpenAI proof points, translate them into APAC account hypotheses, then find the local accounts where the buyer map, workflow pain, governance readiness, and urgency make the story believable.

Account categories:

- Financial services and insurers: lookalikes for governed knowledge work, compliance, customer operations, risk workflows, and developer productivity.
- Healthcare groups: lookalikes for patient access, care navigation, operational load, multilingual support, and review-heavy workflows.
- Retail, telco, and platform companies: lookalikes for customer support, seller or merchant operations, trust and safety, personalization, and internal knowledge.
- Regional HQs and conglomerates: lookalikes for cross-market operating complexity, reporting, research, procurement, and compliance coordination.
- Developer-heavy companies: lookalikes for API adoption, Codex, product AI, internal tools, and agentic workflow systems.

### APAC And Outbound Execution

#### APAC Nuance

> APAC is not one market; each country requires its own approach.
>
> At Boxo I worked across APAC, selling to or partnering with major fintech and wallet platforms in different markets: MoMo and Zalo or ZaloPay in Vietnam, Touch 'n Go in Malaysia, DANA in Indonesia, TrueMoney in Thailand, GCash in the Philippines, and others.
>
> The biggest lesson was that product-market fit and sales motion do not travel cleanly from one market to another.
>
> The country-level thesis matters:
>
> - **Singapore:** Lead with enterprise HQ density, financial-services concentration, government AI credibility, and cross-border operating complexity.
>
> It is often the easiest SEA starting point because many global and regional enterprises run APAC strategy, finance, legal, procurement, compliance, data, and transformation teams out of Singapore.
>
> The buyer map is usually clearer, English-language executive access is stronger, and the local market has credible signals around AI governance, public-sector adoption, and regulated financial services.
> - **Thailand:** Lead with first-mover fintech muscle. Based on my APAC fintech experience, Thailand has a pattern of moving fast when the use case is practical, consumer-facing, and infrastructure-backed.
>
> PromptPay launched in 2016 as national payment infrastructure for low-fee transfers using citizen ID or mobile number.
>
> That was early relative to peer SEA rails: Singapore's PayNow launched in 2017, the Philippines' PESONet / InstaPay rails launched in 2017 / 2018, Malaysia's DuitNow launched in late 2018, Indonesia's QRIS launched in 2019 and BI-FAST in 2021, Cambodia's Bakong launched in 2020, and Vietnam's VietQR launched in 2021.
>
> Visa's 2024 study found 97% of Thai consumers using mobile banking apps weekly and Thailand leading regional real-time-payment usage frequency at 86% weekly.
>
> The point is not "Thailand is ahead of everyone in AI." It is: Thailand has been an early mover in fintech before, so I would watch Thai banks, insurers, payment providers, and large consumer platforms for early AI adoption too.
> - **Indonesia:** Lead with scale, but show current awareness. Indonesia is too large to ignore: it is Southeast Asia's biggest economy, has a huge consumer/platform market, and has the largest developer-account base in Southeast Asia in the GitHub data I checked.
>
