> But in 2026 I would be more cautious about the sales posture because the country is dealing with a confidence problem: a weak rupiah, stock-market stress, MSCI scrutiny over free float and ownership transparency, and concern around Prabowo-era government execution, fiscal discipline, and state-led policy.
>
> The stress is showing up in real companies too: Barito Renewables, Chandra Asri, Petrindo, Amman Mineral, Dian Swastatika, and Alfamart were among the large names removed from MSCI's Indonesia standard index, while major state-linked banks have had to answer questions around sovereign and policy pressure.
>
> So the OpenAI wedge should not be "AI transformation as shiny spend." I would focus on resilient banks, telcos, platforms, state-linked enterprises, and conglomerates where AI can improve customer operations, compliance/risk review, reporting, internal productivity, and developer velocity with clear ROI and governance.
> - **Vietnam:** Lead with engineering depth, digital-economy growth, developer/building appetite, and product/API use cases. I would avoid saying "largest engineering outsourcing hub" globally, because India is the obvious global giant, and I would even be careful inside SEA because the counting method matters.
>
> On GitHub's Q4 2025 developer-account data, Indonesia is larger at about 4.9M developer accounts, while Vietnam is about 2.8M, just ahead of the Philippines at about 2.6M and ahead of Singapore, Thailand, and Malaysia.
>
> Vietnam's wedge is not "biggest by every measure"; it is the combination of a large technical pool, strong software-outsourcing/product-engineering reputation, and digital-economy growth.
>
> Recent market sources cluster around roughly 530K-560K software / IT professionals and 55K-60K new tech graduates per year, while the U.S.
>
> International Trade Administration says Vietnam's digital economy reached about 18% of GDP in 2024 and has been outpacing ASEAN growth. But senior-buyer access, language, local relationships, data/cybersecurity rules, and partner strategy matter more.
>
> I would not assume a Singapore-style enterprise sales motion travels cleanly into Vietnam.
> - **Philippines:** Lead with English-language operations, customer experience, BPO/IT-BPM, support, finance ops, and GCash-style digital adoption.
>
> The macro context matters: AMRO says the Philippine IT-BPM sector generated USD38B in 2024, equal to 8.2% of GDP, employed 1.8M workers, and remained heavily contact-center-led, with contact centers accounting for 83% of revenue and 89% of employment.
>
> That means the AI pitch cannot just be "replace cheap labor." A sharper example is voice AI for contact centers: noise cancellation, accent localization, speech-to-speech translation, sentiment/tonal analytics, QA, agent assist, and bounded outbound voice bots that qualify or reach the right person before handing off to a human.
>
> The value case is better revenue conversion, fewer errors, faster QA, lower handle time, stronger compliance, and moving Filipino operators from routine call handling into higher-value AI-assisted CX work.
> - **Engineering-seat nuance:** I would be careful selling pure engineering-seat productivity in SEA exactly the way it is sold in the US. The US has more mega-accounts with huge engineering budgets and very high developer-cost leverage.
>
> In SEA, developer labor can be cheaper and the large engineering books are thinner, so the better wedge may be developer productivity plus internal tools, workflow automation, knowledge work, support operations, or API/product use cases rather than "buy seats for every engineer" as the only story.
> - **Voice/customer-support wedge:** Voice and customer support look like especially practical SEA wedges because the region has large support operations, multilingual service complexity, and many companies still rely on call centers or IVR-style flows.
>
> The fact that vendors such as Sierra, HappyRobot, Deepgram, and similar voice/support AI companies are pushing into the category is a useful market-signal hypothesis, but I would verify current regional traction before citing it live.
>
> The safer interview version is: "customer operations is one of the first places I would look for workflow pain, because the volume, QA, handoff, and language problems are visible."
>
> But the bigger point is the adoption curve. In the US, for some categories, you might be selling after the category has hit product-market fit and the market is already aware. So the pitch can sound like "we are the best platform for X." In APAC, you may be earlier, so I would not simply copy a US enterprise playbook.
>
> You are not always selling to the majority yet; you are recruiting the first innovators and using that to build an APAC-specific reference base. Early adopters can be rarer in APAC. Trust cycles are long. References travel slowly.
>
> I would match the account strategy to buyer readiness to innovate. The sharper APAC motion is: "We are new here, but we have proof in the US. We are looking for the first few innovators in this region that want to define what AI-enabled work looks like in their industry."
>
> And I would focus a lot on trust and showing that we really understand the company's identity. The job is to act as a trusted advisor over time: ask good questions, understand the problems keeping this specific company up at night, and translate OpenAI's proof into a path that feels credible for them.


#### First-Touch Email Philosophy

> The first email should prove I did enough work to earn 15 seconds. It should not try to explain all of OpenAI. One account signal, one workflow hypothesis, one business reason, one small ask.

#### No-Interest Reply

> I would treat no interest as signal, not failure. I would thank them, ask one optional learning question if appropriate, route the account to nurture if the fit is still strong, and update the reason: bad timing, wrong persona, weak proof, wrong workflow, no budget, incumbent, or low urgency.
>
> The goal is to improve account intelligence, not force the thread.

#### Multi-Threading

> I think of multi-threading as mapping who has to believe, approve, defend, implement, and use the workflow. In an OpenAI account that may include the business owner, technical champion, governance/security owner, economic buyer, procurement/legal, and executive sponsor. I would not multi-thread by spamming everyone.
>
> I would sequence based on the buyer's internal path and what proof each stakeholder needs.

#### Account Director Handoff

Strong AD handoff should include:

- Account thesis.
- Why now.
- Stakeholder map.
- Champion and champion risk.
- Business workflow.
- Current state.
- Desired outcome.
- Proof already shown.
- Proof missing.
- Security/procurement/legal state.
- Competitor/incumbent context.
- Next best action.
- What not to say or assume.

Handoff line:

> My job is to make the AD smarter before they enter the account, not busier.

## Commercial And Deal Answers

### Commercial Principles

#### Value-Based Selling

> Value-based selling means the buyer's current state becomes expensive or risky enough to change. I would start by understanding the reference point: target, KPI, deadline, board priority, customer-experience issue, or operational constraint.
>
> Then I would quantify the current gap, understand why it exists, and clarify what happens if nothing changes.
>
> Only then should the product enter as the bridge.

OpenAI version:

> For OpenAI, that means not selling "AI" generally. It means finding the workflow where delay, manual work, risk, customer experience, developer velocity, or compliance load has a measurable cost.

#### What An RFP Actually Looks Like — KISS Version

Question it answers: "What is an RFP?" or "What does an RFP process actually look like?"

> An RFP — Request for Proposal — is basically a structured document or procurement portal a customer sends to several vendors so they can compare them consistently.
>
> In practice, it usually feels like a long questionnaire plus a proposal brief.

A typical RFP contains:

1. **Background:** who the customer is and what problem they are trying to solve.
2. **Scope:** the product, workflow, users, regions, integrations, or services they want.
3. **Requirements:** a checklist of functional and technical capabilities, often answered with yes, no, partial, explanation, and supporting evidence.
4. **Security and legal:** questions about data, privacy, compliance, reliability, support, and contract terms.
5. **Implementation:** proposed approach, timeline, responsibilities, training, support, and success criteria.
6. **Commercials:** pricing template, contract length, payment terms, and any requested assumptions.
7. **Process:** submission format, deadline, Q&A window, demo or presentation dates, and buyer contacts.
8. **Evaluation:** how the customer will score vendors—for example product fit, security, implementation, experience, and price.

Common documents or evidence the vendor may need to provide:

1. **Completed RFP response / compliance matrix:** every requirement answered with `yes`, `no`, `partial`, an explanation, and evidence.
2. **Company information:** legal entity, ownership, locations, leadership, financial stability, and relevant experience.
3. **Product and technical pack:** product overview, architecture or data-flow diagram, integration/API documentation, and technical requirements.
4. **Security pack:** security questionnaire plus available certifications or reports such as SOC 2 or ISO 27001; sometimes penetration-test, incident-response, business-continuity, and disaster-recovery information.
5. **Privacy and data pack:** data-processing terms, subprocessor list, data location/residency, retention and deletion approach, and privacy documentation.
6. **Implementation plan:** project phases, timeline, responsibilities, dependencies, migration or integration approach, testing, training, and go-live plan.
7. **Support and SLA:** support hours, response times, escalation path, uptime or service commitments, and account-management model.
8. **Commercial proposal:** pricing, usage or volume assumptions, implementation fees, contract term, payment terms, and optional services.
9. **Contract documents:** proposed MSA/order form, DPA, SLA, statement of work, and any exceptions or requested redlines.
10. **Proof and administration:** customer references or case studies, insurance certificates, tax/bank/vendor-onboarding forms, and any industry-specific documents.

Easy interview recall:

> The evidence pack normally falls into six buckets: company, product and technical, security and privacy, implementation and support, commercials and contracts, and customer proof. I would not personally invent every answer; I would coordinate the correct internal owner and make sure every claim was current, approved, and supported.

What normally happens next:

`RFP issued → vendors submit → buyer scores responses → shortlist/demo or clarification → preferred vendor → security/legal/commercial negotiation → contract and approval → implementation`

What I would do as the seller:

- Read every requirement and build a simple checklist.
- Assign each section to the right owner: sales, solutions, product, security, legal, or finance.
- Ask clarification questions before the deadline.
- Answer the exact question and attach proof instead of pasting generic marketing copy.
- Track what happens after submission, because winning the RFP does not automatically mean the contract is signed.

Interview-ready answer:

> An RFP is basically a structured way for a company to compare vendors. It normally includes the business problem, scope, product and technical requirements, security questions, implementation expectations, pricing, response instructions, and the scoring criteria.
>
> We would divide the response across the right internal owners, make sure every requirement was answered with evidence, submit it in the requested format, and prepare for the next gates—usually clarification, a demo, security and legal review, commercial negotiation, and final approval.
>
> The key distinction is that the RFP is one step inside procurement. Procurement is the broader journey from deciding to buy through evaluation, contracting, approval, and onboarding.

Unabank truth boundary:

> I would not say the Unabank deal used a formal RFP unless I confirm that. I can confidently describe its decision process—CEO interest, Product and Engineering validation, legal/compliance and commercial negotiation, then implementation—without inventing an RFP.

Source basis: [GSA's solicitation guide](https://www.gsa.gov/sell-to-government/step-2-compete-for-a-contract/research-active-solicitations) and [Acquisition.gov FAR 15.2](https://www.acquisition.gov/far/subpart-15.2).

### Case Tables And Proof Library

#### Canonical Case Table: Sample Healthcare Referral Intake Workflow

Question it answers: "Tell me about an AI workflow you helped map or build," "How do you think about forward-deployed AI deployment?", "What is a technical workflow story outside sales?", or "How do you make AI useful in a regulated workflow?"

Short answer:

> The simplest way to tell the Sample Healthcare story is referral intake. The clean example is SUN Behavioral.
>
> A referral partner sends a patient referral through a fax, form, portal message, email, or call note, and the intake team has to turn that messy packet into a clean case: who the patient is, why they are being referred, what service they may need, what insurance they have, what consent or admin details are missing, and what the next step should be.
>
> The system was not deciding care. It was helping with the front-office coordination work before a human decision: read the referral, pull out the key fields, spot what is missing, flag the exceptions, and prepare the case so the right human can review it, check coverage, or move it toward scheduling.
>
> The difficult part was the exceptions. A normal referral is straightforward.
>
> The messy ones are where the work lives: an unreadable fax, a duplicate patient, missing insurance, missing consent, a minor without guardian details, unclear service requested, wrong facility, out-of-network coverage, a patient who cannot be reached, or language that suggests the case needs human review.
>
> So the goal was not "AI handles referral intake end to end." The goal was: move clean cases faster, and make the exception queue much easier and safer for humans to work through.
>
> My role was closest to workflow mapping and implementation design. I was not the clinician, and I was not claiming the model could own care decisions.
>
> The useful work was sitting close to the operator workflow, understanding what the intake team actually checked, turning that into fields, branches, review rules, missing-information loops, and handoffs, then using low-code / node-based workflow tools to make the process concrete.

Internal source note: this is grounded in the career-ops SF technical reinvention notes, healthcare workflow maps, enterprise AI case-study response pattern, and Sample conference GTM index. Use SUN Behavioral as the live example.

Keep the workflow basis to referral intake only: referral packet, fax/form/portal/call-note inputs, patient identity, service requested, clinical reason, insurance, consent/admin details, missing fields, coverage readiness, review queue, scheduling readiness, CRM/EHR handoff, and audit trail.

Guardrail: do not claim clinical judgment, patient-care decisions, triage ownership, final routing authority, full EHR replacement, claims/billing ownership, prior-auth ownership, or autonomous scheduling without human oversight.

Personal contribution to say live:

- Mapped the current intake workflow with operators: what arrives, where it arrives, who touches it, what they check, what slows them down, and where cases get stuck.
- Translated the workflow into a system shape: required fields, source documents, confidence thresholds, exception branches, missing-information loops, review queues, and CRM/EHR handoff.
- Used the internal low-code / node-based workflow builder to think through the control flow: trigger -> parse -> normalize -> decide -> act -> review -> recover -> record.
- Kept the trust boundary clear: AI prepares the intake packet and exception queue; humans review sensitive cases, coverage ambiguity, care fit, and final operational decisions.
- Treated the build like a forward-deployed workflow exercise: sit close to the work, build the first wedge, watch the exceptions, and turn repeated exceptions into rules, schemas, eval cases, or product feedback.

Sample Healthcare example and playbook:

| Principle | Sample Healthcare detail | Playbook lesson |
|---|---|---|
| **Diagnosis before tactics** | The problem was referral intake, not "healthcare AI" in general. A referral partner sends over a patient referral, often through fax, form, portal, email, or call notes. Someone on the intake team has to read it, understand the patient, copy the key details, check what is missing, and decide where the case should go next. That manual work is slow, repetitive, and easy to get wrong when volume is high. The diagnosis was that the real bottleneck lived in the messy middle: intake staff were translating unstructured referral material into a case state the rest of the organization could trust. | Start with the workflow, not the model. The useful question is: what is the human doing with every referral today, what slows them down, what is risky to automate, and what output would actually help them move faster? |
| **TAM segmentation before ICP** | I would narrow the story to teams with high referral volume and messy referral packets. The best fit is not every clinic. It is a provider group where referrals come in through multiple channels, contain partial information, and require staff to chase missing details before the patient can be scheduled. The anti-ICP is a workflow where the buyer expects AI to make care decisions, replace clinical judgment, or auto-route sensitive cases without review. | The ICP is not "healthcare company interested in AI." It is "team with high-volume referral intake where AI can organize the case and humans can review exceptions." |
| **Market map before account list** | The referral flow was simple to explain. First, the referral comes in. Second, the system reads the packet and pulls out the basic fields: patient name, contact details, referral source, service requested, clinical reason, insurance, and admin/consent details. Third, it flags what is missing or unclear. Fourth, it sends the case to the right queue: ready for scheduling, needs missing information, needs coverage check, or needs human review. The technical control flow was: trigger from fax/form/portal/email/call-note input; parse the document or note; normalize patient, insurance, service, consent, and referral-source fields; decide based on completeness and confidence; act by preparing the packet or follow-up task; route to review; recover from bad OCR, missing attachments, or conflicting records; and record the state for the downstream team. | The map is the workflow. In this kind of AI deployment, you have to know the input, the fields, the missing-information loop, the review queue, and the handoff before you can build or sell anything credible. |
| **Finite-TAM trust** | Trust mattered because this is the front door of care. If the system gets a referral wrong, the patient can wait longer, the referral partner can lose trust, or the staff can waste time cleaning up bad data. The trade-off was speed versus safety: move clean referrals faster, but do not silently push risky or incomplete cases forward. The system needed source evidence, missing-field flags, clear human review points, and a PHI-aware operating posture: only access the documents needed for the case, preserve source traceability, keep audit logs, and use de-identified or tightly controlled examples during shadow testing where possible. | The buyer does not need a magical AI answer. They need a workflow they can trust: show the source, flag uncertainty, stop when something is sensitive, and let humans approve the next step. In healthcare, governance is part of the product: permissions, logs, review queues, source citations, and bounded actions are what make adoption possible. |
| **Tiering** | I would split the work into three lanes. High-confidence referrals can be structured and moved toward scheduling. Medium-confidence referrals become review packets with the missing fields highlighted. High-risk referrals stop for a human: missing consent, minor/guardian issues, unclear service requested, possible high-acuity language, identity mismatch, or anything that could affect care routing. | The practical maturity path is not "automate everything." It is: automate the clean repetitive work, make exceptions easier to review, and keep high-consequence decisions with people. |
| **Right signal, right timing** | The right signal was referral pain: a backlog of faxes, staff retyping the same information, missing insurance or consent details, referral sources calling for updates, patients waiting to be scheduled, or leadership wanting more throughput without only hiring more intake staff. | AI curiosity is weak signal. A live referral bottleneck is strong signal because it affects patient access, staff capacity, referral conversion, and revenue. |
| **Proof proximity** | The proof should use the customer's own referrals. I would want a small test set: a clean referral, a blurry fax, a duplicate patient, missing insurance, missing consent, a minor without guardian details, unclear service requested, wrong facility, out-of-network coverage, patient unreachable, high-acuity language, and one case that should clearly go to human review. The point is to show whether the system can create a useful intake packet without inventing information. | The buyer needs to see the system handle their messy reality. A generic demo matters less than a shadow run on real referral packets with source evidence and reviewer agreement. |
| **Buyer committee** | Intake/admissions cared about speed and missing fields. Clinical reviewers cared that sensitive cases were not auto-routed. RCM or finance cared about insurance, coverage readiness, and avoidable denials. Leadership cared about patient access, referral leakage, and operating leverage. IT/security/compliance cared about PHI, permissions, logs, and audit trail. | One workflow has multiple definitions of value. The seller has to translate referral intake into operational speed, clinical safety, revenue readiness, and governance. |
| **Talk tracks by stakeholder / message market fit** | **Intake team:** "This helps you avoid reading every fax from scratch. It creates the first-pass referral packet and shows what is missing."<br><br>**Clinical reviewer:** "This does not make care decisions. It flags sensitive or unclear cases so humans can review them with better context."<br><br>**RCM / finance:** "This surfaces insurance and coverage-readiness issues earlier, before the patient reaches scheduling."<br><br>**IT / compliance:** "Every field should trace back to a source document, with permissions, logs, and human approval for sensitive steps."<br><br>**CEO / operator:** "The business value is faster referral processing, fewer dropped referrals, less staff retyping, and better visibility into where cases are stuck." | Use normal workflow language: referral packet, missing fields, review queue, coverage check, scheduling readiness, source document, and human approval. That sounds much more credible than saying "AI automates healthcare intake." |
| **Gap selling to CEO** | **Current state:** Referrals arrive through messy channels and staff manually read, copy, chase, and route them.<br><br>**Desired state:** Every referral becomes a clean intake packet with key fields, missing items, source evidence, and a next step.<br><br>**Measurable shortfall:** Referral turnaround time, backlog size, missing-field rate, duplicate referral rate, time spent retyping, time spent chasing referral sources, percentage of cases ready for scheduling, exception rate, and intake staff trust in the packet. I would avoid claiming audited ROI unless the evidence is in front of me, but those are the operating metrics I would use to prove the workflow.<br><br>**Root cause:** The information exists, but it is scattered across faxes, forms, packets, portal notes, insurance cards, and staff judgment. The work is not one hard clinical decision; it is a repeated translation problem from messy inbound evidence into a reliable case state.<br><br>**Cost of doing nothing:** Slower patient access, more manual work, dropped referrals, frustrated referral partners, staff burnout, avoidable coverage surprises, and less visibility into why cases are stuck.<br><br>**Bridge:** AI organizes the referral and flags exceptions so humans can move faster without giving up control. | The executive case is simple: current state, desired state, measurable gap, why the gap exists, what happens if nothing changes, and how a bounded AI workflow bridges it safely. |
| **Fast follow-up loop and onboarding** | I would start in shadow mode. Take a batch of recent referrals, map how the intake team handled them, define the fields that matter, run the AI extraction, and compare the output against what the human team actually did. Then review the misses: wrong field, missing field, unclear source, wrong queue, or case that should have stopped for human review. In practice, this is where the low-code / node-based mindset mattered: every miss becomes a branch, validation rule, confidence threshold, missing-info task, review queue condition, or source-citation requirement. | Onboarding is part of discovery. You learn the workflow by testing real referrals with operators, not by asking abstract questions in a conference room. A good deployment feels like the system is learning the team's actual workflow, not forcing the team to adapt to a demo. |
| **Wedge product and land-and-expand** | The wedge was referral packet creation. Land by helping the team turn messy referrals into clean review packets. Only after that is trusted would I expand into missing-info outreach, coverage checks, scheduling support, referral-source updates, or reporting. | Start with the smallest workflow that unlocks the rest. In this case, if the referral packet is clean, the next handoff gets easier. |
| **AI increases judgment density** | AI takes the repetitive reading and copying work off the human's plate. The human then spends more time on the cases that actually require judgment: is this the same patient, is consent complete, is the service request clear, does this need urgent review, is the case ready for scheduling, or do we need to chase the referral source? | AI should make the human more focused, not remove the human from the loop. The win is better use of expert attention. |
| **Living feedback loop** | Every correction becomes training material for the workflow: wrong field, missed insurance, duplicate patient, unclear service requested, bad OCR, missing consent, referral source not reachable, or a case routed to the wrong queue. Those examples become eval cases, prompt/schema changes, validation rules, human-review gates, and product feedback. The forward-deployed lesson was that the most valuable workflow logic often lives in the exceptions. You learn the real product by watching where operators correct the system. | The workflow should improve from real operator feedback. Without that loop, the system stays a demo instead of becoming part of daily operations. |

Sample Healthcare metrics drill:

| If they ask | Sharp answer |
|---|---|
| **Was it prior auth or referral intake?** | "For the clean interview version, I would make it referral intake. I had adjacent exposure to prior-auth-style evidence extraction, but I would not lead with that. The simple story is: referral comes in messy, AI creates the first-pass intake packet, humans review exceptions." |
| **What did you personally do?** | "My contribution was workflow mapping and implementation design. I worked from the operator workflow: what arrives, what fields matter, what is missing, what should stop for human review, and what downstream handoff needs to happen. Then I translated that into a node-based control flow: trigger, parse, normalize, decide, act, review, recover, and record." |
| **What exactly was the workflow?** | "A referral partner sends a patient referral by fax, form, portal, email, or call note. The system reads it, pulls out patient details, referral source, service requested, clinical reason, insurance, consent/admin fields, and missing information, then prepares a review packet for intake staff." |
| **What made it technically hard?** | "The hard part was not the model call. It was control flow and data shape: messy PDFs and faxes, OCR errors, duplicate patients, inconsistent field names, missing attachments, insurance ambiguity, confidence thresholds, human review queues, and making sure the output was traceable back to the source." |
| **What made it difficult?** | "The exceptions: blurry fax, duplicate patient, missing insurance, missing consent, minor without guardian details, unclear service requested, wrong facility, out-of-network coverage, high-acuity language, patient unreachable, or referral source waiting for a status update." |
| **What was the measurable outcome?** | "I would measure referral turnaround time, referral backlog, missing-field rate, duplicate referral rate, percentage ready for scheduling, exception rate, and staff time spent retyping or chasing information. I would avoid claiming audited ROI unless the evidence is in front of me." |
| **Why is this relevant to OpenAI sales?** | "It taught me that enterprise AI opportunities are workflow-first. You cannot just ask if someone wants AI. You need to understand the input, the source of truth, the reviewer, the exception path, the business metric, and the proof that would make the customer trust it." |
| **Where did humans stay in the loop?** | "Humans reviewed sensitive or unclear cases: possible acuity, consent issues, minor/guardian questions, unclear service request, coverage ambiguity, and any final operational decision. The system prepared the packet; it did not decide care." |
| **What would you test before production?** | "Field extraction accuracy, duplicate detection, missing-field detection, source citation quality, routing accuracy, exception rate, audit trail quality, and whether intake staff trusted the review packet." |
| **What would you not automate?** | "I would not automate clinical judgment, patient-care decisions, final routing authority, high-acuity triage, autonomous scheduling, claims/billing decisions, or anything where the system cannot show source evidence and a human review path." |
| **What did this teach you about FDE-style deployment?** | "You have to sit inside the workflow long enough to learn the operator logic. The reusable product primitive is not the demo; it is the pattern: extract the right fields, preserve the source, flag missing context, route exceptions, and use corrections to improve the workflow." |
| **What is the one-line lesson?** | "The model is useful only when wrapped in the workflow: source packet, required fields, missing-info flags, review queue, audit trail, and human approval." |

90-second version:

> At Sample Healthcare, I would keep the story simple and tell one example: behavioral-health referral intake.
>
> A referral partner sends a patient referral by fax, form, portal message, email, or call note.
>
> The intake team's job is to turn that messy packet into a clean case: who the patient is, why they were referred, what service they may need, what insurance they have, what is missing, and whether the case is ready for scheduling or needs human review.
>
> The deceptively hard part was the exceptions. A clean referral is easy. Real referrals have blurry faxes, duplicate patients, missing insurance, missing consent, minors without guardian details, unclear service requests, wrong facility, coverage surprises, or behavioral-health language that should go to a human.
>
> So the goal was not to blindly automate referrals end to end. The goal was to move clean referrals faster and make the exception queue easier and safer for humans to review.
>
> The system would read the referral packet, pull out the key fields, show the source evidence, flag missing information, and suggest the next queue: ready for scheduling, needs missing information, needs coverage check, or needs human review.
>
> My role was mapping that operator workflow and turning it into system logic. The shape was trigger, parse, normalize, decide, act, review, recover, and record. That sounds a little mechanical, but that was the real lesson: useful AI in healthcare is not the model call.
>
> It is the harness around it: the source packet, required fields, confidence threshold, exception queue, audit trail, and human approval path.
>
> The business case was not generic AI. It was faster referral processing, fewer dropped referrals, less staff time spent retyping or chasing information, cleaner scheduling handoffs, and better visibility into where cases were stuck.
>
> The lesson I took from it is that enterprise AI sales has to be workflow-first: define the input, the required fields, the reviewer, the exception path, and the metric before you sell the model.

Truth boundaries:

- Do say: SUN Behavioral was the referral-intake example for behavioral health.
- Do say: referral intake, referral partner, fax/form/portal/call-note inputs, patient identity, service requested, insurance details, missing fields, scheduling readiness, review queue, auditability, and human review.
- Do say: your role was workflow mapping and implementation design: fields, branches, missing-info loops, review queues, source traceability, and handoffs.
- Do say: the technical spine was trigger, parse, normalize, decide, act, review, recover, and record.
- Do say: prior-auth / evidence extraction was adjacent Sample context only if asked, but keep the main interview example as referral intake.
- Do not say: Sample made final payer decisions, owned the full prior-auth lifecycle, replaced clinical judgment, automated care delivery, owned claims / billing / payer submission end to end, made patient-care decisions, or triaged patients without human review.

#### Canonical Case Table: Salescraft / Uptick Vertical TAM + CRM Enrichment System

Question it answers: "What practical AI workflow would you build for sales?", "Tell me about a GTM engineering project," "How do you use AI for account selection?", or "What is a Salescraft example beyond email generation?"

Short answer:

> The Uptick story is my best proof that AI in sales is much more interesting than writing outbound emails.
>
> Uptick had inbound leads, but the customer wanted one clean view of the wider US fire-protection market. The raw data from the CRM, Google Maps, websites, and other sources was messy: duplicate listings, broken websites, out-of-country results, false positives, and multiple branches of the same company.
>
> I built the workflow in layers. Simple rules removed obvious bad records first. Then AI read the website content to separate real fire-protection service companies from fire departments, equipment sellers, and other poor fits. We calibrated the ICP using about 50 strong customers from the CRM and by watching how reps judged company websites. That showed us recurring maintenance and service mattered more than installation alone.
>
> Finally, we grouped branches under the right parent company where the evidence was strong enough, separated maintenance, install, and mixed-service companies, enriched people and phone numbers, and gave the cold-calling team a cleaner account list. The outcome was better market visibility and a more usable sales queue, not a claim about autonomous pipeline.

Source and deployment basis:

- Seth-specific source basis: [Salescraft Uptick portfolio artifact](/Users/sethlim/Documents/career-ops/deploy/salescraft-gtm-systems/salescraft-uptick-vertical-tam-crm-enrichment-system.html),
  [Salescraft GTM systems portfolio](/Users/sethlim/Documents/career-ops/deploy/salescraft-gtm-systems/salescraft-gtm-systems-portfolio.html), [Uptick scoring documentation](/Users/sethlim/Documents/Uptick-Coding/scoring/SCORING_SYSTEM_DOCUMENTATION.md),
  [Uptick scoring quick start](/Users/sethlim/Documents/Uptick-Coding/scoring/README_QUICK_START.md), and [Uptick project CLAUDE guide](/Users/sethlim/Documents/Uptick-Coding/CLAUDE.md).
- Workflow basis: crawler JSONL, HubSpot CSV enrichment, classification, 0-100 fit score, local discovery, entity resolution, source evidence, CRM match status, QA sampling, CRM patch queue, and net-new account queue.
- Deployment spine to keep in mind: stale CRM, vertical market model, local source discovery, website/crawler evidence, HubSpot enrichment, entity resolution, custom fields, scoring, QA gates, CRM-safe activation, and feedback from sales usage.
- Truth boundary: do not claim a revenue or pipeline lift unless separately verified. The safe outcome is CRM quality, TAM visibility, routing quality, source evidence, and QA-safe activation.

Salescraft / Uptick example and playbook:

| Principle                                           | Salescraft / Uptick detail                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                     | Playbook lesson                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                         |
| --------------------------------------------------- | -------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------- | ------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------- |
| **Diagnosis before tactics**                        | Uptick's problem was not simply "we need more leads." It was that the GTM system was making decisions from an incomplete and stale market map. The product sold into fire protection and HVAC service companies, but the real market was not cleanly represented in standard SaaS databases. Many good-fit companies were local operators, branch networks, subsidiaries, service businesses, or companies with weak LinkedIn and messy web presence. If the CRM does not reflect the real market, reps waste time, territories are wrong, and outreach starts from bad assumptions.                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                           | In GTM engineering, diagnose the data system before automating activity. If the account universe is wrong, more sequences only scale noise. The first problem is: do we actually know who the market is, what makes them fit, and whether our CRM reflects that reality?                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                |
| **TAM segmentation before ICP**                     | The TAM had to be segmented from the product reality, not generic company categories. Uptick cared about fire protection contractors, HVAC operators, facility-service groups, asset maintenance, compliance workflows, field technicians, building owners, service reporting, and recurring inspection or maintenance work. The scoring system separated categories like Maintenance & Service Only, 50/50 Split, Install Only, Other, and Not Classifiable. The best accounts had evidence of service/maintenance work, operational complexity, field teams, compliance or asset-maintenance burden, and enough scale to care. The anti-ICP was broad construction, purely install-only, non-fire-protection companies, or businesses where the website evidence did not support the fit.                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                    | A TAM is only useful when it is segmented by buying likelihood. For vertical SaaS, "fire protection" is still too broad. The real ICP depends on service mix, maintenance focus, field complexity, geography, digital maturity, and whether the company actually has the workflow the product improves.                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                 |
| **Market map before account list**                  | The market map combined several source layers: existing HubSpot/CRM records, crawler JSONL from company websites, Google Maps, local directories, keyword search, service pages, LinkedIn/company clues, and public website evidence. Then the system had to normalize the account identity: company name, domain, branch locations, parent/subsidiary clues, duplicate listings, alternate trading names, and whether the record already existed in CRM. Only after that could the workflow decide whether a row was a CRM enrichment task, a net-new lead, a manual review item, or a suppress candidate.                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                    | A good account list is the output of a market map, not the starting point. For messy vertical markets, the map has to include source strategy, entity resolution, CRM comparison, confidence labels, and routing logic before sales can trust it.                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                       |
| **Build the market map, then clean it in layers**       | Uptick already had inbound leads, but the customer wanted one complete view of the US fire-protection market in a normalized sheet. Google Maps helped fill the gaps, but the raw data was noisy: overlapping searches could return the same company more than once, some results fell outside the target country, and some listings pointed to broken or missing websites. Under the customer's rule, a credible fire-protection company should have a working website, so those records could be removed with simple rules before using AI.<br><br>After that first clean-up, AI handled the judgment calls. It read the scraped website content and separated real fire-protection service companies from obvious false positives such as fire departments or businesses that mainly sold equipment. We then looked for useful, but less reliable, size and structure signals: whether the company appeared to have multiple locations, how large the team seemed to be, and whether owners or employees could be found on LinkedIn. Those signals were treated as clues, not facts.<br><br>The client calibration was just as important as the data work. We pulled a golden set of about 50 strong companies from the CRM, watched how the reps reviewed company websites, and used AI to look for common patterns. That changed the ICP. The original brief included companies that installed or maintained fire-protection systems, but the stronger pattern was recurring service and maintenance. So the final list separated maintenance-led companies, install-led companies, and companies that did both.<br><br>At the end, we resolved branches and duplicate listings back to one parent company where the evidence supported it, kept the branch locations under that account, enriched relevant people, and pulled the main phone number for the cold-calling team. Ambiguous parent, branch, size, or category decisions stayed in review rather than being pushed blindly into CRM. | Start with rules for the obvious decisions, then use AI for the messy judgment. Calibrate the model against real customers and the way reps actually evaluate accounts. Treat LinkedIn headcount, branch structure, and parent-company matches as evidence with confidence levels, not guaranteed facts. The final output should help a rep work one clean account with its locations, contacts, and call number—not hand them a pile of duplicate map listings. |
| **Tiering**                                         | Tiering used both fit and confidence. Stronger accounts had clear fire protection or HVAC service relevance, maintenance/service focus, professional website quality, useful service pages, employee count or scale indicators, contact availability, and evidence of operational complexity. The scoring system produced a 0-100 fit score, with classification carrying the most weight, then website quality, maintenance focus, services offered, employee count, and contact availability. A Tier 1 account might be a maintenance-focused multi-location fire protection company with clear compliance and field-service workflows. A Review account might have ambiguous branch structure, weak domain evidence, or unclear parent/subsidiary relationships. Suppressed accounts were low-fit, duplicate, non-fire-protection, or too uncertain.                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                        | Tiering should separate priority from certainty. A high-potential account with weak evidence may need review before activation. A clean but low-fit account should not get sales attention. The playbook should route accounts based on fit, confidence, evidence quality, and the cost of being wrong.                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                 |
| **Right signal, right timing**                      | The right signal was not just "this is a fire company." The messageable signals were maintenance/service focus, recurring inspections, compliance workflows, field technicians, multiple locations, branch networks, asset reporting, competitor software mentions, weak or fragmented digital workflows, and evidence that the company was big enough to feel operational pain. The system also separated public-source evidence from CRM context, such as existing notes, lead status, current software, owner, contact availability, and prior sales touches.                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                               | A prospecting signal has to explain why the account deserves attention now. In vertical SaaS, a useful signal is tied to the workflow: service complexity, compliance burden, field operations, current software pain, or CRM evidence that the account is active or worth re-opening.                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                  |
| **Proof proximity**                                 | Proof proximity came from showing the evidence, not just the score. For every inferred service category, branch clue, parent-company hint, or account route, the system needed source evidence: website URL, service page, directory listing, CRM match, or QA note. The output was stronger when a seller could open the row and see why the account was classified, why it was prioritized, and what caveat applied.                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                         | In AI-enriched GTM, the proof is the evidence trail. Sales should not have to trust a black-box score. They should see the source, rationale, confidence, and next action so they can decide whether the signal is messageable.                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                         |
| **Buyer committee**                                 | The buyer committee for this kind of project sits inside the revenue operating system. Sales leadership cared about whether the team had a more accurate market map and better territory focus. RevOps cared about CRM hygiene, dedupe, field quality, import safety, routing, and governance. SDRs and AEs cared about whether the output helped them choose accounts and write from evidence. Data/ops cared about source reliability, scoring logic, QA sampling, and whether inferred fields were clearly labeled. If any one group lost trust, the system would not be used.                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                              | A GTM engineering project has a buyer committee too. The system has to work for leadership, RevOps, sellers, and data operators. A workflow that creates beautiful enrichment but breaks CRM trust is not a win.                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                        |
| **Talk tracks by stakeholder / message market fit** | **Sales leadership: real TAM and better focus.** The leadership talk track was: the team cannot run a serious territory or outbound strategy if the account universe is wrong. A vertical market graph gives leadership a cleaner view of which accounts exist, which ones are high-fit, which ones are already in CRM, and where the whitespace is.<br><br>**RevOps: CRM-safe activation.** The RevOps talk track was: this is not a blind import. Existing CRM records, stale fields, missing data, duplicates, misclassified accounts, and net-new leads are separated into different queues with source evidence and QA checks.<br><br>**SDR / AE: account briefs and messageable reasons.** The seller talk track was: the system should tell you why this account, why this workflow, what evidence supports the angle, what current software or operational clue matters, and whether the account is ready for outreach or needs more research.<br><br>**Data / ops: confidence and quality gates.** The data talk track was: inferred fields are useful but dangerous unless confidence, source URL, QA notes, and manual review rules are preserved. The system should make uncertainty visible.                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                      | Message-market fit in GTM engineering means translating the same data system into each stakeholder's pain: leadership gets market clarity, RevOps gets hygiene and control, sellers get better account judgment, and data teams get traceability.                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                       |
| **Gap selling to CEO**                              | **Current state:** The CRM was stale and incomplete. It did not reflect the full fire protection and HVAC service market, and standard B2B databases had poor coverage of local operators, branch networks, subsidiaries, and messy service companies.<br><br>**Desired state:** A source-backed vertical market graph that identified the actual TAM, matched discovered companies against CRM, enriched existing records, created net-new account queues, scored fit, and preserved evidence so sellers and RevOps could trust it.<br><br>**Measurable shortfall:** The shortfall can be measured by CRM match status, number of missing accounts, number of duplicate or ambiguous entities, percentage of rows with source URLs, field completion rate, QA sample pass rate, distribution across CRM patch versus net-new lead queues, and seller acceptance of the output. I would not claim revenue lift unless separately proven.<br><br>**Root cause:** Standard SaaS databases do not index this market cleanly. The useful buying signals were buried in maps, local directories, websites, service pages, branch structures, CRM notes, and messy public evidence.<br><br>**Cost of doing nothing:** Reps chase the wrong accounts, miss real TAM, duplicate records, waste time on low-fit companies, misroute territories, and distrust the CRM. Sales leadership cannot see the market clearly enough to run the motion well.<br><br>**Bridge:** The enrichment workflow became the bridge: combine CRM and public-source evidence, resolve entities, classify fit, score accounts, QA sample the output, and route records into CRM patch, net-new queue, manual review, or suppress.                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                           | Gap selling applies to sales ops too. The current-state pain is not always a lost deal; sometimes it is a broken revenue operating system. The CEO or CRO case is that better data quality changes account selection, rep focus, territory design, and confidence in the operating cadence.                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                             |
| **Main friction / what made it hard**               | **1. The buyer had already seen automated AI outreach fail.** The challenge was not convincing them that AI could write an email. They had already tried versions of automated AI outreach, and it did not work well enough. That made the sales conversation harder because another "AI SDR" pitch would sound like more of the same: more generic messages, more automation, and more risk of burning accounts without improving pipeline. The failure mode was not just copy quality. It was that automation had jumped too quickly to sending before solving the deeper GTM questions: which accounts are actually in the TAM, which ones are ICP-fit, which signals matter, whether the CRM record is correct, whether the evidence supports the outreach angle, and whether a human seller would actually trust the recommendation.<br><br>**2. I had to shift the frame from AI SDR to account intelligence.** The reframe was: do not ask AI to do all the selling. Use AI to make the seller's judgment better. Instead of "let the AI send outbound," the project became a CRM-safe vertical TAM and account-intelligence system: discover the real market, resolve entities, classify fit, preserve source evidence, score accounts, QA sample outputs, separate CRM patches from net-new leads, and give sellers a clearer reason for why this account, why now, and what angle might be worth a human follow-up.<br><br>**3. The trust problem was both seller trust and CRM trust.** Sales leadership needed to know this would improve account focus, not just create activity. RevOps needed to know it would not poison HubSpot with duplicates, weak matches, or overconfident AI fields. SDRs and AEs needed to know the output would help them think, not replace their judgment with a black-box score. So the product had to show its work: source URLs, confidence labels, QA notes, routing logic, and human review before activation. | **How I overcame it:** I changed the sales narrative from "AI can automate outreach" to "AI can make account selection and rep preparation much sharper." I started with the failed-motion diagnosis: if the list is wrong, the signal is generic, or the CRM is stale, AI just makes the wrong motion faster. Then I built the workflow around evidence and human judgment: sample rows first, source-backed classification, entity-resolution checks, fit scoring, CRM match status, QA sampling, manual-review queues, and seller-facing account briefs. The output was not "send this email automatically." It was: here is the account, here is the evidence, here is the fit logic, here is the confidence, here is the recommended route, and here is where a human should decide the next move. |
| **Fast follow-up loop and onboarding**              | Onboarding should start with a sample set, not a full CRM overwrite. I would take a representative batch across subsegments, show the discovered source evidence, compare it against HubSpot/CRM records, review duplicate and parent/branch cases with RevOps, calibrate the scoring rubric with sellers, and only then push the high-confidence records into the right queue. The first review should answer: which fields are useful, which fields are vanity, which classifications are wrong, which records need manual review, and what threshold is safe for activation?                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                | Fast follow-up in a GTM system means quick calibration with operators. Do not disappear for weeks and return with a giant CSV. Bring sample rows, review the evidence, tune the rubric, agree on import rules, and build trust before scaling.                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                          |
| **Wedge product and land-and-expand**               | The wedge was CRM enrichment and TAM repair, not full autonomous outbound. Start by fixing the existing map: enrich current CRM records, identify stale or missing fields, dedupe messy entities, and separate net-new accounts from CRM patches. Once the team trusts the market graph, the expansion paths are natural: net-new account queues, territory planning, seller account briefs, buying-signal monitoring, campaign segmentation, outreach angle generation, and CRM hygiene automation.                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                           | The land-and-expand logic is to start where trust is broken and measurable: CRM quality and TAM visibility. Once the system proves it can improve the account universe safely, it can expand into higher-leverage sales workflows.                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                      |
| **AI increases judgment density**                   | AI increased judgment density by reading messy websites, comparing CRM and public evidence, classifying service categories, extracting branch and service clues, scoring fit, and packaging rationale. But the human still owned the important judgment: what taxonomy mattered, what evidence was strong enough, which inferred fields were safe for CRM, and which accounts should be routed to sellers.
