                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                   | Multi-threading is not spamming every stakeholder. It is understanding why each stakeholder cannot say yes yet, then giving each one the proof they need to become comfortable. In a technical enterprise deal, the buyer committee and workflow map are connected: each stakeholder owns a different risk in the user journey, integration path, support model, or internal business case.                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                       |
| **Talk tracks by stakeholder / message market fit** | **CEO: growth and launch speed.** With the CEO, the conversation was not really about an SDK. It was about what Unabank could become. They already had the financial relationship, the logged-in users, and the BNPL payment layer. Boxo gave them a faster way to add ecommerce and other services around that core relationship, instead of waiting for the internal team to build every merchant integration from scratch. The CEO-level message was: more reasons for users to come back, more partner revenue, faster category launches, and a clearer path from BNPL app to broader superapp-style platform.<br><br>**Product Head: make it feel like Unabank.** With product, the conversation was mainly about user experience and trust. The risk was not only "can we technically open a merchant page inside the app?" It was: will this feel like part of Unabank, or will it feel like a random website that happens to open inside Unabank? That mattered because Unabank was a financial product. If the experience felt off-brand or confusing, users would not trust the marketplace or the BNPL checkout. The simple product talk track was: Boxo can help the merchant journey feel native to Unabank. The colors, theme, and overall feel could be adapted so the user still felt they were inside Unabank, not being thrown somewhere else.<br><br>**Engineering: prove this is real and supportable.** With engineering, I would explain it like a product flow, not like a pile of SDK features. The desired user journey was simple: a Unabank user opens the app, taps into a merchant, browses products inside Unabank, checks out with the Unabank BNPL option, and returns with a clear payment or order status. To make that work, each party needed a clear job. **Unabank** stays the source of truth for the customer and financial relationship: identity, login, consent, BNPL eligibility, payment approval, and transaction record. **The merchant** stays the source of truth for the commerce experience: catalog, inventory, pricing, order details, fulfillment, and product support. **Boxo** provides the connecting layer between them: it lets the merchant journey open inside Unabank, passes only the context needed, hands login and payment back to Unabank at the right moments, returns order or payment status to the merchant flow, supports testing before launch, and turns the same handoff pattern into something reusable for the next merchant.<br><br>That made the engineering discussion much clearer. Instead of debating "can this SDK work?" in the abstract, we could walk through the flow step by step: what happens when the user opens the merchant, what context is passed, when login is needed, when payment is triggered, where payment status comes back, what happens if payment succeeds but the merchant order fails, what gets logged, how we test it before launch, and who supports the customer if something breaks. Engineering skepticism was healthy because it forced the deal to become real. The goal was to leave them feeling that there was a clear path from demo to test environment to production, and that nobody was hiding the hard parts around login, payment, data, support, or failure handling.<br><br>**Legal / compliance / risk: user data.** For legal and compliance, I would keep the conversation anchored on one concrete question: what user data moves, and why? The baseline policy was simple: Unabank remains the source of truth for the customer and payment relationship; Boxo only passes the minimum context needed to make the journey work; and the merchant only sees what it needs to fulfill the order. So if a user is just browsing a merchant, there should be little or no personal data passed. If the user needs to log in or create a session, the flow can use a limited user reference or the minimum required fields, with consent where needed. If the user checks out, Unabank still owns the BNPL/payment decision and transaction record, while the merchant gets the order details it needs: items, amount, shipping or contact details if required, and payment status. The merchant should not receive the user's full Unabank profile, credit decisioning logic, wallet details, or unnecessary financial data. The easy way to explain the security posture was consent plus encryption: if the user needed to log in or share account information, they should explicitly consent, and any sensitive user data passed into the miniapp flow should be encrypted. I would phrase it as consented, encrypted, and minimized, not fully anonymized, because checkout still needs real order and fulfillment data. The principle was data minimization: pass only what the use case needs, keep Unabank as the source of truth, make consent clear, and make the boundary easy for legal and compliance to review. That was easier to underwrite than a vague answer like "we are secure."<br><br>**Internal product: protect delivery credibility.** Internally, my job was also to keep Boxo honest. Early-stage sales can go wrong if you sell the future as if it already exists. So I had to translate customer concerns into clear product requirements, then translate Boxo's real capability back to the customer without overpromising. That meant being precise about what already worked, what needed custom work, what depended on Unabank, what depended on the merchant, and what should stay out of phase one. The sale only mattered if the company could deliver it. | Message-market fit means each stakeholder hears the same deal in their own language. The CEO hears growth and strategic speed, product hears trusted user experience, engineering hears a credible path to production, legal/compliance hears user-data boundaries, and the internal team hears scoped commitments. The seller does not need to be the engineer, but they do need enough product and technical fluency to turn vague concern into the right next proof point.                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                     |
| **Gap selling to CEO**                              | **Current state:** Unabank wanted a broader app experience, but the company still had to keep the core BNPL product competitive. That core BNPL work had two heavy parts. The transaction flow had to work properly: checkout experience, instant approval logic, payment authorization and capture, refunds, credit repayments, settlement, and reconciliation. Then there was the KYC and risk layer: onboarding, fraud/risk checks, compliance, customer support, and merchant economics. The marketplace opportunity was strategically important, but every new merchant or service category could become a separate product, engineering, vendor, risk, and support project layered on top of the BNPL roadmap.<br><br>**Desired state:** Unabank could keep its team focused on the core BNPL app and payment experience, while using Boxo as the production-ready merchant / miniapp integration layer. The desired state was not "outsource the whole strategy." It was: keep Unabank as the trusted financial app, connect ecommerce merchants to the BNPL payment option inside the app, and make the marketplace launch repeatable instead of bespoke.<br><br>**Measurable shortfall:** I would frame the shortfall in three practical metrics. **First, time-to-market:** if Unabank built the app-integration layer themselves, they would have to build a whole product system around merchant launch. In plain language: how does a merchant get approved, how does it open inside the Unabank app, how does the user log in, how does BNPL checkout work, how does payment status come back, how is the flow tested before going live, what does the merchant need to follow, and who supports the customer when something breaks. As a rough interview-safe estimate, that could easily become a 6-12 month internal-build path before the first meaningful merchant cohort was live, especially once UAT, app-release cycles, and merchant QA were included. With Boxo, the pitch was that the core layer was already production-ready, and because we had pre-integrations around common merchant backends like Magento, Shopify, and WooCommerce, a key merchant could plausibly launch in around a week once it passed Unabank's normal UAT-to-production gates.<br><br>**Second, engineering opportunity cost:** every month spent building the marketplace SDK internally was a month not spent improving the core BNPL product: approval experience, repayment flows, app performance, customer trust, fraud/risk controls, or market-standard BNPL features. The CEO did not need to believe Boxo removed all engineering work; they needed to see that Boxo removed the undifferentiated integration layer so Unabank engineering could focus on the financial product that made Unabank special.<br><br>**Third, merchant onboarding capacity:** without a reusable SDK and pre-integrated backend paths, each merchant could become its own custom integration. With Boxo, Unabank could expose a clearer integration pattern to merchants: launch through the Boxo layer, connect to Unabank's BNPL payment option, handle payment status and callbacks cleanly, and preserve the host-app experience. That changed the measurable question from "how many one-off integrations can our team survive?" to "how many merchants or categories can we activate through the same platform pattern?"<br><br>**Root cause:** The internal build path would force Unabank to build two products at once: the core BNPL product and the app/merchant integration platform around it. The hard part was not only writing code. It was maintaining docs, sandbox testing, event flows, payment states, merchant support, security controls, production rollout, and ongoing edge cases across every merchant and category.<br><br>**Cost of doing nothing:** Slower marketplace launch, slower merchant activation, engineering distraction from the core BNPL roadmap, fewer reasons for users to return to the app, missed partner revenue, weaker superapp narrative, and continued dependence on bespoke integration work for each new category.<br><br>**Bridge:** Boxo became the bridge from internal-build drag to faster category launch: start with ecommerce merchants on familiar backends, use Boxo's SDK / miniapp layer and merchant outreach support, connect the journey to Unabank's BNPL payment option, pass through UAT and production release properly, then use the same platform pattern for more merchants and later categories like eSIMs or travel. The champion package was not just "collateral." It was a forwardable internal case the CEO / Product Head could repeat: why now, why not internal build, what first use case proves, what technical risk is controlled, what launch timeline is realistic, what remains owned by Unabank, and how the first marketplace expands into a larger platform capability.                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                               | Gap selling should make the cost of the status quo concrete. In this story, the gap was not simply "we can save engineering time." It was: internal build could mean 6-12 months of SDK, merchant, payment, testing, and support plumbing before the first meaningful marketplace cohort; Boxo compressed that into a production-ready integration path so Unabank could focus on the core BNPL product and launch merchants faster. The CEO does not need every SDK detail, but they do need to see the measurable shortfall: time-to-market, engineering opportunity cost, merchant onboarding capacity, and strategic delay.                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                   |
| **Main friction / what made it hard**               | **1. Adoption was political, and the blockers were very specific.** The hardest part was that this was not just a product evaluation. Unabank liked the idea, but the internal politics were real. Engineering did not necessarily feel they needed Boxo; part of the resistance was, "we can build this ourselves," and there was a natural sense of threat because Boxo was sitting close to their app, their roadmap, and their technical ownership. Legal also needed the data boundary to be airtight: what data passed from the Unabank app into the miniapp layer, what stayed inside Unabank, what the merchant could see, what required consent, and what happened if something broke inside a regulated fintech experience. So the buyer map was not just champion, economic buyer, and technical evaluator. I had to map blocker personas: engineering as the build-versus-buy and ownership blocker, and legal/compliance as the data, consent, and liability blocker.<br><br>**2. Internal coordination kept slowing the deal.** Deadlines kept slipping because coordination on their side was hard. One stakeholder would be interested, another would need more proof, another would worry about ownership, and another could quietly slow the deal if their risk was not addressed. So the challenge was not just creating excitement; it was turning scattered internal interest into a coordinated decision path.<br><br>**3. I had to teach the buyer how to buy and build urgency without fake pressure.** Because this was a new category, Unabank did not have a perfect evaluation process sitting on the shelf. I had to help define what success should look like, what the first ecommerce wedge should prove, who needed to be involved, what technical validation had to happen, what legal/compliance questions needed answers, what should stay out of phase one, and what timeline made sense from demo to UAT to production. The strongest materials were not just Boxo-generated collateral; the buyer had to help correct the POV, sharpen the business case, and define success in their own language. That made the deal more real because it became something they could defend internally. The urgency was not "sign this quarter because I need the deal." It was the cost of internal build drag: if every merchant or category became a bespoke product, engineering, risk, and support project, Unabank's marketplace roadmap would keep slipping.                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                      | **How I overcame it:** I broke the deal into separate proof tracks instead of treating it like one generic sales process. For engineering, I walked through the user journey and integration flow step by step: what happens when a user opens a merchant, what data is passed, when login is needed, where BNPL checkout happens, how payment status comes back, how UAT works, and who supports issues. For legal/compliance, I kept the answer concrete: data minimization, consent, encryption, Unabank as the source of truth for the customer and financial relationship, and only the minimum merchant data needed for order fulfillment. For the CEO and Product Head, I kept the business case tied to urgency: internal build would keep turning every merchant launch into a bespoke product, engineering, risk, and support project. The move was to make the first ecommerce wedge narrow, define what it had to prove, make the evaluation path explicit from demo to technical validation to UAT to production, and keep Boxo honest about what was real now versus what belonged in later phases. |
| **Fast follow-up loop and onboarding**              | Selling the platform was only one piece. Once the buyer got comfortable with the SDK, auth, payment, legal, and risk questions, the game was not over. The next question was: how do we turn merchant launches into a repeatable operating motion? The commercial model helped because it aligned incentives. Seth remembers the structure as a significant base platform fee, roughly USD 150K per year, plus expansion economics for additional merchants: around USD 2K per merchant per year for simpler merchants, and potentially closer to USD 5K per year when the build was more complex. That meant Boxo was commercially incentivized to help Unabank bring merchants live quickly, while Unabank had a predictable base-plus-expansion model instead of a separate bespoke project for every merchant.<br><br>The onboarding process then had to become almost factory-like. For each merchant, the team needed to confirm the merchant backend: Magento, Shopify, WooCommerce, or something more custom. Then the work was: wrap or adapt the merchant web experience as a native-like miniapp inside Unabank's app, map the relevant catalog and SKU data, handle product variants, price, quantity, shipping and order fields, connect the checkout to Unabank's BNPL payment option, and make sure payment status could move cleanly between the miniapp, Boxo, and the host app. In plain English: the customer should be able to browse a merchant inside Unabank, choose products, see the right SKU/order details, pay with Unabank's BNPL option, and get a reliable order/payment status without feeling like they were kicked out to a random website.<br><br>The release process also mattered. Boxo's docs had a sandbox / testing concept where miniapps in testing could be exposed before production, while production should only show approved miniapps. In practice, that meant each merchant needed a clean path through setup, sandbox testing, UAT, app-release / production gates, and support handoff. The live threads were: merchant outreach, backend readiness, miniapp build or adaptation, SKU/order/payment mapping, SDK launch behavior, payment callbacks, custom events, UAT cutoff dates, production approval, support ownership, and escalation paths if a merchant order or payment failed. It also meant keeping the internal Boxo team honest about which merchants were simple, which were complex, what could launch in a week, and what needed more custom work. For the buyer, the goal was to feel that every merchant launch had an owner and next step. For Boxo, the goal was to protect delivery credibility while scaling merchant count.                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                             | In enterprise sales, post-close confidence matters, but so does post-close repeatability. A deal can be "closed" commercially and still fail if every merchant launch becomes a bespoke fire drill. The playbook should turn implementation into an operating rhythm: pricing aligned to expansion, merchant readiness checks, sandbox/UAT/prod gates, clear ownership, and a repeatable launch path from commercial promise to live merchant.                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                    |
| **Wedge product and land-and-expand**               | The wedge was ecommerce merchant integration, specifically merchants running on common ecommerce backends like Magento, Shopify, and WooCommerce. That mattered because Boxo had already done pre-integration work with those merchant backends, so the promise was not "give us six months and we will custom-build every merchant." The promise was closer to: we can help you launch key ecommerce merchants in roughly a week, provided the work passes through Unabank's normal UAT-to-production lifecycle for launching a new app feature. That made the first wedge concrete and credible: launch a mobile-first ecommerce marketplace inside the Unabank app, connect merchant journeys to Unabank's BNPL payment option, and prove the app experience, SDK integration, support model, and buyer-side trust without asking Unabank to commit to every future category at once. Boxo also brought more than the technical layer. Early in the customer conversation, we said we could help reach out to merchants on Unabank's behalf and propose the integration solution, so we were not just bringing "done-for-you technical integration"; we were helping them build the merchant supply side of the marketplace. The expansion story was broader embedded-services capacity: if Boxo could help Unabank launch one ecommerce marketplace, the same layer could support more merchants, categories, and services. Seth remembers eSIMs and travel as examples of later live categories. Commercially, the remembered model was roughly USD 150K per year as a base platform fee, plus around USD 2K per additional merchant per year for simpler merchants, and potentially closer to USD 5K per merchant per year where the build was more complex. The overall deal is remembered around the low-six-figure annual range, potentially around USD 300K/year depending on basis, but exact basis should be caveated.                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                 | The strongest enterprise wedge is narrow enough to prove and broad enough to expand. In this case, the wedge was not abstract "superapp infrastructure"; it was a specific marketplace launch path: pre-integrated merchant backends, merchant outreach support, a faster launch path through UAT and production, and a first category that could prove the platform pattern. Do not sell "everything this could become" before proving the first category, but make sure the first category is strategically connected to a bigger expansion path.                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                               |
| **AI increases judgment density**                   | If Seth were running the same motion today, he would use AI to build account dossiers, map stakeholders, summarize technical and compliance objections, track proof gaps, draft champion memos, prepare implementation-risk checklists, and maintain an account-plan log after every customer or internal product conversation. The useful output would not be generic email copy. It would be: who cares about what, what proof is missing, which objections are unresolved, which stakeholder needs which answer, what was promised, what should not be promised, and what the Account Director or founder needs before the next meeting. But the judgment would still be human: which stakeholder matters now, what proof is missing, what risk is real, and what not to promise.                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                               | AI would make the enterprise seller more prepared, not more aggressive. It should increase judgment density: better account intelligence, sharper stakeholder maps, cleaner proof gaps, stronger champion packages, and stronger AD handoff.                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                      |
| **Living feedback loop**                            | Unabank taught Seth the shape of the Boxo enterprise playbook. The objections became reusable: vendor maturity, SDK/API feasibility, auth/payment/user-journey risk, support ownership, legal/compliance concerns, and how to move from one integration to platform expansion. That learning helped Boxo move from product hypothesis toward credible fintech platform sale. It also paved the way for Seth to close e-pon Digital Inc. using the same sales cycle: identify the right platform buyer, anchor on the first embedded-services wedge, make the technical and risk questions specific, and turn one launch into a broader category-expansion story. Later, after Seth left, Boxo also won World App / the Worldcoin ecosystem associated with Sam Altman, which should be used only as company-trajectory proof, not personal attribution.                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                            | A first enterprise deal should become a playbook. Every objection, proof gap, implementation issue, and stakeholder concern should feed the next enterprise motion. The best proof that a motion is real is that it works again: first Unabank, then e-pon, and eventually bigger platform logos after the same category logic became more mature.                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                |

Unabank MEDDICC-style drill:

Use this if the interviewer pushes for sales rigor. Phrase it as a reconstruction, not as a formal Boxo sales methodology.

| MEDDICC-style field  | Unabank answer                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                            |
| -------------------- | --------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------- |
| Metrics              | Category launch speed, integration effort avoided, number of service categories / merchants supported, user engagement, partner revenue, and whether ecommerce could become a repeatable embedded-services pattern instead of a one-off build.                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                            |
| Economic buyer       | CEO / senior business owner who cared about strategic app expansion, revenue opportunities, and faster platform growth.                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                   |
| Decision criteria    | Reusable app surface, acceptable UX / brand control, SDK/API feasibility, auth/payment/session clarity, support ownership, data / consent boundary, vendor maturity, and expansion path beyond ecommerce.                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                 |
| Decision process     | CEO and Product Head alignment, engineering validation, legal/compliance/risk comfort, Boxo product / engineering feasibility, commercial terms, and implementation handoff.                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                              |
| Identify pain        | Internal build made every new category feel like a custom roadmap project. The pain was not only engineering cost; it was strategic delay.                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                |
| Champion             | The CEO functioned as both economic buyer and business champion, with Product as the execution champion. The CEO had ambitious marketplace / platform targets to hit, but the internal team was not moving fast enough to launch every merchant or category by itself. He needed a safe internal story: why now, why not build, what first use case proves, and what risk controls make the decision underwriteable. Relationship texture helped too: Seth remembers the CEO and Boxo founder sharing a regional / Russian-speaking background, which made trust-building easier. I would keep that as rapport context, not the main reason the deal won. |
| Competition          | Internal build, status quo, vendor maturity concerns, and the buyer's natural instinct to avoid putting a young vendor inside a regulated app experience.                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                 |
| Paper process / risk | Legal, compliance, data, user consent, liability, support, SLAs, and reputational risk if a third-party service failed inside Unabank's customer journey.                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                 |
| AD handoff quality   | Best handoff would include stakeholder map, current business thesis, use case, proof still missing, technical validation status, risk owner, unresolved objections, internal Boxo constraints, and next-best action.                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                      |

Unabank metrics drill:

| Metric / fact | Interview-safe phrasing | Caveat |
|---|---|---|
| Boxo total ARR | "At Boxo, I helped build enterprise traction to roughly USD 450K ARR across three fintech clients." | Use as Boxo-level proof, not necessarily one-deal value. |
| Strategic partnerships | "Boxo also had nine strategic super-app partnerships." | Confirm which names are safe to cite externally. |
| Unabank deal value | "The Unabank-style deal is remembered around the low-six-figure annual range, with a base license and expansion mechanics." | Do not hard-claim exact USD 300K ARR unless Seth confirms basis. |
| Base license | "The commercial model is remembered as a base annual license around USD 150K-200K ARR." | Internal prep memory; caveat if pressed. |
| Expansion mechanic | "The model expanded as more merchants/categories were integrated, with additional merchant and custom implementation economics." | Exact per-merchant figure should be caveated unless confirmed. |
| Deal complexity | "CEO in Singapore, Product Head in the Philippines, engineering in Ukraine, plus legal/compliance/risk and internal product threads." | Strong story detail; avoid inventing individual names. |

Unabank / Boxo enterprise math:

| Metric / fact | Interview-safe phrasing | Math / caveat |
|---|---|---|
| **Role timeline** | "I was not running this enterprise ARR motion from day one. For roughly my first 1.5 years at Boxo, the motion was more channel-partnership led. We were acting as an extender of the superapp ecosystem: helping companies that used Alibaba's miniapp platform onboard services, and operating almost like a service provider building on top of Alibaba's platform before Boxo had fully built its own. The Unabank-style direct enterprise motion came later, once Boxo was selling its own platform more directly." | Useful context if the interviewer asks why the earlier Boxo motion sounds more like partnerships than enterprise sales. |
| **Target / expectation** | "I did not have a mature Airwallex-style quota sheet at Boxo, but the operating expectation for the enterprise motion was roughly two enterprise deals a year and about USD 400K ARR." | Treat this as an internal operating expectation, not a formal comp-plan quota unless Seth confirms the plan. |
| **Deal size range** | "The floor for a real enterprise deal was roughly USD 200K+ ARR. Larger platform deals could be USD 600K+; World App later closed around that level after I had left." | Use World App only as company-trajectory proof, not personal attribution. Base-case planning should use USD 200K-300K, not the largest possible deal. |
| **Named account universe** | "The universe was small and high-stakes: roughly 50 named accounts a year, not thousands of generic apps." | At a USD 200K minimum deal size, 50 named accounts represented at least roughly USD 10M of raw annual ARR potential before qualification. Bigger platform accounts made the theoretical TAM larger, but many accounts had to be actively disqualified. |
| **Stakeholder coverage design** | "For each named account, I usually tried to reach the three commercial drivers first: C-suite, product, and partnerships. Technical conversations usually came later once there was real business interest." | 50 named accounts x 3 primary stakeholder lanes = about 150 planned stakeholder lanes per year. This is not the same as 150 completed meetings, because I would not always reach all three stakeholders in every account. |
| **Pipeline coverage** | "If the annual expectation was USD 400K ARR, the healthier enterprise pipeline target was around 3x coverage, or roughly USD 1.2M in qualified ARR pipeline." | At USD 200K-300K per qualified opportunity, USD 1.2M of pipeline means roughly 4-6 qualified enterprise opportunities. In practice, I would want closer to 6-8 live qualified opportunities because technical, legal, and product timelines can slip. |
| **Win-rate math** | "Two wins from a 50-account named universe is about a 4% account-level conversion rate. On truly qualified enterprise opportunities, the planning win rate would be much higher: if I carried 6-8 qualified opportunities and closed two, that is roughly 25-33%." | This is the clean way to avoid confusing named-account conversion with qualified-opportunity win rate. The low raw conversion is normal because the account universe includes many accounts that look interesting but fail ICP, timing, sponsorship, build-versus-buy, or budget tests. |
| **How to say it live** | "Boxo was not a high-volume SMB motion. It was a finite named-account enterprise motion. I was working a universe of about 50 serious platform accounts a year, usually multi-threading C-suite, product, and partnerships before technical validation. The enterprise expectation was about two wins a year or roughly USD 400K ARR. Because deal sizes were usually USD 200K+ and some could be much larger, the planning math was roughly USD 1.2M qualified ARR pipeline for 3x coverage, with about 6-8 serious opportunities live to land two." | This is the most useful spoken version. It shows sales rigor without pretending Boxo had the same mature quota machinery as Airwallex. |

90-second version:

> Unabank is my best enterprise deal story because it taught me that enterprise sales is structured risk removal. At Boxo, we were an early startup selling SDK-based miniapp infrastructure to a serious fintech buyer.
>
> The initial use case was launching an ecommerce marketplace inside their app, but the broader value was helping them launch more embedded services faster than building every integration themselves.
>
> The buyer map was distributed. I dealt with the CEO in Singapore, the Product Head in the Philippines, and engineering in Ukraine, with legal/compliance concerns around vendor maturity, data flow, support, and user-facing risk. The CEO cared about strategic app expansion. Product cared about UX and roadmap fit.
>
> Engineering cared about SDK/API feasibility, auth and payment handoffs, and support ownership.
>
> My role was to hold those threads together: map who cared about what, translate objections back to our product team, keep the business case tied to platform expansion, and avoid overpromising. The deal became an early enterprise proof point for Boxo and contributed to the broader USD 450K ARR fintech book.
>
> The lesson I carried forward is that the seller's job is not just to persuade. It is to make the buyer's decision underwriteable.

Truth boundaries:

- Do not invent proper names for stakeholders.
- Do not hard-claim exact Unabank ARR/ACV basis unless confirmed.
- Do not imply Seth personally engineered the SDK. Say commercially technical/account orchestrator.
- Do not overspecify security certifications or implementation details not in the source.

#### Canonical Case Table: Zhao / Leader Food OrderOps Project

Question it answers: "What is a messy workflow you turned into an AI system?", "How do you scope AI around real operations?", "How do you sell or package a workflow project?", or "How do you avoid overbuilding?"

Short answer:

> Zhao / Leader Food OrderOps is my cleanest proof that I can sit with an operator, understand the real workflow, and turn it into a review-first AI system and commercial scope. The customer did not need a new ERP.
>
> They needed a private operating layer that could capture messy WhatsApp and WeChat orders, preserve evidence, match vague product language to SKU/spec/customer history, route ambiguity to review, and generate morning handoff artifacts without pretending the AI could run the business by itself.

Source and deployment basis:

- Seth-specific source basis: [Zhao OrderOps PRD](/Users/sethlim/Documents/Seth%20Second%20Brain/wiki/workflow-hustle/zhao-orderops-prd.md), [raw Zhao workflow sketches](/Users/sethlim/Documents/Seth%20Second%20Brain/raw/intentional/pasted/2026-06-15-zhao-orderops-workflow-sketches.md),
  [Zhao production PRD v2](/Users/sethlim/Documents/zhao-orderops/docs/spec/zhao-orderops-production-prd-v2.md), [Zhao data request](/Users/sethlim/Documents/zhao-orderops/docs/spec/data-request.md), and [Leader Food quotation](/Users/sethlim/Documents/zhao-orderops/docs/commercial/leader-food-orderops-quotation-v1.md).
- Second Brain methodology basis: AI workflow readiness / "boring layer" checklist for workflow owner, source of truth, SOP, data flow, QA rubric, decision log, and feedback loop; Context Graphs for decision traces; OpenAI Enterprise Product Framing for Workspace Agents versus FDE-style qualification.
- Deployment spine to keep in mind: workflow interview, source-system map, data package, Phase 0 channel feasibility, review-first MVP, UAT examples, operator calibration, acceptance milestones, and support window.
- Truth boundary: treat the S$40K scope as proposal / fixed-scope commercial packaging unless closed revenue is confirmed. Do not claim measured production ROI, ERP replacement, ERP write-back, or fully autonomous order acceptance.

Zhao / Leader Food example and playbook:

| Principle | Zhao / Leader Food detail | Playbook lesson |
|---|---|---|
| **Outreach wedge before discovery** | Before Zhao / Leader Food, I had a market thesis. Food order intake felt like one of those workflows that had been historically held together by manual labor, relationship memory, and people just getting things done. From my Airwallex experience with traditional businesses, I knew many customers in this kind of market would not use a formal ordering app or platform. They would call, WhatsApp, WeChat, send a voice note, or message the salesperson because that was the relationship. More importantly, their customers were not going to change behavior just because the supplier wanted cleaner data. If competitors were still accepting orders through phone and WhatsApp, asking customers to fill in a form would feel impersonal and risky. So I used Google Maps to build and scrape a list of food manufacturers and suppliers, then reached out by cold email and LinkedIn to CEOs with a simple offer: would you be open to letting me audit your business, because I suspect there are manual workflows AI could help with, especially order intake. That is how I landed on Leader Food: a supplier to Haidilao, led by a second-generation owner who was already an active user of AI tools and wanted to modernize the business his father had built. | Good outbound starts with a workflow wedge, not a product pitch. The reason to reach out was not "do you want AI?" It was "I think this specific manual workflow is probably painful in your market, and I can audit whether AI can help without forcing your customers to change behavior." That made the outreach respectful, concrete, and tied to a real operating pattern. |
| **Diagnosis before tactics** | The first diagnosis was that the hard problem was not "read messages." Customers ordered through WhatsApp and WeChat groups using informal product names, English/Chinese/broken Chinese, voice notes, images, quantities, pack sizes, and customer-specific shorthand. Night-shift staff had to translate that into the right SKU/spec before morning warehouse and ERP work could begin. The core issue was interpretation and operational safety, not chatbot UI. | Do not start with the AI primitive. Start with the operator's actual bottleneck. In this case, the product problem was: "what did the customer mean, and is that interpretation safe enough for warehouse, driver, invoice, and ERP handoff?" |
| **TAM segmentation before ICP** | The workflow was narrowed to phase-one order intake and morning handoff for a food distributor. It explicitly excluded ERP replacement, live stock truth, payment collection, purchase-order automation, full route optimization, driver mobile apps, customer portals, and fully autonomous order acceptance. The ICP was not "all F&B software." It was a private operations layer for messy group-order businesses where customer shorthand, SKU matching, customer history, route/lane context, and handoff accuracy matter. The strongest fit was a business where orders arrive in informal chat groups, the same customers repeat similar orders, product naming is inconsistent, and the cost of a wrong interpretation travels downstream into warehouse, driver, invoice, and customer-service work. The anti-ICP was a company that already had clean ecommerce ordering, simple SKU language, no meaningful repeat-customer history, or a willingness to replace the ERP immediately. | A useful first AI workflow is usually a narrow slice of the operating system. The discipline is deciding what not to build so the first release can be useful and quoteable. A good workflow ICP names both the pain and the boundary: messy recurring order interpretation, yes; ERP replacement or full logistics platform, no. |
| **Market map before account list** | The workflow map ran from WhatsApp/WeChat group messages and off-channel phone orders, to raw evidence preservation, to parsing, SKU/spec matching, exception review, soft confirmation, 4:20 AM working picklist/Bible, driver route stack, ERP/import-ready export, and invoice reconciliation. The ERP remained the source of record; OrderOps sat between customer channels and existing operations. The map also had a timing layer: late-night customer messages, review before morning operations, warehouse pick/pack, driver route handoff, office ERP/invoice check, and post-hoc correction learning. This mattered because the product was not an abstract agent; it had to produce the exact artifact each team needed at the exact point of the morning workflow. The decision trace was part of the product: raw message or media, parsed line, SKU match evidence, staff edit, approved alias, Bible line, route stack, and ERP/import record. | For workflow AI, the "account map" is also a process map. You have to know where work enters, where truth lives, where humans review, what artifact gets printed or exported, when it is needed, and where the system must stop. Serious workflow AI should preserve not only the output, but why the output was produced. |
| **Finite-TAM trust** | Zhao did not want a competitor-owned F&B platform because customer order history, buying patterns, product/pricing behavior, and operational process data were sensitive. A privately hosted or self-owned deployment was part of the trust story. The system also had to preserve raw message/media evidence so operators could trace what happened. | Trust is not abstract. In operational AI, trust means data ownership, evidence preservation, auditability, and respecting the systems the business already trusts. |
| **Tiering** | The phase-one scope was deliberately piloted around selected WhatsApp/WeChat groups, route/customer subsets, and a data package of 20-50 normal orders plus ambiguous, corrected, duplicate, voice, image, picklist, invoice, and route-stack examples. The plan was not to enable every group and edge case on day one. | Tiering applies to workflows too. Start with the route, group, customer subset, and sample set that proves the hardest repeatable pattern without turning phase one into an ERP rewrite. |
| **Right signal, right timing** | The strongest signal was not "they use WhatsApp." It was that night-shift order interpretation created downstream risk before a hard morning deadline. The 4:20 AM working picklist/Bible was the forcing function: warehouse and drivers needed clean instructions early, even when customers used vague or mixed-language orders at night. | A workflow signal becomes messageable when it connects to operational urgency. "You receive messages" is weak. "Your night team has to turn messy orders into a reliable 4:20 AM handoff" is sharp. |
| **Proof proximity** | The proposal used the customer's actual operating artifacts: call notes, transcripts, workflow sketches, finalized order forms, invoice examples, driver route stacks, picklist/Bible behavior, and ERP/export constraints. The PRD preserved traceability to source materials and separated transcript evidence from visual artifact observations. | Proof is strongest when it uses the customer's own work, not a generic demo. In AI workflow sales, real examples are the difference between plausible and operationally credible. |
| **Buyer committee** | The buyer map included owner/sponsor, night-shift operator, morning warehouse team, driver/delivery team, office/ERP operator, and customers. The owner cared about reducing pain and errors without replacing ERP or buying a competitor platform. Night shift needed review and edit tools. Warehouse needed a printable 4:20 AM Bible. Drivers needed an ordered route stack. Office/ERP needed controlled invoice/export reconciliation. Customers needed to keep ordering naturally with soft confirmations only. | Operational AI has more stakeholders than the budget owner. The system has to respect the people who operate the workflow at 2 AM, 4:20 AM, in the warehouse, in the driver route, and in ERP. |
| **Talk tracks by stakeholder / message market fit** | **Owner/sponsor:** reduce operational pain and errors while keeping ERP as source of record and data private. The owner-level pitch was not "AI ordering app"; it was a private operating layer that protects customer/order-history data and makes the business less dependent on tribal memory.<br><br>**Night-shift operator:** one review table with source message/media evidence, SKU/history match explanations, AI comments, and quick edit/hold/cancel/review actions. The operator should feel faster, not replaced.<br><br>**Warehouse:** a 4:20 AM printed working picklist/Bible with product/spec/quantity plus write-in space for actual weights, substitutions, cancellations, and correction notes. The warehouse cares less about AI and more about whether the morning sheet is usable.<br><br>**Driver:** a route/lane stack that can be printed or reviewed stop-by-stop, preserving driver judgment while reducing re-sorting and last-minute confusion.<br><br>**Office/ERP:** ERP/import-ready records, read-only ERP sync where feasible, invoice import/reconciliation, and no risky ERP write-back in phase one. This was important because admin needed cleaner handoff, not a dangerous second source of truth.<br><br>**Customer:** no new portal; keep WhatsApp/WeChat ordering and receive formal soft confirmation without false promises about final stock, weight, price, invoice, or delivery. | The pitch lands when it uses each operator's language. The same project is "data privacy" to the owner, "review table" to night shift, "Bible" to warehouse, "route stack" to drivers, "no write-back risk" to ERP/admin, and "keep ordering normally" to customers. |
| **Gap selling to CEO** | **Current state:** Late-night order capture was manual, context-heavy, and dependent on staff memory. Vague product phrases like "pork 5 kilos" could map to many SKUs/specs depending on customer history, group notes, product rules, and what that customer usually meant. That ambiguity did not stay in the chat; it flowed into warehouse picking, driver routing, invoice preparation, and customer follow-up.<br><br>**Desired state:** Messy orders become structured, reviewable order lines with raw evidence, match explanations, exception routing, soft confirmation, picklist/Bible output, route stack, and ERP/export handoff.<br><br>**Measurable shortfall:** The measurable gap was operational reliability and cycle time around night-to-morning handoff: capture-to-review under one minute, Bible/route/export generation under one minute for typical batches, invoice reconciliation under five minutes after files/exports are available, fewer ambiguous lines reaching warehouse, fewer duplicate/missed orders, fewer invoice mismatches, and better staff confidence in the morning handoff. The requested data package itself became part of the proof: 20-50 normal orders plus ambiguous, corrected, duplicate, voice, image, picklist, invoice, and route-stack examples.<br><br>**Root cause:** The company had real operational knowledge, but it was scattered across chats, staff memory, SKU lists, ERP history, aliases, group notes, and manual correction habits.<br><br>**Cost of doing nothing:** More night-shift interpretation load, more warehouse confusion, more correction cycles, more invoice mismatches, more dependence on experienced staff, and more operational risk during busy periods.<br><br>**Bridge:** OrderOps was the bridge: preserve existing customer channels and ERP, but add a private review-first layer that makes the workflow inspectable. The champion package was the PRD, the quote, the data checklist, the UAT criteria, the support window, and the explicit out-of-scope list that made the project feel controlled rather than open-ended. | Gap selling for AI workflow should quantify time, risk, errors, handoff quality, and what happens if ambiguity reaches the next operator. For AI projects, the business case is often not one headline ROI number; it is fewer manual interpretation loops, fewer exceptions leaking downstream, faster handoff, and a clearer operating surface. |
| **Main friction / what made it hard** | **1. The friction was operational trust, not AI interest.** The owner was already open to AI, so the hard part was not convincing him that AI was interesting. The hard part was proving that the workflow could be trusted in a business where mistakes show up immediately: wrong SKU, wrong quantity, wrong customer, wrong route, wrong invoice, or warehouse confusion before the morning handoff. In food distribution, a small interpretation error does not stay inside the software. It hits warehouse, drivers, invoices, customer service, and customer trust.<br><br>**2. Adoption had to preserve existing relationships.** The buyer could not simply force customers onto a clean portal. Customers were used to ordering through WhatsApp, WeChat, voice notes, images, shorthand, and informal relationship-driven messages. If the solution required customers to change behavior, it would create commercial risk. So the sales challenge was to show that modernization did not mean making the business less human. The system had to preserve the customer channel and make the internal handoff cleaner behind the scenes.<br><br>**3. I had to prevent scope explosion.** The temptation was to make it sound like an AI ordering platform, ERP replacement, route optimizer, invoice engine, customer portal, and operations brain all at once. That would have made the project feel risky and impossible to buy. I had to keep the scope tight: phase-one order intake and morning handoff, with ERP remaining the source of record, no risky write-back, no autonomous order acceptance, no full route optimization, and no customer portal. | **How I overcame it:** I sold control, not autonomy. I started with an audit and real workflow mapping, then narrowed the project to the highest-pain handoff: messy night orders into a reviewable 4:20 AM working picklist, route stack, and ERP-ready export. I made the system review-first: preserve raw messages and media, show SKU/history match evidence, route ambiguous lines to humans, and let operators edit before anything moved downstream. I also made the commercial package concrete: data request, phase-zero feasibility, MVP scope, UAT examples, acceptance milestones, deposit structure, support window, and explicit out-of-scope list. The buyer did not have to believe in "AI running the business." They only had to believe in a controlled layer that reduced interpretation pain while keeping customers, staff, and ERP workflows intact. |
| **Fast follow-up loop and onboarding** | The commercial package made the next step concrete: send proposal/PRD context, official quotation, deposit invoice, then start after signed quotation and S$16K deposit. The implementation path included Phase 0 channel feasibility, source data/review core, outputs/export, pilot hardening, handover, UAT, and support. The first customer dependencies were explicit: SKU master, customer list, group mapping, order history, aliases, examples, picklist, route stack, invoice/export samples, and test group access. This was the "boring layer" before agents: one workflow owner, one source of truth per category, structured data, mapped data flows, review templates, QA standard, decision log, and feedback loop. The scope also made acceptance practical: S$16K upfront, S$16K when MVP shipped, S$8K after final acceptance, 5 business days of UAT, and 14 calendar days of in-scope bug-fix support. That gave the buyer a clear route from "interesting AI idea" to "what do I need to send you by when?" | Strong follow-up turns an AI idea into an executable project.
