The buyer should know the scope, deposit, timeline, data package, acceptance criteria, support window, and what is out of scope before work begins. Before an agent can work reliably, the business has to make the workflow legible. |
| **Wedge product and land-and-expand** | The wedge was not ERP replacement. It was order intake, SKU/spec matching, review, soft confirmation, 4:20 AM Bible, route stack, and ERP/import-ready export. That wedge was chosen because it was painful, visible, close to daily operations, and bounded enough to ship in a first phase. Future expansion could include ERP API read path, ERP write-back if explicitly approved, driver mobile workflows, analytics, payments, purchase-order automation, broader routing/TMS, customer portals, customer-specific pricing logic, or day-shift email/PO intake, but those were kept out of phase one. The land-and-expand logic was: first prove the private review layer, then expand into adjacent workflow surfaces only after the team trusts the source evidence, review controls, and output artifacts. | The best wedge respects the customer's current source of truth. Land the workflow layer first, prove value, then expand only after the operating boundary is trusted. Do not ask the buyer to trust an autonomous operating system before they trust the first reviewable handoff. |
| **AI increases judgment density** | The system used AI to turn messy input into structured suggestions, comments, match explanations, and exceptions, but it did not silently accept risky orders. Low-confidence SKU/spec, quantity, language, media, duplicate, stale history, or ERP conflicts became review exceptions. Staff corrections could improve future aliases after approval. The point was to move staff judgment from hunting across chats, memory, and spreadsheets into a tighter review surface: "this is what the system thinks the customer meant, here is the evidence, here is the risk, here is what needs review." | AI should concentrate human judgment where it matters. The goal is not to remove the operator; it is to remove manual hunting while making the operator's decisions faster, clearer, and better evidenced. |
| **Living feedback loop** | Every reviewed exception, staff correction, customer alias, stale ERP field, duplicate, ambiguous order, and invoice mismatch became learning material. The system design included editable aliases, approved staff corrections, source evidence, audit trail, and future eval/test sets using real Leader Food examples. The stronger version is a context graph for operations: not just "what was the final order," but what input was used, what policy or alias applied, what exception was raised, who approved the correction, and why the next similar case should behave differently. | A workflow system should improve from use. The playbook is not "ship model once"; it is source evidence, corrections, alias updates, eval cases, and a tighter review loop over time. Decision traces are what let a workflow agent get better without becoming a black box. |

Zhao deployment and eval drill:

Use this if the interviewer asks how the system would actually become reliable, or how this maps to OpenAI-style workflow deployment.

| Deployment component | Zhao answer                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                            |
| -------------------- | -------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------- |
| Boring layer         | One owner for the order-intake workflow; SKU/customer/order history as source-of-truth inputs; documented order paths; mapped data flow from chat to review to Bible to route to ERP/import; QA standard for what a correct order line looks like; decision log for staff corrections. |
| Decision trace       | Raw WhatsApp/WeChat input, OCR or transcript if media/voice, parsed line item, SKU/spec candidates, customer-history evidence, route/group context, reviewer decision, approved correction, final Bible/route/export artifact.                                                         |
| Eval / test set      | Start with 20-50 real examples across normal text orders, ambiguous shorthand, corrected orders, duplicates, voice notes, images, route-stack cases, invoice/reconciliation cases, and customer-specific aliases.                                                                      |
| Graders              | Deterministic checks for required fields, duplicate detection, SKU/spec match, exception routing, and export format; human/operator review for judgment calls, alias correctness, and whether the output is actually usable at 4:20 AM.                                                |
| Failure modes        | False acceptance of ambiguous orders, wrong SKU/spec, stale customer history, duplicate order, overconfident soft confirmation, ERP/export mismatch, and unsupported WeChat capture path.                                                                                              |
| Improvement levers   | Fix source data, add aliases, clarify SOP, tighten review thresholds, require human approval for risky categories, improve schema, add examples, or narrow the first workflow scope.                                                                                                   |
| OpenAI translation   | This is the kind of account that could start as a Workspace Agent-style bounded workflow if the customer owns the SOP and source systems, or become FDE-style if the deployment requires deeper multi-system redesign, custom integrations, and operating-model change.                |

Zhao / Leader Food metrics drill:

| Metric / fact | Interview-safe phrasing | Caveat |
|---|---|---|
| Total project fee | "The fixed-scope quotation was S$40,000." | Commercial proposal, not proof of fully realized production ROI. |
| Payment split | "The proposed split was S$16K upfront, S$16K on MVP shipped, and S$8K on final acceptance." | Use to show commercial scoping discipline. |
| Timeline | "The indicative timeline was 4-6 weeks after acceptance, deposit, and data handoff." | Dependent on channel feasibility and data readiness. |
| UAT / support | "The quote included 5 business days of UAT and 14 calendar days of bug-fix support inside scope." | Shows acceptance discipline, not deployment outcome. |
| Phase 0 | "Phase 0 was a 2-4 working day channel feasibility spike for WhatsApp and WeChat capture/reply." | WeChat path was explicitly higher-risk. |
| Data sample | "The requested pilot data package included 20-50 normal orders plus ambiguous, corrected, duplicate, voice, image, picklist, invoice, and route examples." | Shows eval/sample-set thinking; not a measured performance result. |
| Performance targets | "Targets included capture-to-review under one minute, Bible/route/export generation under one minute for typical batches, and invoice reconciliation under five minutes after files/exports are available." | PRD targets; not actual production measurements. |
| Truth boundary | "Strongest safe claim: built/scoped a working review-first OrderOps system from real operator discovery, PRD, proposal, and commercial artifacts." | Do not claim measured time savings, error-rate reduction, or ERP integration completion unless later confirmed. |

90-second version:

> Zhao / Leader Food is the cleanest example of me turning messy operator discovery into an AI workflow and commercial scope. The customer received late-night food-distribution orders through WhatsApp and WeChat groups. The problem was not simply reading text.
>
> Customers used shorthand, Chinese, broken Chinese, voice notes, images, vague product names, and customer-specific habits. Night-shift staff had to turn that into the right SKU/spec before the morning warehouse and driver workflow.
>
> I scoped the system as a review-first layer, not an ERP replacement.
>
> It captured messages, preserved source evidence, matched orders against SKU data, customer history, group notes, aliases, and route/lane data, flagged ambiguity for human review, sent soft confirmations when safe, generated the 4:20 AM working picklist/Bible, produced driver route stacks,
>
> and created ERP/import-ready outputs while leaving ERP as the source of record.
>
> Commercially, I packaged it into a fixed S$40K scope with deposit, MVP, and final acceptance milestones, UAT, support, explicit assumptions, and clear exclusions. The lesson is that enterprise AI value often lives in the messy middle of a workflow.
>
> You need to understand the source of truth, the handoff, the risk, the human review point, and the artifact the business actually runs on.

Truth boundaries:

- Do not claim measured production time savings or error-rate reduction.
- Do not claim ERP write-back or ERP replacement.
- Do not imply fully autonomous order acceptance.
- Do not expose private customer data, raw transcripts, or operational details beyond sanitized workflow description.

#### Canonical Case Table: Sunder Legal Document Processing Workflow

Question it answers: "What is the most impressive AI workflow you built?", "How did you sell AI to a professional-services buyer?", "How do you design review-first AI?", or "How do you translate customer discovery into product?"

Short answer:

> Sunder is my strongest document-processing workflow story. I sold and built a paid pilot with Hoh Law, a leading Singapore personal-injury and road-accident claims firm.
>
> The problem was not "lawyers need a chatbot." It was that high-volume PIPD work creates messy evidence packets: medical bills, medical reports, income documents, photos, scans, duplicates, and combined PDFs.
>
> Sunder turned that into a reviewable claimant-side dossier: classify, split, extract, cite, validate, review, and generate a first-draft work product while keeping lawyers in control.

Source and deployment basis:

- Seth-specific source basis: [Sunder README](/Users/sethlim/Documents/Sunder/README.md), [Sunder product notes](/Users/sethlim/Documents/Sunder/PRODUCT.md), [Hoh Law client config](/Users/sethlim/Documents/Sunder/src/config/clients/hoh-law.ts), [extraction types](/Users/sethlim/Documents/Sunder/src/types/extraction.ts),
  [Gemini processing route](/Users/sethlim/Documents/Sunder/api/gemini/process.ts), [Sunder selling guide](/Users/sethlim/Documents/career-ops/interview-prep/sunder-selling-guide.md),
  [Sunder organized sales pitch](</Users/sethlim/Documents/career-ops/interview-prep/life-history/artifacts/sunder/notion-native-export-2026-06-12/extracted-small/Private & Shared/Sales Pitch (Organized by Product Features Jobs to 2c22b940864c80ce8f61ce9bdd383321.md>),
  [PIPD sales slides](/Users/sethlim/Documents/career-ops/interview-prep/life-history/artifacts/sunder/slides/pipd-sales-slides.pdf), [PIPD medical expense slides](/Users/sethlim/Documents/career-ops/interview-prep/life-history/artifacts/sunder/slides/pipd-medical-expense-breakdown.pdf),
  [Hoh Law ICP analysis](</Users/sethlim/Documents/Northbridge Lab Scoping Process/Hoh Law/hoh_law_icp_analysis.md>), [Hoh Law end-to-end workflow](</Users/sethlim/Documents/Northbridge Lab Scoping Process/Hoh Law/hoh_law_end_to_end_workflow.md>), and the Sunder architecture / validation deep dives in career-ops.
- Second Brain methodology basis: Context Graphs for decision traces and customer-owned context, workflow readiness for SOP / QA / decision-log discipline, legal AI source captures for attorney review and citation boundaries, and High-Signal Enterprise Sales for proof proximity and gap selling.
- Deployment spine to keep in mind: discovery across legal workflows, narrow PIPD wedge, selected pilot stakeholders, taxonomy/schema setup, document processing, source-linked review, reviewer correction, phased rollout, and feedback into validation/eval rules.
- Truth boundary: say three-month phased paid pilot and review-first legal document operations. Do not claim broad scaled deployment, PMF, paralegal replacement, final legal advice, or production-grade evals unless supporting evidence is recovered.

Sunder example and playbook:

| Principle | Sunder detail | Playbook lesson |
|---|---|---|
| **Diagnosis before tactics** | The original diagnosis came from field discovery, not a premade product pitch. The way Seth remembers it, the team went to TechLaw.Fest / legal-tech conferences, scraped attendee lists, printed simple name cards, and used the floor as live market research. Seth had studied law, so he knew the practice-area map at a high level, but the useful insight came from talking to lawyers about what their work actually looked like: what they did weekly, where documents got stuck, what paralegals hated doing, what clients chased, what partners cared about, and what tools were already being used. The Cambridge Law background helped because it made the outreach feel credible and made lawyers curious, but Seth still treated the market as unknown. The early segmentation was sobering: international firms, Big Four-style practices, and sophisticated corporate / arbitration teams often had budget, internal AI support, or access to top-tier tools like Harvey or Legora; competing there as a young local startup would be difficult. At the other end, many SME firms were not using AI at all, or were using ChatGPT in a loose ad hoc way without the workflow, source control, review surface, or firm-specific templates needed for legal work. After that, the team used a lightweight research ask adapted to law firms: Cambridge-led Singapore AI team, not a sales call, would you spare 15-30 minutes to help us understand where admin work breaks. That compounded into close to 50 lawyer conversations. The repeated lesson was that lawyers did not need AI to tell them routine PIPD law. They needed the file prepared: documents sorted, fields extracted, missing information flagged, calculations structured, and a first useful review pack created. | The first question was not "who wants legal AI?" It was "which part of the legal market is underserved, repetitive, painful, and still trustable enough for AI to touch?" Field diagnosis meant separating top-end firms already served by enterprise legal-AI vendors from SME workflows where the pain was real but the tooling was thin. |
| **TAM segmentation before ICP** | The team explored broader legal AI, but the segmentation became clearer through discovery. "Legal" split into very different markets: international / Big Four / top-end firms with internal AI teams and premium vendor budgets; bespoke corporate, arbitration, and complex litigation work where partner judgment is the product; and SME or mid-sized practices doing high-volume operational legal work with much weaker AI infrastructure. The narrow wedge became Singapore PIPD because it had repeated document patterns, high active caseloads, admin bottlenecks, and a clear route to a paid pilot. Hoh Law was a strong pilot because it was not a boutique one-off workflow. Source docs describe 10 branches, roughly 22 lawyers, around 10 handling personal injury, Rosa handling 12-15 new PIPD signups per month through the main intake channel, 3-4 inquiry calls per day, and files often sitting in document / medical-report collection for 3-6 months before transfer. Seth's safe external phrasing remains "a high-volume PIPD practice with hundreds of active matters across multiple branches," because the source docs distinguish Rosa's intake flow from firm-wide active caseload. The anti-ICP was just as important: broad corporate law, one-off bespoke litigation, low-volume advisory work, firms already buying Harvey-style enterprise legal AI, or workflows where legal reasoning and partner judgment were the main product would have been harder to standardize and riskier to automate. PIPD document operations had a better shape: repeatable inputs, measurable admin burden, recognizable output artifacts, and humans who could review the result. | A good AI wedge is not a broad category like "legal." It is a repeatable workflow with volume, pain, recognizable inputs, clear output artifact, and a buyer with urgency. The playbook should separate market glamour from workflow readiness: a famous corporate practice may be a worse first wedge than a mid-sized high-volume claims practice with messy documents and a real admin bottleneck. |
| **Market map before account list** | The simplest two-sentence version is: Sunder took messy PIPD claim materials - police / GIA reports, medical reports, medical bills, income documents, Grab / Gojek / Tada statements, accident photos, scans, duplicates, and combined PDFs - and turned them into a structured evidence pack. The workflow was: ingest the case file, classify and split the documents, detect duplicates, extract the key fields with source citations, flag missing or low-confidence items, let a human review/edit the evidence, and then generate a claimant-side review pack and first-draft letter-of-demand support.<br><br>The hard part was that the documents did not arrive cleanly. They were often unlabelled, bundled into one giant PDF, scanned badly, duplicated, or formatted differently across hospitals, clinics, workshops, employers, Grab, Gojek, Tada, and insurers. They also arrived in drips over a long-running matter, sometimes across a year, so the medical expenses, income-loss numbers, and evidence pack had to be continuously updated. Doing that manually was slow and error-prone, especially when the work was pushed to junior staff who had to rename files, split bundles, copy numbers into spreadsheets, check payer categories, and avoid double-counting old invoices.<br><br>The endpoint was intentionally before final legal submission: Sunder prepared the evidence layer and first-draft artifact; lawyers/paralegals retained legal judgment and final sign-off. The external claim path was claimant evidence, claimant lawyer, insurer/defendant side, negotiation, possible public trustee/court-related process, and the firm's internal need to move matters from intake/admin backlog into active legal work. | In professional services, the workflow boundary matters. The best product object was not "AI lawyer"; it was "one review pack that gets the file ready for lawyer judgment." The map should show what comes before the AI, what the AI prepares, and where professional judgment resumes. |
| **Finite-TAM trust** | Legal workflows involve sensitive medical, financial, income, accident, and claimant data. The pitch had to be restrained: evidence over theater, citations over black-box answers, human review over automation claims, and clear boundaries around not replacing legal judgment. The repo and product philosophy are explicit: AI output is draft evidence, not a final decision. | Trust in legal AI comes from showing the source, limiting the claim, and giving the professional a better review surface. A flashy answer is weaker than a cited, editable field. |
| **Tiering** | Hoh Law was tiered as a strong pilot because it had volume, branch complexity, PIPD specialization, senior leadership interest, and real operational bottlenecks. The pilot was not instantly firm-wide. It ran for roughly three months with selected stakeholders first, then gradually rolled out across more of the firm. | For applied AI pilots, tiering means choosing the right customer and the right internal starting group. Start where the workflow is painful enough and the champion is close enough to the work. |
| **Right signal, right timing** | The signal was not "law firm uses documents." One strong market signal was that the Law Society had hosted a seminar on AI use in legal work, and we saw strong attendance and support around that initiative. That told us the category had attention, but category attention was still too broad. The more actionable signal was high-volume PIPD: repetitive claim evidence, hard-to-hire admin/paralegal capacity, backlog pressure, and a clear value path to faster first-good-LOD draft. The workflow was attached to revenue conversion because moving matters from intake/admin backlog to active claim work mattered commercially. | A signal is messageable when it connects market timing to operational pain. "Law firms are interested in AI" is useful context, but still generic. "The market is now paying attention to AI, and this specific PIPD workflow delays LOD production while tying up scarce staff" is a much stronger reason to start a conversation. |
| **Proof proximity** | The pitch worked when the proof felt like their work, not generic legal AI. The demo and pitch materials used PIPD-specific evidence: police / GIA reports, medical reports, medical expenses, income payslips, Grab / Gojek / Tada statements, accident photos, duplicates, bad scans, interim versus final invoices, Medisave, MediShield, private insurance, employer schemes, government subsidies, missing-document checklists, and LOD / Advice-to-Insurer style outputs. The strongest demo moments were concrete: the system could show the source page for a number like SGD 130.60, flag low-confidence fields, separate cash from other payer sources, catch a double-counted interim invoice or miscategorized medical report, and show a demo variance between an asserted Statement of Claim number of SGD 11,118.54 and a reviewed medical-expense number of SGD 9,306.04. It could also handle gig-economy income evidence where Grab, Gojek, Foodpanda, or Tada statements came in different formats and date ranges. US analogues like EvenUp and Supio helped validate the category, but Sunder's proof had to be localized to Singapore PIPD and then further localized to the firm's own taxonomy, documents, reviewers, and output style. | Proof proximity means same practice area, same jurisdiction, same document mess, same payer categories, same calculation pain, and same output artifact. Generic "AI reads PDFs" proof would not have been enough; the buyer needed to feel, "this has seen my files before." |
| **Buyer committee** | The pilot was not sold only top-down. Mr. Hoh mattered as founder/senior decision-maker. Vasan was the senior partner and day-to-day department champion. Mark and Bryan were lawyers handling PIPD matters and sources of case-handling requirements. Rosa and Karen were intake/document-collection specialists and first-pass reviewers for generated review packs. The product had to work for the people doing the grind, not just impress a partner in a demo. | AI adoption requires buyer, champion, user, and reviewer alignment. The senior buyer approves, but the workflow only works if intake specialists, paralegals, and lawyers can actually use the output. |
| **Talk tracks by stakeholder / message market fit** | **Founder / senior decision-maker:** reduce backlog, support the firm's paperless / AI transformation, and make the practice more scalable without diluting legal quality. The leadership pitch was capacity and control, not "replace lawyers." The Hoh-specific proof was that this was a real multi-branch operation: 10 branches, roughly 22 lawyers, around 10 handling PI, and hundreds of active matters across branches. The broader pitch-deck logic was that high-volume firms cannot scale if lawyers and paralegals are buried in manual intake, document organization, medical analysis, income calculations, and template drafting.<br><br>**Senior partner / department champion:** make PIPD easier to run day to day. Vasan's language was very operational: the firm needed one flow, one view, less clicking around, less physical file movement, and a way for Rosa's work to become visible to the lawyer without printing and dispatch. The champion talk track was: start with new PIPD intake, make Rosa happy, prove the workflow, then extend to backlog or other teams.<br><br>**Next-generation lawyers:** modernize the practice and spend more time on legal judgment, negotiation, strategy, and higher-value client work. The pitch was not "AI does law." It was "AI clears the boring evidence layer so you can do the actual lawyering." The sales pitch framed this directly: lawyers can do legal work, not admin work, but good admin is the foundation of good legal work.<br><br>**Intake/document specialists like Rosa:** stop manually printing, sorting, renaming, distributing, and checking repetitive document fields from messy uploads. Source docs describe Rosa handling 3-4 inquiry calls per day, 12-15 new PIPD signups per month through the main channel, and holding cases through medical-report collection before transfer. The pitch-deck time model broke the pain into concrete tasks: relabelling/classification, missing-document checks, PDF-to-template copying, income calculations, medical report / expense review, template filling, and lawyer review to catch human error.<br><br>**Risk / compliance instinct inside the firm:** preserve human review, avoid unsourced claims, keep medical/income evidence traceable, and never present the AI output as final legal advice. The reassurance was source citation, low-confidence flags, reviewer control, and a clear boundary: this prepares the evidence layer; the lawyer decides. | Message-market fit means the same workflow is translated into each stakeholder's actual job. Leadership hears scale, backlog, and transformation; champions hear one-view workflow control; operators hear less printing and chasing; lawyers hear better evidence and more time for judgment; risk-minded stakeholders hear source, review, and boundary control. |
| **Gap selling to CEO** | **Current state:** High-volume PIPD files arrived messy and stayed administratively heavy. Staff spent hours relabelling documents, identifying missing information, copying PDF facts into templates, consolidating income statements, reading medical reports, and checking paralegal work. The firm had legal expertise, but the evidence-prep layer slowed the path from intake to a useful claim package.<br><br>**Desired state:** A structured review pack and first-draft LOD / Advice-to-Insurer support generated from cited, validated, reviewer-editable evidence.<br><br>**Measurable shortfall:** The pitch deck made the gap measurable. It framed some PIPD lawyers as carrying 150-250 active files, and used a sample Riaz LLC structure of 2 lawyers plus 5 senior paralegals managing 4-5 secretaries to show why small and mid-sized practices struggle to scale. The pilot-scope deck said intake-to-LOD could take 2-4 weeks before revenue recognition begins. It broke the admin work into chunks: roughly 1 hour per case relabelling and classifying documents, 2 hours identifying missing documents/information, 1 hour copying PDF information into the LOD, up to 5 hours calculating income where Grab / Gojek weekly statements run to hundreds of pages, more than 3 hours reading medical reports and tabulating medical expenses / workshop invoices, more than 2 hours filling pre-action protocol and LOD templates, and more than 1 hour of lawyer review to mitigate estimated 5-15% paralegal human error. The target workflow was first-pass document relabelling, classification, extraction, calculation, and document generation in 2-5 minutes, followed by 10-30 minutes of human review and editing. The sales material also modeled 20 hours saved per paralegal per week, 80%+ paralegal-efficiency improvement, roughly SGD 600K annual baseline staffing cost in one scenario, roughly SGD 180K savings potential, and the ability to unlock capacity for more cases. Treat these as pitch-deck benchmark math, not audited production ROI.<br><br>**Root cause:** The work was not one hard legal question; it was a scattered evidence workflow: mixed file types, inconsistent document naming, duplicate scans, missing payer categories, medical expense variance, income evidence, paper / OneDrive / Affinity fragmentation, and template-filling work spread across people and documents.<br><br>**Cost of doing nothing:** Backlog, slower first-good-LOD production, scarce paralegal capacity absorbed by repetitive checking, higher chance of missing or miscopying evidence, slower conversion of viable matters into active claim work, and pressure from more technology-enabled firms. Seth's current memory is that backlog moved from roughly 9 months to roughly 3 months, but that should be treated carefully unless supporting evidence is recovered.<br><br>**Bridge:** Sunder compressed first-pass document operations into minutes plus 10-30 minutes of human review, with citations, validation flags, source-linked review, and generated work product. The champion package was the workflow benchmark, pilot scope, review process, accuracy/validation caveats, pricing proposition, and a clear line that lawyers retained final judgment. | Gap selling here means making the admin bottleneck visible. Do not stop at "documents are messy"; show where time, errors, backlog, hiring difficulty, and revenue conversion get stuck. The CEO case is stronger when it separates legal judgment from evidence operations and shows the cost of letting evidence prep remain manual. |
| **Main friction / what made it hard** | **1. The buyer fear was professional risk, not AI curiosity.** The hard part was not convincing lawyers that AI was interesting. Most of them were already curious because the market was talking about legal AI. The harder part was convincing them that this specific workflow was safe enough to use in real legal work. A lawyer cannot afford an AI system that invents a medical expense, misreads a report, double-counts an invoice, drops a source, or produces a draft that looks polished but is unsupported. So the sales challenge was trust: this could not feel like a chatbot doing law. It had to feel like a controlled evidence-prep system that made the lawyer faster while preserving legal judgment.<br><br>**2. Adoption depended on the operators, not only the senior buyer.** Even if the founder or senior partner liked the idea, the workflow would fail if the people doing the file work did not trust it. Intake/document specialists like Rosa and Karen had to believe it would reduce printing, sorting, chasing, renaming, and checking work rather than create another tool they had to babysit. Lawyers and paralegals had to believe the review pack was useful enough to edit, not so unreliable that they would rather start from the raw bundle themselves. So the friction was partly sales and partly change management: the firm had to believe this would fit into the way PIPD work actually moved through the office.<br><br>**3. I overcame it by narrowing the promise and selling review-first.** I did not sell "AI lawyer" or broad legal automation. I narrowed the wedge to PIPD evidence operations: classify, split, extract, cite, validate, flag missing or low-confidence items, and generate a first-draft review pack. The key was showing source citations, editable fields, confidence flags, reviewer control, and a clear boundary that lawyers retained final judgment. I also made the business case concrete: admin hours, backlog, intake-to-LOD delay, paralegal capacity, and the cost of repetitive evidence prep. Then we sold it as a phased paid pilot with selected stakeholders first, so the firm could test it on real workflow pain before trusting it more broadly. | **How I overcame it:** I sold trust through constraint. I made the workflow narrower, more cited, more reviewable, and more operator-friendly. For leadership, I framed it as capacity and backlog reduction. For the champion, I framed it as one cleaner flow for PIPD. For lawyers, I framed it as better evidence prep, not replaced judgment. For document operators, I framed it as less manual sorting and chasing. The move was to make the first pilot safe enough to say yes to: narrow workflow, real documents, source-linked outputs, human review, phased rollout, and clear limits on what the AI was not allowed to do. |
| **Fast follow-up loop and onboarding** | The pilot was run like an applied AI rollout: selected stakeholders first, benchmarks, before/after measurement, configuration around Hoh's document taxonomy and review workflow, iteration against live feedback, and gradual rollout. Onboarding became workflow-as-code: document types, extraction schemas, validation rules, review UI, and first-draft output logic. Practically, that meant starting with the people closest to the file prep, especially Rosa/Karen as first-pass reviewers, then involving lawyers and partner stakeholders as the review pack became useful. The implementation proof was concrete: Hoh-specific categories such as medical expenses, medical reports, income documents, and other evidence types; extraction schemas / processors; validation rules; citation metadata; and review states that separated clean outputs from items needing human attention. | In AI deployment, onboarding is not a checklist; it is turning the customer's workflow into product configuration, eval cases, training, reviewer habits, and adoption loops. The workflow did include simple first-pass liability triage: organize the accident facts and evidence, compare a straightforward factual pattern against familiar motor-accident scenarios, surface contradictions or contributory-negligence signals, and route judgment-heavy matters to the lawyer. A simple triage layer can prioritize professional attention without pretending to replace the professional's final judgment. Land where the professional can inspect the output, then expand only after the review workflow earns trust. |
| **AI increases judgment density** | Sunder used Gemini for triage/classification/splitting, Extend AI for structured extraction and citations, Hoh-specific schemas and validation rules for medical expenses/reports/income documents, Supabase for case/document state, and report generation for work product. The important technical proof was that the system could store cited field metadata and separate the original extraction from reviewer-edited data, so the firm could inspect and correct the evidence layer instead of accepting a black-box answer. The point was not for AI to decide the claim; it was to make the evidence layer inspectable so Rosa/Karen, paralegals, and lawyers could spend less time hunting and more time applying judgment. | AI increased judgment density by making the evidence inspectable. The reviewer spent less time hunting and more time deciding what was right. In serious workflows, the best AI system is often not the one with the boldest answer; it is the one that makes human review faster and more trustworthy. |
| **Living feedback loop** | The schema got better by watching where Rosa, Karen, Mark, Bryan, and reviewers still had to stop: missing payer category, wrong bill total, claimant identity not linked, unclear injury type, income period not normalized, duplicate document, or missing supporting file. Those stops became fields, validation rules, suspicious-value checks, or clearer definitions. The next maturity step would be a redacted eval set: known example documents, expected fields, expected citations, known edge cases, reviewer corrections, and regression checks whenever the prompt, model, schema, or validation rule changed. | Applied AI quality comes from workflow learning, not magic prompting. Sunder evaluation and trust-hardening drill:

Use this if the interviewer asks how to make legal document AI reliable, or how this differs from a "chat with PDFs" demo.

| Trust component | Sunder answer |
|---|---|
| Category boundary | Review-first PIPD document operations, not legal advice automation. The system prepares the evidence layer and first-draft artifacts; lawyers retain final judgment. |
| Source grounding | Every important extracted field should point back to the source document/page or citation metadata. The reviewer should see why the system believes the field is true. |
| Reviewer control | Preserve original extraction, reviewer-edited value, validation status, and reason for correction. Do not overwrite the evidence trail with the final answer only. |
| Validation checks | Missing payer category, suspicious values, duplicated documents, inconsistent claimant identity, unclear income period, missing supporting file, or low-confidence extraction should become `needs review` rather than clean output. |
| Eval set | Build a redacted set of known medical expenses, medical reports, income documents, mixed bundles, duplicates, bad scans, edge payer categories, and expected LOD fields. |
| Graders | Deterministic checks for required fields, citations, totals, date ranges, and schema validity; human / SME review for judgment-heavy items such as whether the review pack is actually usable by the lawyer. |
| Monitoring | Track false-clean rate, reviewer override rate, citation faithfulness, field accuracy, time-to-first-review-pack, and which validation failures recur. |
| Security / data boundary | Phrase carefully as pilot-grade trust work: private legal / medical documents should stay outside public repos, secrets should stay server-side, and case/document access should be scoped. Do not overclaim enterprise certification unless verified. |
| OpenAI translation | The buyer does not just buy model capability. They buy a workflow they can trust: source grounding, permissions, evals, review gates, auditability, and clear human responsibility. |

| Discovery | "We spoke to roughly 50 lawyers across legal workflows before landing on claimant-side PIPD." | Use as founder-led discovery proof. |
| Active caseload | "Safe phrasing: high-volume PIPD practice with hundreds of active matters across multiple branches." | Do not say hundreds of new monthly signups. |
| Baseline task model | "The sales deck broke admin work into hours per case: classification, missing-doc checks, PDF-to-LOD copying, income calculations, medical report review, template filling, and lawyer checking." | Treat as pilot/sales benchmark model unless measured spreadsheet is recovered. |

| Target workflow | "Compress first-pass admin work into minutes, then keep 10-30 minutes for human review." | Target/benchmark, not always measured outcome unless spreadsheet is found. |
| Backlog outcome | "Seth's current memory is backlog moved from roughly 9 months to roughly 3 months." | Use carefully; ideally recover supporting evidence before external use. |
| Demo audit numbers | "Demo claim audit: Statement of Claim SGD 11,118.54, reviewed medical expense SGD 9,306.04, variance SGD 1,812.50." | Demo/example evidence, not production ROI. |
| Commercial proposition | "Sales material framed pricing around SGD 3.5K-5K/month, 3-month minimum, cancel if key metrics did not improve." | Sales-deck proposition; use only if useful and caveated. |
